The image of Yama, the god of death in Hindu mythology, has come to have many variants. Dr. Merh's study tries to capture these against their essential literary settings to explore all possible traits of Yama's personality.

# Yama — The Glorious Lord of the Other World

## Yama — The Glorious Lord of the Other World

Reconstructing Indian History & Culture, no. 12 Kusum P. Merh D.K.Printworld (P) Ltd. New Delhi

In the Hindu pantheon, Yama holds a unique place. A counterpart, in the indigenous tradition, of Avestan Yima, Egyptian Osiris or Greek Pluto, he inspires terror in the heart of an average mortal: not only owing to his overlordship of the abode of the dead, but also for his identification with death itself. Yama's image in Hindu mythology, however, has come to have many variants — which Dr. Merh's study tries to capture against their essential literary settings.

Based on the prodigious mass of India's old-world scriptural literature — the Vedas, Samhitas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas and Upanisads, besides the Puranic texts, the book meticulously explores all possible traits of Yama's personality, highlighting how the mythical view of this glorious, other-world god passes through a striking change over the millennia between the Rgveda and the later Puranas. Unfolding the deity's 'Vedic' and 'Puranic' descriptions — respectively in part 1 and part 2 of the book, the author focuses specially on Yama, the creator, preacher, the philosopher, the lawgiver, the punisher and above all, on his role as an eschatologist.

Invaluable to the scholars of Indology, Hindu mythology and comparative religion.

Kusum P. Merh is a notable Sanskrit scholar, with a brilliant academic career that led to her selection for the UGC's (University Grants Commission's) prestigious research fellowship. Awarded PhD in 1992, she currently teaches in the Department of Sanskrit, Pali & Prakrit, M.S. University, Baroda, India.

(Reconstructing Indian History & Culture, no. 12) Second impression, 2006, xvi, 302 p.; 15 b/w photographs; Bibliography; Glossary; Index; 23 cm. vaivasvataḍ samgamanam jananam yamam rajanam havisa duvasya\| — Rgveda, 10.14.1 tapasa dharmamaradhya, puskare bhaskarah pura\| dharmamsam yam sutam prapa, dharmarajam namamyaham\|\| Brahmauaiuarta Purana, Prakrtikhanda, 26

## Preface

Vedic mythology occupies a very important place among ancient mythologies. Antiquity of the language of the Vedas. Its various interpretations besides the numerous obscure and unintelligible lines have generated a notion that nothing in all mythologies is more difficult than an attempt to get the clear view of a Vedic god.

The post-Saimhita period has preserved the immediate reactions to the symbology of the Vedas. Most of the Vedic gods find a place in later Hindu pantheon also and a considerable change in the concept of these gods is noticed. In the course of time some deities gained tremendous importance, while some others were lost in the oblivion, some gained new attributes while some new gods also came up. As a result, the Rgvedic gods like Varuna. Agni, Visnu, Indra and Yama are quite different from what they appear through the Puranic legends.

Among the Vedic gods, Yama has a unique place. The picture of Yama, as derived from the Vedic references is vague, inconsistent and even paradoxical. He is the son of Vivasvat, but he is called a king, and not a god. Though he was immortal like the other gods, he was the “first to die”; and though he died he is never called a mortal. He is a pathfinder who discovered a new settlement for all the dead where they alive blissfully in his company. The worshipper desires to become immortal in the kingdom of Yama. In the Puranas Yama underwent an evolution. The king of the dead now became the god of death, or death personified.

I decided to take up his study in order to carry out a thorough and detailed investigation into the real character of this Vedic god, as derived from the Vedic texts and its development in the principal Puranas. This investigation required a Joumey through the Samhitas to the Puranas; which is not only very long, but also diverse in language, dicton and spirit. An analytical approach is used to examine the various interpretations of some obscure passages of the Vedas. It is hope that this exhaustive study would be valuable to scholars interested in this field.

I take this opportunity to express my profound gratitude to Prof. S.G. Kantawala, Former Head, Department of Sanskrit, Pali and Prakrit, Ex-Director, Oriental Institute, Baroda whose valuable guidance throughout the period of my this research can only be inadequately acknowledged.

I am also grateful to Prof. R.I. Nanavati, Head, Dept, of Sanskrit, Pali & Prakrit, M.S. University of Baroda. My sincere thanks are due to Dr. B.S. Mehta ofVaranasi who not only took keen interest to my research, but also helped me in procuring some plates of Yama icons.

I am also obliged to my sister Dr. Sudha Pandya, Reader, Dept, of English, The M.S. University of Baroda, for helping me in several ways. I thank the M.S. University, Baroda to granting me permission to publish this book. I am thankful to the authorities of the American Institute of Indian Studies, Ramnagar, Varanasi, for providing me with the plates of Yama icons no. 1 to 8, and also to the National Museum, New Delhi for the photographs ofYama plate nos. 9 & 10, included in this book. I also wish to express my gratitude to the publishers, D.K. Printworld Private Lmited, New Delhi.

I retrospect, this work is an outcome of the constant encouragement I have received from my father Shri L.O Joshl (Retired, I.A.S.). A great Scholar of literature, Sanskrit as well as English — he imbibed in me early in life, love foi literature in general and Sanskrit in particular. I, therefore, dedicate this work to him and my mother who also has been a great source of inspiration to me.

I would like to express a deep sense of appreciation for my family — my husband Pradeep Merh and my children — Vanl and Rohit — for their wholehearted support.

## Part I Yama in the Vedas

## 1. Conspectus of the Vedic Literature

- The word Veda come from vid = to know. This word, primarily signifying knowledge, designated the ‘Sacred Lore’ “which was traditionally believed to enable the expert to know the superhuman powers and methods of influencing them.”The term Veda, therefore, means a whole great literature and not just one literary work. This great literature kept taking shape through many centuries and for many more centuries it was handed down to the next generation through verbal instructions. In this manner this sacred knowledge was passed on verbally for generations from the preceptors to the pupils, and survived in its purest form for an incredible length of time before it was written down.
- In the Indian tradition the Vedas are termed as Sruti since they were, initially, only heard as spoken by the preceptor, and not read or written. The Vedas are also considered apauruseya, i.e., not composed by men. It is believed that this sacred literature was visioned by the ancient sages when they were ‘issued like breath from the Self-Existent (SB, 11.5.8.If; also RV, 10.90.9).The Vedic poets, therefore, are called seers (rsih = rser darsanat Nir., 2.11), because these compositions were not made but seen by them. Hence these Vedic rsis are called the drastarah, not kartarah of the Vedas. With this concept of the nature of their origin, the authoritativeness of the Vedas cannot be questioned. The belief in the Vedas as 'seen' and not as ‘composed’ also shows the reverence with which they were learnt.
- The Vedas consist of four classes of literary works. They are: (1) Samhita. (2) Brahmana, (3) Arany aka and (4) Upanisad.
- The Samhitas are collection of prayers to different gods as well as chants and mantras for sacrificial purposes. This literature is constituted of four Samhitas: (1) The Rgveda, (2) The Samaveda, (3) The Yajurveda, (4) The Atharvaveda.
- The Brahmanas contain prose-texts and deal with sacrificial rites, religious duties and some legends. The Aranyakas, i.e., Forest-texts, contain the objects of meditation for the forest-hermits. The Upanisads contain higher philosophical reflections.
- **Date of the Vedas**
- There is no satisfactory evidence to prove the exact date of the compilation of the Vedas and, therefore, not a single work of Vedic literature can be accurately dated. On the basis of the language and contents the oldest of them is the RV- Sarnhita.
- On the linguistic grounds again, the RVmay be said to be of about 1500-1200 âṣ but its contents are, in the oldest part, of amuch more ancient date whereas some parts are of a later date. Many scholars have presented their views and findings in an effort to determine the age of Vedas, still the controversy persists. Some scholars assign the Vedic hymns to 3000 âṣ. others to 6000 âṣ. Tilak dates the hymns to about 4500 âṣ. Brahmanas to 2500 âṣ and Upanisads to 1600 âṣ. Jacobi puts the hymns at 4500 âṣ. According to Wintemitz the date of the Vedas is between 2500-2000 âṣ.A generally accepted date of the RV is between 2000-2500 âṣ.
- **RV-Samhita**
- It has been rightly observed that “the first place in the long row of books which contain the records of the Aryan branch of mankind belongs for ever to the RV.”This Sawhita has the distinction of being the oldest Indo-European literary monument. It also contains the earliest ideas about gods and religion as entertained by the ancient Aryans. Thus this Veda represents the earliest pha&pound;e of evolution of the religious consciousness. This mammoth book was not written in its present form. Various hymns of the RV were composed at different times, with a number of seers contributing to the creation of this great work which was later compiled.
- The RV is mainly a collection of res (stanzas) (rc \< arc, which are prayers to different gods like Agni, Varuna, Soma, Indra, etc. The only available recension of RV is the Sakala recension which including the 11 Valakhilya hymns contains 1028 hymns.
- On the ritualistic application of the hymns M. Wintemitz observes that there is no doubt that a great number of the RV hymns arose independently of the sacrificial rituals. Although many of these hymns were used later on for sacrificial purposes, it does not prove that they were originally meant for this purpose.
- Of the various recensions of the RV only one, namely the Sakala recension is available in an apparently complete form. Its 1028 hymns are divided in 10 Mandalas (i.e., books). According to another division the RV has 8 Astakas. Each Astaka is further divided in 8 Adhyayas (chapters). Mandalas Ṣ-VII are called the ‘Family-Books' (a term used by Geldner) because they contain family-hymns. Each of them is assigned by tradition to the eponym ancestor of a particular priestly family. The names of these rsis are Grtsamada (II), Visvamitra (III), Vamadeva (IV), Atrl (V). Bharadvaja (VI) and Vasistha (VII) respectively. The family-hymns are composed by the rsi himself or by members of his family.
- The family-books contain the knowledge of the sacrifices performed by the family and also some information or reminiscences of important events of that family or gotra. The eighth Mandala of the RV is known as Pragatha Mandala, for it contains Pragathas which were indispensable for Udgatr rituals. It is known as Kanva Mandala, as many hymns are by Kanvas. The ninth Mandala ts also known as Pavamaniya Mandala/Soma Mandala. It is a collection of hymns addressed to Pavamana Soma. These hymns are believed to have been picked up from the other Mandalas, compiled and placed in a separate Mandala. About half a dozen hymns are however, found scattered in other Mandalas.
- The tenth Mandala of the RV has some hymns containing philosophical thoughts as well as speculations on the problem of creation. These hymns, viz. RV. 10.90; 121; 129; are the earliest specimens of the Aryan philosophical thoughts. In X.121, i.e., the Hiranyagarbha-Sukta, the Creator appears under the name of Hiranya-garbha, 'germ of gold’. The X. 129 or the Nasadiya-Sukta is noteworthy for the daring speculations which finds utterance in so remote an age. It explains the origin of the world in a philosophical manner. The X.90 describes the creation of Universe from the different parts of the Cosmic Man (Purusa) who being thousand-headed and thousand-footed extended even beyond earth as he covered it.
- The Mandala X contains almost all what is said in RV about Yama and the Pitrmedha (sacrifices for the dead). There are three hymns addressed to Yama. They are X. 10; 14; 135. The X. 15 is addressed to the Fathers. The X. 16 and 18 are connected with the cremation of the dead and other funerary rites. This set of hymns connected with Yama and Fathers is termed as Yama-Samhita on the analogy of Soma-Samhita (the Mandala IX) which contains all the Soma hymns. The fact that all hymns connected with Yama appear in the Mandala X does not necessarily suggest that they were composed at a later date. It is quite possible that at the time of the compilation of the RV these hymns were brought together and placed in the Mandala X, same as all the Soma hymns were collected in the Mandala IX.
- The Rgvedic seers attribute divinity to the striking phenomena of nature. The hymns are addressed to the deities like Surya, Soma, Agni, Dyaus, Prthivi, Maruts, Vayu. Apah and Usas. Though several gods are praised in the Rgvedic hymns, it Is not hard to discern an undercurrent of their oneness which is well-expressed in the RV, 1.164.46, according to which various gods are different manifestations of one Supreme Being (ekam sad vipra bahudha uadanti — RV, 1.164.46).
- A study of the hymns of the RV is indispensable for getting an adequate account of the Indian thought. “They are a source of the later practices and the philosophy of the Indo-Aryans, and the study of these hymns is necessary for the proper understanding of subsequent thoughts."5
- **Samaveda**
- The word Saman means melody and therefore the Samaveda is a collection of melodies. The texts used as musical notes in this Samhita are almost wholly drawn from the RV. There are only seventy-five verses of the Samaveda which do not occur in the RV, so the Samaveda has little literary value and is of no importance in the study of the Vedic mythology.
- **Yajurveda**
- This Samhita is essentially a guidebook for the Adhvaryu priest, who has to perform the sacrificial rites. There are several recensions of the YV, out of which six complete recensions are available. Samhitas of the Vajasaneyi school consist only the verses and formulae to be recited at the sacrifice. It does not contain the explanatory matter which is collected in the Brahmanas. It is, therefore, called Sukla Yajurveda. In contrast to this the Black (Krsna) Yajurveda contains both kinds of matter mixed up in the Samhita. The six complete available recensions of the YV are: (1) Madhyandina and (2) Kanua6 of the Sukla-Yajurveda and (1) Taittiriya, (2) Kalhaka, (3) Maitrayani and (4) Kapisthala of the Krsna-Yajurveda. Yajurveda resembles the Samaveda in having been compiled for application to sacrificial rites only. But while the SV deals only with one part of the ritual, i.e., the Soma-sacrifice, the YV supplies the formulae for the whole sacrificial ceremonial. Like the SV it is also connected with the RV but while the former is extracted from the RV. the YV, though borrowing many of its verses from the same rituals. It is known as Kanva Mandala, as many hymns are by Kanvas. The ninth Mandala Is also known as Pavamaniya Mandala/Soma Mandala. It is a collection of hymns addressed to Pavamana Soma. These hymns are believed to have been picked up from the other Mandalas, compiled and placed in a separate Mandala. About half a dozen hymns are, however, found scattered in other Mandalas.4
- The tenth Ṅṁndala îf the RV has some hymns containing philosophical thoughts as well as speculations on the problem of creation. These hymns, viz. RV, 10.90; 121; 129; are the earliest specimens of the Aryan philosophical thoughts. In X. 121, i.e., the Hiranyagarbha-Sukta, the Creator appears under the name of Hiranya-garbha, 'germ of gold'. The X. 129 or the Nasadiya-Sukta is noteworthy for the daring speculations which finds utterance in so remote an age. It explains the origin of the world in a philosophical manner. The X.90 describes the creation of Universe from the different parts of the Cosmic Man (Purusa) who being thousand-headed and thousand-footed extended even beyond earth as he covered it.
- The Mandala X contains almost all what is said in RV about Yama and the Pitrmedha (sacrifices for the dead). There are three hymns addressed to Yama. They are X. 10; 14; 135. The X. 15 is addressed to the Fathers. The X. 16 and 18 are connected with the cremation of the dead and other funerary rites. This set of hymns connected with Yama and Fathers is termed as Yama-Samhita on the analogy of Soma-Samhita (the Mandala IX) which contains all the Soma hymns. The fact that all hymns connected with Yama appear in the Mandala X does not necessarily surest that they were composed at a later date. It is quite possible that at the time of the compilation of the RV these hymns were brought together and placed in the Mandala X, same as all the Soma hymns were collected in the Mandala IX.
- The Rgvedic seers attribute divinity to the striking phenomena of nature. The hymns are addressed to the deities like Surya, Soma, Agni, Dyaus, Prthivi, Maruts, Vayu, Apah and Usas. Though several gods are praised in the Rgvedic hymns, it is not hard to discern an undercurrent of their oneness which is well-expressed in the RV, 1.164.46. according to which various gods are different manifestations of one Supreme Being (ekam sad uipra bahudha uadanti — RV, 1.164.46).
- A study of the hymns of the RV is indispensable for getting an adequate account of the Indian thought. “They are a source of the later practices and the philosophy of the Indo-Aryans, and the study of these hymns is necessary for the proper understanding of subsequent thoughts.”
- **Samaveda**
- The word Saman means melody and therefore the Samaveda is a collection of melodies. The texts used as musical notes in this Samhita are almost wholly drawn from the RV. There are only seventy-flve verses of the Samaveda which do not occur in the RV, so the Samaveda has little literary value and is of no importance in the study of the Vedic mythology.
- **Yajurveda**
- This Samhita is essentially a guidebook for the Adhvaryu priest, who has to perform the sacrificial rites. There are several recensions of the YV, out of which six complete recensions are available. Samhitas of the Vajasaneyi school consist only the verses and formulae to be recited at the sacrifice. It does not contain the explanatory matter which is collected in the Brahmanas. It is, therefore, called Sukla Yajurveda. In contrast to this the Black (Krsna) Yajurveda contains both kinds of matter mixed up in the Samhita. The six complete available recensions of the YV are: (1) Madhyandina and (2) Kanvaof the Sukla-Yajurveda and (1) Taittiriya, (2) Kathaka, (3) Maitrayani and (4) Kapisthala of the Krsna- Yajurveda. Yajurveda resembles the Samaveda in having been compiled for application to sacrificial rites only. But while the SV deals only with one part of the ritual, i.e., the Soma-sacrifice, the YV supplies the formulae for the whole sacrificial ceremonial. Like the SV it is also connected with the RVbut while the former is extracted from the RV: the YV, though borrowing many of its verses from the same source, is largely an original production. Some Important changes are noticed in connection with the gods. Prajapati, who is introduced only in the latest hymns of the Mandala X of the RV is now the chief of the gods. Rudra of the RV is mentioned by the name Siva as well as by Sankara and Mahadeva. Visnu now occupies a more prominent place than in the RV and a new feature is his constant identification with the sacrifice. In the YV high importance is given to the sacrifice and the correct performance of its each and every detail.
- **Atharvaveda**
- The Atharvaveda means the Veda of the Atharvans, i.e., the knowledge of the magic formulae. It is also called a “prayer-book of the simple folk haunted by ghosts and exploited by brahmins"
- The full title of the AV. i.e., Atharuahgiras (AV, 10.7.20) shows that is contained Atharvan as well as Angiras. The former refers to the auspicious practices used for healing purposes (Bhesajyani AV, 11.6.14). The hostile practices and black magic belonged to Angiras. The former is medicine and later is witch-craft. The AV is also called by a few other names. They are
- (1) Arigirasaveda (GB, 1.8; SB, 13.4.3.8)
- (2) Brahmaveda (AV, 5.5.6)
- (3) Bhrgvangiroveda (GB, 3.1)
- The AV is a collection of 730 hymns which contain about 6000 verses in its best preserved Saunakiya recension. The other recension is Paippalada, a part of which is available.
- The AV is divided in 20 books ((condos). The 20th book almost entirely consists of the hymns which have been taken from the RV. Besides this about 1/7th part of the AV is taken from RV. The most interesting of all the (condos of A V is the fifteenth. It is composed in prose and is devoted to the mystic exaltation of Vratya. The 15,17,18,19,20 kandas are regarded as later additions, but there is no reason to doubt the antiquity of the major part of the AV. For the study of the history of Vedic people the AV is certainly the most important of all the four Samhitas. It contains popular beliefs and superstitions prevalent among people.
- The AV contains songs and spells for the treatment of various ailments. Numerous charms are addressed to takman (fever) (AV, 5.2.2), who is imagined to be a demon. The subject-matter of the AV can be divided under the following heads:
- (1) Bhaisajyani Suktani — prayers for the treatment of diseases.
- (2) Ayusyani Suktani — prayers for health and long life.
- (3) Paustikani Suktani—prayers for benedictions by which the farmers, shepherds, merchants and others hope to gain happiness in their undertaking.
- (4) Mryara Suktani— they contain general prayers for happiness and protection against danger and evil.
- (5) Prayascitta Suktani — these are expiatory formulae and spells for cleansing oneself of guilts and sins.
- (6) Strikarmani (women’s rites) — magic songs referring to marriage and love, e.g. 3.25.
- (7) Abhicarakani — they deal with exorcism against demons, wizards and enemies.
- (8) Rajakarmani (king's rites)—these are a rather large class of magic-songs which are intended for the needs of the kings. They contain exorcism and formulae against enemies as well as belonging to the magic-rites which refer to the life of a king.
- (9) Philosophical and cosmogonical verses.
- **Brahmanas**
- The Brahmanas form the second part of the Veda. They are ritual text-books intended to guide the priest through long and complicated sacrificial rites. Elaborating the sacrificial ceremonies with the combination of the verses and rites, a new class of literature took shape in these theological treatises entitled Brahmanas. In contrast with the Vedas, which are poetical in matter and form, the Brahmanas are prosaic and written in prose. The Brdhmana-texts together with the prose part of the YV and AV are the early prose-text which Sanskrit as a popular language has produced.They represent the oldest prose writing of the Indo-European family.
- The period of Brahmanas was also that of Important changes in the religious thoughts. A great stress was laid on sacrifice, the observance of caste-system and asramas and the superiority of the priest.
- The AB and KB are the Brahmanas of the RV. Of them the former is older in date and bigger in bulk, but the later is rich in contents. Two Brahmanas of the SV are preserved; they are (1) Jaiminiya-Brahmana, (2) Tandya-Mahabrahmana, (also called Pancauirhsa Brahmana on the basis of its twenty- five chapters).
- Of the Krsna-Yajurveda, in the Taittiriya school, there is the oldest and the most important Brahmana, viz. Taittiriya Brahmana. The ritual explanation of the Sukla Yajurveda is to be found in extraordinary details in the Satapatha-Brahmana. This Brahmana is available in two recensions, viz. Madhyandina and Kanva.
- The Gopatha-Brahmana is attached to the AV. This Brahmana consists of two books. The matter of the former half is, to a considerable extent, original, the rest being borrowed from books XI and XII of the SB besides a few passages from the AB. The main motive of this portion is glorification of the AV and that of the fourth priest, i.e., the Brahman priest.
- **Aranyaka**
- They are the concluding portions or appendices to the Brahmanas. Brahmanas discuss the rituals to be observed by the householder but when in his old age the house-holder enters the uanaprastha asrama and lives in the forest, some substitute for the rituals is supplied to him in the Aranyakas. At this stage the symbolic and spiritual aspect of the sacrifices is meditated upon. Thus here the meditation replaces the actual performance of the sacrifices. The Aryanakas form a transition-link between ritualism of the Brahmanas and the philosophy of the Upanisads. The Aranyakas and Upanisads were originally called Vedanta; literally, the concluding part of the Veda. The RV has Aitareya Aranyaka which is attached to the Aitareya Brahmana and Sankhayana (Kausitaki) Aranyaka which also forms the concluding part of Sankhayana (Kausitaki) Brahmana. The Krsna Yajurveda has Taittiriya Aranyaka. The Sukla Yajurveda has Brhadaranyaka, the last six chapters of which constitute the Brhadaranyakopanisad.
- **Upanisads**
- Upanisads form the concluding part of the Veda. They are also called Vedanta, literally the end of the Veda, “not only because they came at the end of the Vedic period or they were taught at the end, i. e., completion of the Vedic instruction but also because the later philosophies found in them are the final aim of the Veda."Thus this name (i.e., Vedanta) suggests that the Upanisads contained the essence of the Vedic teachings. The term Upsnisad is derived from upa + ni + sad which means ‘to sit down near someone’. This refers to pupils sitting down near the preceptor at the time of instruction. Later this term was applied to a secret communication or doctrines which the pupil learnt from the preceptor towards the completion of his studies. The texts which contained these doctrines came to be called Upanisads and they are the foundation of many later philosophies.
- This term, i.e., Vedanta, used to denote the Upanisads is applicable to them because (1) they, on the basis of relative literary chronology, stand at the end of the Vedas, (2) they were taught to the pupil towards the completion of his studies, and (3) they formed the last portion of the dally Vedic recital.
- The number of the treatises called Upanisads is quite large, but some of them are not as old as others. The oldest Upanisads are partly included in the Aranyakas or the 'Forest-texts' and partly appended to them.
- It Is difficult to determine the chronology of the Upanisads due to different strata of composition In the various Upanisads. R.D. Ranadeobserves that the various 'tests’ used for determining the chronological order of the Upanisads, i.e. (1) the test of the language, the style, the vocabulary, grammatical peculiarities, (2) prose or verse style of the Upanisads, (3) the regular ideological development of the ideas like that of the ‘Two Souls' or of the Transmigration, etc. are not sufficient criterion to serve the purpose.
- The 13 major Upanisads are divided In these groups.
- I. Brhadaranyaka and Chandogya
- II. Isa and Kena
- III. Aitareya, Taittiriya and Kausitaki
- IV. Katha, Mundaka and Svetasvatara
- V. Prasna, Mailri and Mandukya
- **Brhadaranyaka Upanisad**
- This Upanisad contains six chapters.
- In the first chapter there is a good description of the Cosmic Person considered as a sacrificial horse and a parable to establish the Supermacy of prana which is followed by a number of creation myths put together. The second chapter contains the famous conversation between Gargya, the proud brahmin and Ajatasatru, the quiescent ksatriya king. The great sage Yajnavalkya Is Introduced making the division of his estate between his wives. The sage Yajnavalkya becomes the prominent figure in the chapters three and four, and as in chapter two he is seen discoursing with his wife Maitreyi, similarly he is seen in the chapter three discoursing with a number of philosophers in the court of the king Janaka and in chapter four with Janaka himself. These passages contain philosophical teachings of Yajnavalkya. The next two chapters contain a number of miscellaneous reflections on ethical, cosmological and eschatological matters, and the celebrated parable of the senses.
- The doctrine of karman is seen developed in the BrUp and is explained like this:
- Just as an embroiderer undoes a small portion of a piece of embroidery, and out of it creates a different, quite new and more beautiful form, so man, when he has stripped off his body and has rid himself of non-knowledge, creates for himself a different, quite new and more beautiful form.
- The ChaUp contains eight chapters. It declares the great fundamental dogma of the identity of Brahman with the Atman (chapter III) and the earliest statement of the doctrine that this manifold world is unreal (chapter V). The chapter V is noteworthy for its theory of transmigration. It also declare the one-ness of the individual soul and the Supreme Self by saying tat tuam asi (thou art that). The ethical basis the Upanisadic doctrine is expressed by a simile:
- As water does not stick to the leaves of the lotus flower, so evil actions do not stick to him who knows this that the Self is Brahman.
- (ChaUp. IV. 14.3).
- This is not to be construed to mean that a knower is given all laxity to do evil actions. The simile rather shows that the knower was regarded as inherently incapable of doing evil action. The sixth chapter contains the philosophy of Aruni who establishes an absolute equation between individual and Universal Spirit.
- Isa and Kena both are named after the initial words of these treauses. Isa Upanisad (40th chapter of the VS) contains only 18 verses and gives a valuable mystical description of Atman, a description of the Ideal sage who remains unruffled In the midst of temptations and sorrows, an adumbration of the doctrine of karmayoga and Anally a reconciliation of the claims of knowledge (jnana) and work (karma). Kena Upanisad consists of four sections, of which the first two are composed In verse and the last two In prose. The verse part of the Upanisad gives an argument for the existence of Atman as the inspirer of the various sense functions, It also favours the worship of the Ultimate Reality conceived as Atman and Anally makes an essay In spiritual agnoiology telling in a paradoxical fashion that those who know really do not know and those who do not know may alone be said to know the Ultimate Reality.
- **Aitareya Upanisad**
- There are three chapters of this Upanisad. The first is given to a description of the creation of the world by the Primeval Atman through Viraj. The next chapter contains the famous philosophy of Three births’, probably belonging to the sage Vamadeva, Vedic sage mentioned in the RV, 4.27.1. The idea of the life after death Is Introduced in this chapter.
- **Taittiriya Upanisad**
- This last four of the ten sections of the ḌṀ constitute the two Upanisads of the Taittiriya school, viz. chapter VII to IX forming the Taittiriya Upanisad and the chapter X forming the Mahanarayana Upanisad.
- **Kausitaki Upanisad**
- Kausitaki Upanisad is divided in four chapters of which the first Is merely an enlarged variant of the description of the path of the gods and the path of the Fathers as occurring in the Chandogya Upanisad and Brhadaranyaka Upanisad. According to the philosophy of the Kausitaki Upanisad prana is the first principle of life, then the principle of consciousness, and then is equated with Ultimate Reality, namely the Atman. This Upanisad also contains a number of social customs of that time.
- The Katha, Mundaka and Svetasvatara Upanisads are related to each other as all of them aim at visaging the highest philosophical truths in a poetic manner and thus became the chief sources from which the Bhagavadgita and later philosophical poems freely borrow. The only difference between them being that the Katha Upanisad is more or less a metaphysical work, the Mundaka is an emotional work and the Svetasvatara is a commixture of philosophy and mysticism. "All the three Upanisads seem... to have been written at the time when the Samkhya and Vedanta had not yet parted ways”.
- The whole of the Katha Upanisad which deals with the legend of Naciketas contains ideas about immortality of the soul as well as suggestion for the attainment of Atman. Two of the most important features of Katha are the description of the “chariot of the body” and the death and dream approaches to the problem of Reality.
- **Mundaka Upanisad**
- Mundaka Upanisad derives its name from being the Upanisad of the tonsured (munda), an association of the ascetics who shaved their heads. Its cosmology is suffused both by Samkhya and Vedantic Ideas. Its metaphysics Is based on Vedic ideas and has a ritualistic tinge. While as a work which can incite to mystic thought, it has no parallel in the whole literature of the Upanisads.
- **Svetasvatara Upanisad**
- Suetasuatara Upanisad derives its name from an individual author. In this Upanisad lie embedded side by side the Samkhya and Vedantic doctrines of cosmology, psychology and metaphysics and it is a very valuable Upanisad for the genetic study of the parting of the two great systems, i.e., the Samkhya and Vedanta.It exhibits various Vedanta-doctrines which do not figure in the earlier Upanisads. Among these maybe mentioned the destruction of the world by Brahman at the end of the Cosmic Age (kalpa) as well as its periodic renewal out of Brahman and especially the explanation of the world as an illusion (maya) produced by Brahman.
- **Prasnopanisad**
- It Is written in the prose. The nature, the style and the manner of presentation of the argument in it are comparatively modem. The six sages are mentioned as going to the sage Pippalada to learn wisdom and each of them asks him a question. The order of these questions is thus that they adduce an evolving philosophy from Pippalada. These question concern the origin of matter and life (prana) from Prajapati, the superiority of life (prana) over the other vital powers, dreaming and dreamless-sleep, meditation on the syllable Îḍ and the sixteen parts of man.
- **Maitri Upanisad**
- This Upanisad consists of seven chapters written in prose. Its many quotations from other Upanisads, the occurrence of several late words, developed Samkhya doctrine presupposed by it — all combine to render it a stamp of lateness. The text can be divided in two strata and the first stratum consisting of the first four chapters is comparatively earlier.
- The main body of the treatise expounds the nature of Atman. The king Brhadratha goes to Sakayana and begs of him what he himself has learnt from the sage Maitri who may thus be regarded as the promulgator of the doctrine of this Upanisad The first point in his philosophy is a description of the pure noumenal self who “arising from the body shines in his own greatness”, and second is the description of the phenomenal self called bhutatman who is subject to the influence of his good and bad actions and who, therefore, undergoes transmigration.
- **Mandokya Upanisad**
- It Is a very short prose Upanisad and Its importance lies in the fact that it lays the foundation of the later Vedantic philosophy. It is used more than any other Upanisad by the author of the well-known later epitome of the Vedanta doctrine, the Vedantasara. The great originality of the Mandukya Upanisad consists in positing the four states of consciousness, namely, wakefulness, dream, sleep and a fourth unnameable state of consciousness.
- **Vedanga**
- Contrary to the 'revealed literature’, i.e., the Vedas, there is another class of works which is regarded as a human work. This class of literature is closely associated with the Vedic literature and it is called Vedanga (literally — limbs of the Veda), the auxiliary sciences of the Veda. The Vedanga are six in number and they contribute towards the proper understanding of the Vedas. They cover six subjects which help in the proper understanding, recitation and sacrificial use of the Vedic mantras. These six subjects are: (1) Siksa (phonetics), (2) Kalpa (rituals), (3) Vyakarana (grammar). (4) Nirukta (etymology), (5) Chandas (metre), and (6) Jyotis (astronomy).
- The Vedanga literature is written in Sutra-style.
- The earliest references to the Vedangas is found in the Mundakopanisad (1.1.5) where they are mentioned as apara vidya. In this passage the term para vidya refers to Brahma-vidya, i.e., the knowledge of Brahman, while apara vidya refers to all the four Vedas along with the six Vedangas.
- **Siksa**
- These works termed as Pratisakhyas instruct the student in the correct pronunciation, accent, euphonic changes which the sounds undergo in the composition of the words, and other such topics which come under Vedic phonetics. Thus the Pratisakhyas are the oldest books on this subject. They are the earliest specimen, if occasional excursions in the Brahmanas are excluded, of the Indian endeavour in the field of the science of linguistics.
- As the name suggests the Pratisakhyas belong to every recension of a Vedic Samhita. The following Pratisakhyas are available to us
- (1) Rgveda Pratisakhya (ascribed to Saunaka),
- (2) Taittiriya Pratisakhya,
- (3) Vajasaneyi Pratisakhya(both ascribed to Katyayana).
- (4) Atharva Pratisakhya (supposed to belong to Saunakas),
- (5) Panca-vidha Sutra (It belongs to the Samaveda and shows the manner in which the Saman are to be sung at the sacrifice.
- **Kalpa**
- The period of Kalpa-Sutras is roughly estimated to be from 400 âṣ-200 Bc.The practical side of the Vedic religion can be seen in the Srauta and Grhya Sutras which deal with different, but complementary topics relating to the rituals. They are manuals for providing guidance for performing the complicated and long sacrificial rites. They provide valuable explanations of the Brahmanas. In this way they are directly connected with the Brahmanas. No Kalpa-Sutra represents rigidly any one Samhita. Even though a Sutra generally follows some Samhita, materials from other Samhitas are also included in it.
- According to their subject-matter, the Sutras are divided in two categories:

(1) Srauta-Sutras

(2) Grhya-Sutras

Srauta-Sutras

They provide guidance about the several rites performed at the sacrifices. They deal with elaborate forms of rituals in which the presence of a priest and usually of many priests in necessary. They teach the laying of the three sacrificial fires and performing big sacrifices.

Grhya Sutras

They deal with the rituals and other duties of the housholders. They also contain instructions on various customs, ceremonies and functions performed at the different stages of life of a man. They describe five great sacrifices, i.e.. the daily-sacrifices to the Brahman, gods, Fathers, men and creatures.

Two complete collections of the Sutras of the RV are available. They are Asvalaóana and Sankhayana Srauta and Grhya Sutras. The Samaveda has Srauta-Sutra of Masaka. Latyayana, Drahyayana; and Grhya-Sutras by Jaimini. Gobhila and Khadira. The Krsna Yajurveda has both the Srauta and Grhya Sutras by Manava, Baudhayana, Bharadwaja, Apastamba and Hiranyakesin. The Sukla Yajurveda is represented by Katyayana Srauta-Sutra and Paraskara Grhya-Sutra. The AV has Kausika-Sutra which contains an accurate account of the magic rites which are accompanied by the formulae of the text.

Dharma Sutra

This is another class of the Sutras which especially deals with customary laws and practises. The difference between the Grhya and Dharma Sutras is that “the weight in the Dharma-Sutra is laid on the wider relation of a man with the state, so that those sections, which deal the family became condensed and subordinate."They enumerate the duties of people according to their varna and asrama. The beginning of civil and criminal law can be seen there. The important subjects covering the civil law are taxes, inheritance and position of women. In the criminal laws the assault, adultary and theft are the main topics. The Dharma-Sutras of Gautama, Baudhayana. Apastamba and Vasistha are considered to be the oldest and the most important of the Dharma-Sutras. Their importance lies in the fact that they contain references to religion and that the gradual change from the Vedic religion to Puranic religion is noticed in them. The Grhya Sutras and the Dharma-Sutras are of a special value since they preserved for the posterity the domestic side of the religion practised by the ordinary householders, as opposed to the great sacrifices which were solely in the hands of the priests.

**Sulva-Sutras**

This is another class of the Sutras, directly connected with the Srauta-Sutra. They provide minute details regarding the measurement of the place of sacrifice and the fire-altars. They may, therefore, be regarded as the oldest books in geometry.

**Vyakarana**

The only representative of this Vedanga is the Astadhyayi of Panini. The principal subject of Panini was bhasa, the language of the common people and not the sacred language of the seers. The Vedic grammar, therefore, receives only sectional treatment from Panini.

**Nirukta**

This class of works also have only one representative viz. Yaska’s Nirukta, since the works of Yaska’s predecessors (mentioned by Yaska in his Nirukta) are not available to us. Yaska’s treatise is a commentary on the Nighantu, which is comprised of a list of the obscure words of the Vedas. This list (i.e., the Nighantu) was prepared in an attempt to protect those words from being unintelligible to the future generations.

**Chandas (Metre)**

Very little literature is now available on this Vedanga. The Nidana-Sutra (which has ten prapathakas) belonging to the SV deals with metre.

**Jyotis**

There is no work available at present dealing with ancient astronomy in Sutra style. The Jyotis-Vedanga is a later work of practical utility. It gives some rules for calculating and fixing the days and hours for performing the different sacrifices. Earlier works are lost to us.

**Vedic Gods**

The compilation of the jRV extends over a long period of time and hence it also contains the religious and philosophical thoughts prevailing in that period. Thus, the RV, being the oldest Indo-Aryan literary document, represents the earliest phase in the evolution of the religious consciousness.

The religion in its widest sense means men’s conception of divine and supernatural powers and also a belief that their welfare depended upon those powers. This belief finds expression in various forms of worship. Myths are woven around agod describing his origin, his actions, his companions and enemies, etc.

The Rgvedic hymns are addressed to various gods, like Agni, Indra, Varuna, Soma, etc. and goddesses like Usas, Ratri, etc. Through his prayers to the various gods, the worshipper wants to get wealth, good health, victory, progeny and in short all the worldly happiness. The epithet deva (brilliant) (from div), which was originally applicable to sky, sun, moon, dawn, fire and lightening, etc., was afterwards extended to earth, storm, rain, and even night. Yaska calls every object of worship a devata.

The Vedic poetiy and the Vedic mythology are, to a considerable extent dominated by priestly conventions. These details often obscure the mythology and sometimes present it in a different shape making an attempt to get a clear view of Vedic god very difficult. Moreover, the language of the Vedas, which is far removed even from the earliest commentators and exegetes like Yaska, Sayana and others, is difficult and obscure at many places. The obscurity as well as deviation in the meaning, due to various possible interpretations of the mantras, have also contributed to some extent to the vagueness in the true picture of a Vedic divinity. Sayana's commentary, though very helpful, deals mainly with the ritualistic application of the hymns and the mythology contained in them is mentioned only secondarily. Nevertheless. Sayana quotes Yaska and Saunaka to elucidate the mythology connected with the text. The personality of many a Vedic divinities is complex and heterogeneous, since their characteristic features are not only inconsistent and Incomplete but sometimes, even mutually contradictory. This can be attributed to the fact that the Vedic mythology is essentially an evolutionary mythology which has been reacting and responding to the various vicissitudes in the cultural history of the Vedic age.It can be assumed that in the different stage of evolution, the Vedic mythology was dominated by different gods, which has resulted in the complexity to their personality. To get a clear view of the personality of a god, therefore, it is necessary to study it as a growing personality and the whole mythology sis an evolutionary mythology.

Some Vedic myths seem to have started with an attempt to explain some natural phenomenon which puzzled the Vedic seers’ minds. In the process the natural phenomenon got deified and myths grew around it. The Vedic poets were the children of nature. Many natural events or phenomenon filled them with awe, admiration and reverence, which got expression in worshipping them as gods and seeking their blessings. Thus a great majority of the Vedic gods, excluding Prajapati and other later conceptions, can be connected with some element or event of nature. The dawn filling the sky with its golden light was personified as Usas. The thunder preceding a downpour was Imagined to be Indra’s fight with Vrtra in order to get the rains released from his hold-up (RV, 2.11.5, 2.14.2, AV, 18.89.7). The natural phenomena like the sun’s definite course, its daily appearance, the thunder, the storm, etc. also posed intellectual difficulty for the Vedic seer to which he attempts to find an answer by personifying the natural phenomenon and weaving a myth around it. These myths are, therefore, essentially early explanation of the mystery of natural phenomena observed by man. To Vedic poets a natural event was the work of a divine being resembling a human agent. Thus many powers of nature were deified. As these powers are closely associated with each other, the deities who represent them are also similar in character. Thus most of the Vedic gods like Dyaus, Surya, Agni, Savitr Usas, Maruts, Parjanya. Prthivi are symbolic representation of various elements and powers of nature. It is also seen that certain aspects of one deity have got separated in the form of another deity; for example, Vivasvat, Savitr, Mitra. Pusan are fourgods representing different aspects of the sun’s glory. Savitr, i.e., the inspirer, Pusan, the life-giver and Vivasvat, the luminous were at first epithets of the sun, then names of the sun and finally they became independent solar gods beside Surya. The general line of mythological evolution of the deified phenomenon of nature is like this.

First, the phenomenon of nature, then its personification, then (in some cases) separation of some abstract conception of some prominent quality of the deity, and possibly again the personification of that quality of deity.

In some cases, like Usas where the name of the deity is the same as its natural phenomenon the personification is not complete. Such gods represent the natural phenomenon as well as its presiding deity. Both of them exist in the poet’s mind and find expression in turns. Dyaus, Prthivi, Surya, etc. fall in this category. Of many hymns whose deity is said to be Usas, Surya or Prthivi, etc. the subject-matter is primarily or even exclusively not that deity but a superhuman and, in most cases, a kind power behind a physical phenomenon. Therefore, the personification of these deities is not complete. The personification of Agni and Soma is also checked due to their visible forms, i.e., the element of fire and the sacrificial drink respectively, which are called by the same name. But in other cases the name of the deity is not same as that of the connected natural phenomenon. The origin of Indra and Varuna cannot be easily traced back to some natural phenomenon as the personification in such cases is complete.

**Prayers to the Gods**

The prayers to the Vedic gods extol their attributes, their form, their deeds, and also their companion gods.Through these prayers the worshipper wishes for the favour of the gods bestowed upon him in the form of prosperity, good health, long life and progeny of strong sons.

**Gods at Sacrifice**

The Vedic gods are invoked by worshippers and urged to accept the offerings made to them. On being invoked the gods come to the sacrifice in their luminous chariots drawn by steeds, etc. Various offerings are made to them at the sacrifices. The gods get these offerings through Agni. Soma is the favourite drink of the gods which the sacrificer makes sure to offer. Gods are also offered milk and honey.

The Vedic gods are sometimes invoked in pairs, e.g. Mitra-Varunau, Agni-Somau, and sometimes also in groups. Rudra, however, is an exception who dislikes being invoked along with other gods. The worshipper wishes not to make Rudra angry by inviting him to the sacrifice along with other gods by reciting a common invocation Ma tva Rudra cukrudhama namobhih/ma dustuti ursabha ma sahuti// (RV, 2.33.4).

**Abstract Deities**

There are some deities who are deified forms of abstract ideas. Sraddha (faith), Many ṝ (anger), Visvakarman (the world's master-builder) and Prajapati (lord of creatures) are abstract deities. Nearly all these deities appear in the tenth Mandala of the RV.

**Minor Deities**

Minor deities like Rbhus, Apsaras, Gandharvas also appear in the RV. Several demons and evil-spirits are mentioned as enemies of the gods whom they subdued. However, in the RV as ṝãṁ does not mean an enemy of the gods as in the epics and later literature. In the RV it means “one possessing wonderful powers”or 'god’. For evil demons the word dasa or dasyu is used.Non-Aryan aboriginal habitants are also called dasa and dasyu.

**Fathers**

Apart from the gods the dead also received worship from the living members of their families. For the Vedic people death was not the end of one’s existence. The ancestors, called pitr (Fathers), continue to be respected as elders in the family. They are worshipped like gods and offered food and water. Thus, the worship of the ancestors is connected with the sense of solidarity of the family.

**Characteristics of the Vedic Gods**

Most of the Vedic gods are personification of some element or event of the nature. They possess certain distinguishing characteristics, while many attributes and powers are common to all the deities. All of them are described as brilliant, powerful and benevolent.

**Indefinite Character**

Unlike the Greek gods, the Vedic gods have no clear outlines to distinguish them fully from each other. There is a lack of distinctive attributes. Moreover, the epithets of one god are often shared by many other gods. Thus a Vedic god possesses many attributes which are common to several gods and the few distinctive attributes which he has are also sometimes transferred to other gods. This has often resulted in the indefiniteness of the character of the god. Several gods are said to have produced the sun, placed it in the sky and prepared a path for it. This practice has resulted in the gods having very few individual characteristic qualities. Too many figures are overlapping and too many offices are commonly held by two or more deities. Agni is also given a thunderbolt, he wins cows and waters (RV, 1.59.6) — all attributes belonging to Indra. Visnu and Indra are both mentioned as possessing powers belonging to the other. Agni is said to be knowing all the created beings (RV, 3.55.10) whom he watches (RV, 10.187.4). At another place (i.e., RV, 3.62.9) similar words are said for Pusan. About Agni.a terrestrial god it is said that he stretched the heaven and earth (RV, 3.6.5) and that his greatness exceeds that of the heaven.(RV, 1,59.5)

**Transference of Epithets**

One Vedic god receiving the epithets of another god is not rare. Often the epithets of one god are shared by many gods. Agni (RV, 1.59.6; 3.20.4) as well as Soma (RV, 9.26.3; 28.3; 9.37.5; 9.98.5) is called a “Vrtra-slayer” (urtrahan).

This is an epithet of Indra since the killing of Vrtra is considered to be the most heroic deed of Indra.Brhaspati too receives many epithets like uajrin, ‘killer of Vala’, ‘releaser of cows', etc. which are mainly ascribed to Indra.

**Identification**

Due to the lack of definite characteristics and the transference of distinctive epithets one god is sometimes identified with other gods. The opening verses of the RV, 2 is marked by the identification of Agni with all gods, i.e., Dyaus, Indra, Visnu, Rudra, Bhaga, etc. Aditi is identified not only with all gods, but with all that has been and shall be born; air and heaven. The tendency of identifying one god with another has led to a monotheist doctrine — “All gods are same, only sages describe them differently." (RV, 1.164.46)

**Dependentness**

The Vedic deities are also represented as dependent on other gods. Varunaand Surya are subordinate to Indra; Varuna and Asvins submit to the power of Visnu. Indra, Mitra, Varuna, Aryaman, Rudra cannot violate the ordinances of Savitr.

**Moral Tone**

The character of the Vedic deities is moral. All the gods are truthful and wise. They are friends and guardians of honesty and righteousness. Varuna is considered to be the chief upholder of the moral law, i.e., rta.

**Benevolence**

The Vedic gods are benevolent and kind-hearted. They bestow prosperity upon the worshippers and their attitudetowards them is veiy friendly. Prayers to these gods express profound love, respect and admiration. As friends of the sacrificer they destroy his enemies.

**Hostility Of The Gods**

Some gods are described displaying hostility. Agni. Surya and Indra (RV, 3.48.4; 6.18.2; 4.10.12; 10.79.4) are the killers of their parents. Indra is said to be breaking the harmony of heaven. He appears to have fought against the gods (RV, 4.30.3), broken the chariot of Usas (RV, 2.15.6) and threatened to slay Maruts (RV, 1.170.2).

**Origin Of The Gods**

The Vedic gods are in general described as the children of Heaven and Earth and sometimes of other gods. In the RV, 10.63.2 a triple origin is ascribed to the gods and here they are said to have been born from Aditi, from waters and from the earth (RV, 1.139.11). In one entire hymn (RV, 4.18) the birth and infancy of Indra are mentioned. The birth of Agni is referred to in many stanzas of the RV. The birth of Asvins, Maruts, Usas and others are mentioned. The generations of deities is also referred to in many stanzas, while some earlier (purue) gods are also referred to (RV, 7.21.7 etc.).

**Mortality Of The Gods**

In the AV, 11.5.19; 4.11.6 it is said that all gods were originally mortals. In the RV too it is suggested that the gods were mortals first and were granted immortality by Savitr (RV, 4.54.2 = VS, 33.34); by Agni (RV, 6.7.4; AV,4.23.6). They obtained immortality by drinking Soma (RV, 9.106.8, cf. 9.109.2-3). In the RV, 10.53.10 also they are said to have acquired immortality, but by what means is not clear.

The Brahmanas state about the mortality of all gods SB. 10.4.3.3) and of the individual gods, e.g. Indra (AB, 8.14.4). Agni (AB, 3.4) and Prajapati (SB, 10.1.3.1).The gods are said to have overcome death by austerity (AV, 11.5.19) also ḌÂ. 3.12.3.1). In the TS, 7.4.2.1 the gods are stated to have overcome death by performing certain ceremonies (i.e., sacrifices).

**Anthropomorphism**

A few anthropomorphic features of the gods are described. These features represent the main activity of that god. Head, face, mouth, cheeks, eyes, hair, shoulders, belly, jaws, arms, hands. Ungers, feet of the gods are mentioned. Head, arms, hands and chests are described as powerful assets of the warrior Indra. The arms of Sun are his rays. The tongue and limbs of Agni are but his flames. The belly of Indra is described to show his ability to drink an enormous amount of Soma.

**Henotheism**

The prayers to Vedic gods describe their attributes and powers. The Vedic poets engrossed in praising a particular deity exaggerates his attributes and enthusiastically eulogises him as the highest god, therefore, many gods seem to have exalted to the position of the supreme god. This tendency has added to the indefiniteness to the personality of these gods. In the RV. 2.1.3-4 where Agni is identified with six deities, two of them are Indra and Varuna who hold a very high position in the Vedic mythology. Varuna is considered to be the highest god, while Indra is an extremely popular warrior god with almost one-fourth of the RV hymns addressed to him.

This undeniable communalism among the Vedic gods has led Max Muller to propound his theory of Henotheism or Kathenthiesm, i.e., the individual gods alternately regarded as the highest. This theory has some element of truth but cannot be accepted in its full bearing. The feature of many Vedic gods alternately attaining the position of the highest god can be ascribed to the extraordinary zeal of the Vedic seer in eulogizing the particular god who is the subject of his composition. This has resulted in the epithets and remarkable feats of one god being transferred to another which forms the basis for the idea of Henotheism.

The hymns of the RV were composed during a long literary period and so it is not far-fetched to believe that different gods were given importance at different spells during this long period. It is not, therefore, necessary to believe that many gods were given the highest importance simultaneously. As Zimmermann has rightly observed “the indefinite shape and office of many gods as well as the absence of one recognised head in the Vedic pantheon is responsible for the possible though not real usurpation of Supremacy.”The tendency of the Vedic religious thought is far from henotheistic. Many of the gods are frequently brought together in a dvandva compound or mentioned in groups. None of them is raised above the rest. Moreover, in the hymns addressed to All Gods (visve-devah) even the minor gods are also praised along with the bigger deities. In this light it is difficult to accept the suggested henotheism in the Vedic mythology.

Among the Vedic gods no one is recognised as the master of other celestials like Zeus or Jupiter is in the Greek mythology. Zimmermann observes that “the Henotheism is out of question fora simple reason that ancient India did not possess a pantheon in the strict sense of the word, with a head acknowledged by all the gods.”

Henotheism in the Vedic gods can be attributed to an under-current of the thought that all the gods are different manifestation of one Supreme Principle. This has led the Vedic seers to arrive at the great monotheistic doctrine that “all the gods are same, only sages describe them differently", (RV, 1.164.46). In the RV, 1.16.50 it is stated that Agni takes the form of any god who is being invoked.

The Vedic gods are not always described as independent of the rest. Even the great. Varuna is subordinate to Indra. Sometimes their interdependence is also seen, for example, Savitr is said to 'rule over Indra’ (RV. 2.28.9), who lords over Surya (RV, 1.101.3) the concrete basis of Savitr.

**Number of Gods**

In the Nir, 7.5 Yaska says that according to his predecessors there were only three deities. They are

- (1) Agni — whose sphere is the earth
- (2) Vayu — whose sphere is the atmosphere
- (3) Sun — whose sphere is the heaven.

Each of these gods receives many appellations on account of his supereminence or the diversity of his functions, just as a priest according to his function is called Hotr (the sacrificer), Adhvaryu (the director of the sacrifice), Udgatr (the chanter) and Brahman (the possessor of the sacred lore). This view may be based on such passages as the RV, 10.158.1.Saunaka too upholds the idea of three deities, other deities being different manifestations of these three (Brhaddevata, 1.69).

In the RV, 1.139.11 the number of gods is stated to be thirty-three,who, in the groups of eleven each belong to the three regions of the universe, i.e., the earth, the mid-region and the heaven. The 5th chapter of the Nighantu, on which Yaska (Mr, 7.5-12.46) comments enumerates a number of minor deities and deified objects. The number of gods, therefore, far exceeds in each division.

Some drastic deviation in respect of the number of gods is also found. In the RV, 10.52.6 the number of gods is given as 3329 and in the AV, 11.5.2 it is 6333.

**Classification of the Gods**

The Vedic gods are classified in different ways. Though in the RV, 8.30.1 it is stated that — “None of you gods is small, all of you are verily the great", they are distinguished in the RV, 1.27.13 as great gods and small gods, old gods and young gods. But this distinction is nowhere explained.

Yaska (Nir, 7.5) adheres to the triple classification of gods as given in the RV, 1.139.11 which divides the gods on the basis of their place of habitat in nature or in cosmos. They are:

- (1) Terrestrial gods (prthivi-sthanah— Nir, 7.14-9.43)
- (2) Intermediate gods (antariksa-sthanah or madhyama-sthanah — Nir, 10.1 -11.50).
- (3) Celestial gods (dyu-sthanah — Nir, 12.1-46).

It Is significant to note that the list of gods contained in the Nighantu (chapter 5) on which Yaska (Nir, 7.5-12.46) comments, the names of Tvastr and Prthivi appear in all the three spheres, those of Agni and Usas in both the terrestrial and the aerial and those of Varuna, Yama and Savitr in the aerial as well as the celestial.

On the basis of the number of hymns addressed to a god as well as the frequency with which their names are mentioned in the RV, the Vedic gods can be divided into the five categories:

- (1) Indra, Agni, Soma;
- (2) Asvina, Maruts, Varuna;
- (3) Usas, Savitr, Brhaspati. Surya, Pusan;
- (4) Vayu, Dyavaprthivi, Visnu, Rudra;
- (5) Yama, Parjanya.

This statistical standard, however, cannot determine the place of a god in the pantheon. For instance, Varuna is celebrated (mostly along with Mitra) in only about thirty hymns and his name is mentioned about 250 times. In comparision to this Asvina claim over fifty hymns and their names mentioned over 400 times. Yet in the Vedic: mythology Varuna holds a far more important place, even higher than that of Agni and Indra. Thus, such a classification which is based on statistics does not tell about the position held by a particular god in the Vedic pantheon.

Another classification is based on the relative age of that mythological conception, i.e., (i) Indo-European, (ii) Indo-Iranian and (iii) Indian. But this is also not free from objections. Though Rudra, Brhaspati. Visnu, Pusan may be considered purely Indian, there is nothing to ascertain the age of other mythological conceptions. No god except Dyavs can be definitely traced back to Indo-European age. Thus this classification is open to a serious objection that there are no means to determine the order of origin of the gods.

M. Bloomfieldhas classified the gods the following categories:

- (1) Pre-historic — imported by Aryans into India, i.e., Varuna;
- (2) Transparent ot half-personified — the divine personifications whose naturalistic basis and whose starting point in human consciousness is absolutely clear.
- (3) Translucent gods whose origin has been obscured by the later developments of their personalities, i.e.. Visnu, Indra. Pusan, Asvins.
- (4) Opaque gods, who refuse to reveal their origin.
- (5) Abstract or symbolic gods: They embody an action, a wish, a fear in shape of a good or evil divinity.

**Varna-based Classification**

Just like men who are divided in four categories based on the four uarnas, i.e.. brahmin, ksatrlya, vaisya and sudra, the gods are also stated to be divided in these four social orders. Sayana in his commentary on the AB, 1.9mention this with reference to the creation theory of the VS. According to this Vama-based division the gods are classified in the following manner:

- (1) Brahmin gods — Agni and Brhaspati
- (2) Ksatriya gods — Indra, Varuna. Soma, Rudra, Parjanya, Yama, Mitra,Tsana
- (3) Vaisya gods — Vasus, Adityas, Rudras, Visvedevah, Maruts
- (4) Sudra gods — Pusan.

Dumezilhas divided the Aryan gods in three main classes. They are (1) moral, (2) military, and (3) economic. According to him Mitra and Varuna. represent the first class of the gods.

Indra and Vayu are presentalive of the second class and the Asvins of the third.

Zimmermann has pointed out that none of these classifications Is free from defects.The traditional classification of Yaska Is based on the classification on the origin, nature and activity of the gods which is not fully ascertained. It is very difficult to get a clear view of the Vedic gods to be able to classify them in these categories as (1) terrestrial, (2) atmospheric and (3) celestial gods.

A historical division into Indo-European, Indo-Iranian and Indian deities would require complete informaion about the date of every god in all these periods. But the available data leaves doubt about the dates of many gods.

The division proposed by M. Bloomfieldinto pre-historic, transparent, translucent, opaque and abstract or symbolic gods is based on the historical and exegetical principles “and is likely to suffer from the faults of both these principles".It is not ascertained which god belongs to pre-historic period and a god can be considered transparent or translucent on the basis of varying interpretations, hence according to Zimmermann, it is best to revert to Yaska's classification.

To serve a practical measure Vedic gods are also divided in two broad groups — major gods and minor gods. But the major or minor character of a god cannot be determined solely on the basis of statistical consideration. J.R. Joshi suggests the rationale for such classification, according to which the gods who play in a direct manner — a significant role in the evolution of the basic mythology of the RV. are considered major divinities.According to him the basic mythology of the Veda may be said to be constituted of such concepts as cosmic, ethics, hero-worship, fire-worship, Soma-ritual, fertility-rites and the pre-Vedic non-Aryan religious element and related to these concept are the gods Varuna, Indra, Agni. Soma, Visnu and Rudra respectively. The major character of a few other divinities like Pusan can be attributed to the cultural-historical consideration. The distinct and prominentindividuality ofYama may be regarded as suggesting his character as a major divinity in the Veda.

## 2. Yama’s Family and Associates

**Etymology**

The vocable yama occurs In the RV with a difference in accent and yields two different meanings which generally correspond with the accent. The oxytone yama, i.e., having acute accent on the last syllable means ‘a twin’. In this sense it occurs several times in the RV generally in the dual masculine or feminine. Yama was a twin, born with Yami (RV, 10.10.4), and so he acquired this appellative name. In a few places Agni too, who was a twin born along with Indra, (RV, 6.59.2) is called Yama’ for the same reason.According to Guntertthe word yama can be linguistically connected with two series of words: on one hand with Avestan yema, Let tic, Jumis, Mir. emuin—all meaning twins: on the other hand with Latin geminus, gemellus and Greek didumos which means both a twin and a hermophrodite.On the basis of the linguistic consideration and on the analogy of other primitive mythologies it can be deduced that the term yama which connotes 'a twin' is an expressive appellation of a god (i.e., Yama) and it highlights his original character.

An etymology of the word yama is suggested in the RV, 10.14.14 where this god is prayed to grant (a yamat) a long life. Here the vocable yama is derived from yam to grant, to give. In the Nirukta. 10.19 also yama Is derived from yam - yacch and it denotes ‘one who grants (fulfils) the wishes of the worshippers. Sayana gives this derivation of yama in his commentary on the RV, 1.66.8.

Hopkins remarks that the pun of yamat cl, in RV, 10.14.14 in the sense of stretch out’ shows that the sense of twin is lost from the name and as yet no thought of a restrainer (from yam. to control) was there in the poet’s mind. The Sabda-kalpa-druma also derives it from yam- yacchh, to give, and further gives some more meanings of this root.

The TS, 2.1.4 deals with the etymology of this word in a more specific manner. It says that Yama separated (ayuvat) the strenth from the gods, hence his name is Yama. Here the term yama is derived from yu, to separate; to take hold of; or to get possession. In this context Sayana remarks that since Yama deprived the gods of their power, he got the name yava, derived from yu to separate. This Yava became Yama due to the change of letters Ivarna-vyatyaya).Thus from yu the vocable derived is yava, and va of yava is replaced by ḍṁ making the formation as Yama. This can be taken as a case of dissimilation because here the phoneme v is lost and the phoneme m is substituted in its place.

Referring to the etymology of Yama in the TS, 2.1.4 and Sayana’s remark thereon Fatah Singh explains the similarity of the two words yava (i.e., barley) and yama. The Yaua has two parts distinctly marked, yet not separated.Yama too has in his original character of a primeval bisexual being, two personalities inseparably united in one form. The derivation of Yama from yam, to restrain, though normally possible, is not acceptable in this case, i.e., yama on account of the difficulties of the Vedic accentuation it would create.It does not also agree with the personality of the Vedic Yama. It was only at a later stage in the evolution of Yama-mythology that Yama came to be regarded as a controller on account of his self-control or because of his control over humanity.

The vocable yama also stands for some other gods, i.e., for Vayu in the SB, 14.2.2.11, and Aditya in SB, 14,1.3.4.

**Yama's Abode**

Yaska classifies the Vedic gods in three divisions on the basis of their dwelling region:

- (1) Terrestrial gods (Nir, 7.14-9.43)
- (2) Aerial gods (Nir, 10.1 -11.50)
- (3) Celestial gods (Nir, 12.1-46)

According to this classification Yama can be called a celestial god, as his abode is said to be in dyu (RV, 10.64.3). It is to be noted that in the Nighantu, on which Yaska comments enlists the gods of allied spheres and functions in its fifth chapter, Yama appears in more than one category of the gods. He is enumerated among the aerial (Nigh 5.4) as well as the celestial gods (Nigh 5.6), and Yaska also discusses this word at both the places. In the Nir, 10.20 Yama finds a place among the aerial gods. Here Yaska comments that Agni is also called Yama.It is obvious that Agni gets this name, which basically means 'a twin’, on account of his being Indra’s twinand it can be assumed that this Agni (i.e., belonging to the mid-region) is the 'lightning', which can be safely called Yama (a twin) because he is born with Indra (i.e.. thunder),another dominant deity of the mid-region(Nigh. 5.4).

At another place (Mr, 12.29) Yama appears among the celestial gods and referring to the RV, 10.135.1 it is stated that Aditya is here called Yama. Sayana has given two parallel interpretation of the 10.135.1, one of them being in connection with Yama and the other being in connection with Aditya. He interprets the second hemistich as: Yama ‘drinks’ in the company of gods and 2. Aditya ‘goes’ along with the rays (by interpreting 'Yama' as ‘Aditya’, deva as ‘rays’ and sampibate as samgacchate.

As discussed earlier the vocable yama is at some places used as an adjective, or an epithet of a deity, or even as a substitute noun of some deity (as in the RV, 1.66.8: 6.59.2 discussed above). Obviously when the vocable yama is under such usage it does not stand for the god Yama.

As a son of Vivasvat, Yama is a solar god and occupies a place in dyu (heaven). At more than one place Yama's abode is conceived to be situated in a very high region of dyu. Out of the three spheres of dyu which are brightened by Savitr. two are near Savitr, while third-one is a passage for the dead souls to enter the realm of Yama (RV, 1.35.6). Sciyana considers tisro dyavah occurring in this stanza as denoting the three worlds, i.e., dyu (heaven), antariksa (mid-region) and prthivi (earth). Out of these, dyu and prthivi belong to Savitr, because they are brightened by the sun, while the middle region is for the dead for going to Yama's world.

The difficulty with this interpretation is that it mentions two regions, dyu and prthivi (i.e., heaven and earth) being brightened by the sun excluding the third-one (i.e., the mid-region). which is situated in the middle of those two regions. The difficulty can be removed, if the rc is interpreted as follows:

There are three spheres of heaven which are illumined by Savitr. Out of them two are near Savitr, while the remaining one which is brightened variously is near Yama.This third sphere of heaven, which belongs to Yama, can be inferred as the highest. There are some very clear references to this in the RV as well as in other Samhitas.

In the RV, 10.14.8 Yama's abode is mentioned to be in parame vyoman, i.e.. in the uppermost heaven. It is significant because the third step of Visnu is mentioned to be in the highest heaven (RV, 1.154.5, 6).Macdonell observes that the third step of Visnu is indentical with the highest step.The highest step of Visnu is represented as his abode and is also connected with the ideas concerning the life after death. The pious men, after their death live happily in the abode of Visnu (RV, 1.154.5). The RV, 10.14.8 suggests that parame vyoman is Yama’s abode. The VS, 12.63; AV, 6.84.4; SB, 7.2.1 ḌṀ, 6.42 also state that Yama's abode is in the uttame nake. i.e., the highest heaven. The AV, 18.2.48 says that the Fathers live in the third heaven, which is called pradyauh. According to this stanza the lowest dyu contains waters (i.e., the clouds), the middle one contains stars and the third-one (which can be inferred as the highest) Is the place of the Fathers (and hence that of Yama too). According to the RV, 9.113.8, Yama’s dwelling place is stated to be in avarodhanam divah, i.e., the innermost part of the heaven.

It is situated at a far off place in the remote recess of the sky and a long distance has to be covered to reach there. This is also expressed by the RV, 10.14.1 that Yama himself had travelled to lofty heights to reach this region, where he secured a place for the departed souls.

In RV. 10.58.1 about a man, who is apparently dead or unconscious, it is said that his mind has gone to Yama, to a very far off place.The VS. 12.63 speaks of Yama along with Yami as being in the highest heaven. In the region of Yama the Fathers live blissfully. The Vasa cow fulfils all the desires \[AV, 12.4.36). It is conspicuous that although the Yamaloka is connected with death and the life thereafter, the fear of the Yamaloka or the dreadful ideas of the life therein are not present here. On the contrary, the worshipper prays for being granted a permanent stay in the world of Yama (RV, 9.113.8).

Here the dead souls, who are exalted to the position of the Fathers live blissfully with their benevolent kind Yama. Yama’s place (sadanam, 10.135.7) was built by the gods. The music is played here and Yama is propitiated with eulogies (RV, 10.135.7).

When a dead soul reaches the kingdom of Yama, he is received with warmth by Yama. Yama grants him a place there and also a right to share the oblations offered to himself. Yama also declares that by reaching there, the dead soul has become his own (AV, 18.2.37).

Yama is invariably connected with the south quarter (AV, 9.7.20).The RV, 3.27.2 is a rare instance where Indra, who is generally called the 'lord of the east’ is called the ‘lord of the south direction’ (AV, 3.27.2).

Yama’s house, it is stated, was built by the gods (RV. 10.135.7). The AV, 18.4.55 says that panca-janah made the harmya for Yama. Sayana quotes Nir, 3.8 which states the panca-janah as ‘Gandharvas, the Fathers, the gods, the demons and goblins'. Here an alternative group of five men panca-janah according to another view, is the four uamos and nisada.

Yama's harmya is taken to be a tomb or mound of dead by Ehni.

**Parentage**

In the RV there is a brief but clear account of Yama’s divine parentage. The RV, 10.17.1 states that Yama’s mother was Vivasvat’s wife. In the RV, 10.15.1 Vivasvat is mentioned as Yama’s father. Yama’s patronymic Vaivasvata' also refers to his father’s name (RV, 10.164.2; AV, 6.112.2).

This patronymic ‘Vaivasvata’ refers also to Manu, who is also a son of Vivasvat, but in such cases it is followed by his own name viz. Vaivasvata Manu’. When not followed by any name the term ‘Vaivasvata’ connotes Yamaand when it is followed by a proper noun 'Manu' it refers to Manu.

**Yama's Father**

As noted earlier, Vivasvat' is Yama's father. The vocable vivasvat also occurs with a variation in the accent which yields another meaning of the word. Vivasvat’ denotes the sun-god, the father of Yama (RV, 1.44.1; 10.17.1), while Vivasvat' means a sacriflcerand it is frequently mentioned in connection with the sacrifices and sacrificial activities. Agni is called a messenger of Vivasvat (RV, 1.58.1; 4.7.4; 8.39.3; 10.21.5).

Indra rejoices in the prayer of Vivasvat (RV, 8.6.29) and places his treasure beside him. Soma is cleansed by the daughters (i.e., fingers) of Vivasvat (RV, 9.14.5). Thus it is clear that the vocable vivasvat when accentuated differently connotes two meanings — (1) Vivasvat — a god who is the father of Yama, (2) Visvasvat— a sacrificer. The meaning can be determined on the basis of the accent. Barring a few cases (RV, 1.46.13; 1.31.31 this difference in meaning generally corresponds with the variation of the accent. It is apparent that Vivasvat, the sacriflcer,has an identity different from that of the god Vivasvat, who is the father of Yama. The exactness of the accent leaves little room for confusion.The indifference to the usual accent at some places can be attributed to the possibility of the Vedic accent losing their importance towards the end of the Rgvedic period, and these two words getting mixed up.

The vocable Vivasvat is formed as a present participle from the intensive from vivas of vas, to shine.This derivation is given in the SB, where it is said that Aditya Vivasvat illumines (vi - vaste) night and day (SB, 10.5.2.4).In the context of a sacriflcer the word Vivasvat is formed by shortening the second syllable of the perfect participle Vivasvat from vivas to worship.Sayana derives it from vas, nivase

- (1) vivasanam vivah tad yuktam visista nivasopetam
- (2) abiding in the chamber of sacrifices.

Vivasvat is prayed to grant a longlife to the worshipper.
