In the RV, 8.67.20 it is prayed “May the shafts ofVivasvat not strike them before the old age".In the AV, 18.3.63 Vivasvat is prayed to make the worshipper immortal. The SB, 10.5.24 explains the god Vivasvat's name by saying that he brightens night and day. It calls him Aditya, and this name is found elsewhere also (MS, 1.6.12). Thus Vivasvat is conceived as a god of light. Considering the etymology it can be said that originally he represented the morning Sun.In the YV (VS, MS, 1.6.12) and the Brahmanas Vivasvat is called Aditya, while in the post-Vedic literature it is a common name of the sun.

Vivasvat is also considered as the morning sky, which is gradually becoming bright, or as the god of bright sky in general, be it the diurnal sky shining with the radiance of the sun or the nocturnal sky lit by the moon-light.

Oldenbergcomparing Vivasvat with the Avestan Vivanhvant, the first mortal who prepared Haoma, believes that the reasons for considering Vivasvat a god of light are insufficient and that he represents simply the first sacrificer and ancestor of human race.But this theory of parallelism between the Avestan Vivanhvant and Vedic Vivasvat is, on the whole inadeqaute to account for all that is said of Vivasvat. The etymology of Vivasvat establishes his connection with light and brightness. His connection with ASvina, with Agni and with Soma, as well as the importance laid upon his seat, can be explained by the theory that he is the sun, especially the rising Sun.

According to Bergaigne the character of sacriflcer is prominent in Vivasvat, hence he is Agni, of whom sun is a form.Here it is clear that he confuses Vivisvat, the sacriflcer with Vivasvat the sun-god. It appears that he overlooked the accent in the word which has resulted in the difference.

**Yama's Mother**

According to the RV, 10.17.1 Tvastr's daughter Saranyu was Yama's mother and the wife of Vivasvat. This rc records her disappearance. The next rc (i.e., 10.17.2) informs that she was concealed (by the gods, vide Sayana on the RV, 10.17.2) for the sake of the mortals, and another woman resembling Saranyu (Savarna)was given to Vivasvat by them (i.e., by the gods). Saranyu bore Asvina and when this happened she abandoned (ajahat) two pairs of twins (RV, 10.17.2). U may be noted here that the verb ajahat in the RV, 10.17.2 is interpreted differently by scholars. Sayana interprets ajahat as tyaktavati janitvati iti arthah, i.e., as, “gave birth to". According to Muir ajahat means ‘abandoned’.Griffith also translates it as 'deserted'.Suryakanta gives the meaning of ha as (1) to leave; (2) to produce, to give birth toand thus he covers both the meanings of ajahat.

Another important word of this stanza is martyebhyah which is also interpreted in two ways —

- (1) As Ablative Plural—as the ablative plural it denotes that she was hidden “from the mortals". J. Muir's and R.T.A. Griffith's interpretation of martyebhyah as ablative plural creates a difficulty viz. who were these martyah from whom Saranyu was hidden.
- (2) As Dative Plural — in the second way the word martyebhyah, is taken as dative plural and it denotes then that “she was hidden for (the sake of) the mortals". Sayana follows this meaning and interprets that Saranyu was hidden for the sake of a mortal progeny (martyebhyah manusyebhyah tad utpattyartham apaguhitauantah — Sayana on RV, K.F. Geldner also interprets martyebhyah as dative plural."

The text (RV, 10.17.1-2) does not provide anything to suggest as to who concealed Saranyti, and why was she concealed. Furthermore, there is also a question, viz. in what way the motive behind the replacement of Vivasvat's wife was particularly beneficial to the mortals, and another question is here: if she was hidden from the mortals who were they. All these questions remain unanswered.

The data provided by the RV is inadequate for knowing the complete story of Saranyu and her offsprings who are mentioned as 'two pairs of twins’. Yaska (Nir, 12.10) gives the following story in this connection —

Saranyu the wife of Vivasvat gave birth to a twin. One of the children was male and the other female.After placing another woman resembling herself (for their care) Saranyu ran away in the form of a mare. Vivasvat took the form of a divine horse and followed her.

After that Asvina were born. Manu was born to Savarna. i.e. Saranyu's look-alike.

Here Savarna is clearly an appellative and not the proper name of that woman. She might have got this appellative due to her similarity (in looks) with Saranyu, the wife of Vivasvat.

**Brhaddevata**

The Brhaddevata (6.162-7.6) provides some more details of this story but there is no fundamental difference from Yaska’s version, Tvastr gave his daughter Saranyu to Vivasvat in marriage and a twin was born. These children were Yama and Yami. Saranyu placed her children in the care of a woman, who was similar to her in looks. She then transformed herself into a mare and went away. She had taken care of keeping Vivasvat Ignorant of all this. Vivasvat could not discern this change and took that woman as his wife Saranyu. Manu was born to her. Thereafter, on knowing that she was not Saranyu and that Saranyu had gone away in the form of a mare, Vivasvat too transformed himself into a horse and went to Saranyu. Saranyu recognised him. Another twin was born to them — and these two children are Asvina, also named Nasatya and Dasra.

Sayana's account of the story is based on Brhaddevata, 6.162-7.6 and he also quotes it in his commentary on the RV, 7.72.2. To this account Sayana adds that when Saranyu left her home she went to the lands of Uttara Kuru.

On the basis of the RV account, the details provided by Saunaka and Yaska and with the help of Sayana’s and Geldner’s interpretations, the story may be worked out as follows:

Saranyu was the wife of Vivasvat and Yama’s mother. The gods desired the creation of a mortal progeny. With this intention they hid the immortal Saranyu from Vivasvat as she, being immortal could not have produced a mortal progeny. Therefore, after the birth of her first twin, i.e., Yama and Yami — the immortal children of Saranyu. they (the gods) concealed her. Making an exactly similar woman, who is aptly called Savarna, i.e., having the same appearance (literally the same colour), they gave to Vivasvat as his wife. From this woman, i.e., Savarna, Vivasvat got a son called Manu. Thus Manu was the mortal son of Vivasvat by Savarna, who was not an immortal being. The gods had a definite purpose in this whole affair which was their desire to create a mortal progeny. Later Vivasvat and Saranyu also become parents of another twin. i.e., A&vina. It may be noted that here martyebhyah in t erp re t ed as dative pluraJ supports the idea of creation of the human race through Manu.

On the basis of this story it can be concluded that from Vivasvat two types of progenies sprang up, viz. (1) the mortal, (2) the immortal.It is significant to note that the female parent is different in these cases. Thus human progeny started from the first mortal, i.e., Manu, the son of Vivasvat. He is called the progenitor of the human race (manus pita— RV, 1.8.16; 2.33.13).All mortals are called manavas, i.e.. the sons of Manu (manavyo hi prajah— TS, II. 5.6).

It must be noted that the RV, 10.17 does not allude to Manu as the son of Savarna or Saranyu, Max Muller observes that the fable of Manu might have came in at a later date.

The Nirukta, 12.19 considers Saranyu as the night who is the wife of sun and who disappears as the sun rises.

Max Muller has discussed in detail the problem of Saranyu.According to him Saranyu is dawn and Savarna is the gloaming or the evening. He observes that Savarna is not a proper name and means no more than the word implies, i. e. ‘a wife similar to the first’. The evening is similar to the dawn in appearance, i.e., colour, so the name Savarna is appropriate. Day and night are the two children of dawn. In the mythological dialect the day is the twin brother Yama and night is his twin sister Yami.The disappearance of Saranyu after the birth of the twin refers to the disappearance of dawn, when it is day. Thus Max Muller visualises the whole episode depicted in the RV, 10.17.1-2 as a natural phenomenon. He works out the equation in this manner — “Saranyu is wedded to Vivasvat, i.e., dawn embraces the sky; Saranyu has left her twins (Yama and Yami, i.e., day and night) i.e., the dawn has disappeared, it is day; Vivasvat takes a second wife, i.e.. the Sun sets in the evening twilight."

In the RV, 10.10.4 Yama and his twin sister Yami are said to be the children of Gandharva and apya yosa (the water-nymph). Udgithaand Venkatatake Gandharva as ‘Aditya’ and apya yosa as his wife Saranyu. Sayana also considers apya yosa to be Saranyu. the wife of Aditya.

The RV. 10.10 consists of a dialogue between Yama and Yami. This dialogue-hymn has led to an assumption that Yama and Yami were the parent of humanity.

Yami is the seer of the RV, 10.154. This hymn is addressed to the dying sacrificers and it tells about the different drinks offered to them in the next world — soma to some, ghrta to others and madhu (honey) to yet others. According to ḌṀ, 2.20 soma is offered to the Saman-singers, ghrta (i.e., clarified butter) to those who performed sacrifices with Yajus and honey to those who recite Atharvans.

The MS, 1.5.12contains an episode connected with Yama and Yami and it tells why the gods created night. According to the story Yami mourned the death of her brother. She could not be consoled because she thought, 'he died but today’.

The gods, in their bid to comfort her, decided to create night. They created night and it became yesterday. Then she could endure her bereavement.

In some passages e.g., TS, 3.3.8 where Agni is called Yama. Yami is identified with the fire-altar.

Yami’s original personality as Yama’s sister is retained in the Brahmanas. According to the TMB. 10.10.21.2 Yami helped Yama to ascend to heaven with the help of a hymn. In the human sacrifice a barren woman is to be offered to Yama and to Yami is offered a woman who has delivered twins.

**Yama’s Messengers**

Yama’s messengers are mentioned in dual (RV, 10.14.12: AV.5.30.6) as well as in plural AV. 8.2.11; 8.11. Two Dogs, the owl (uluka). the pigeon (kapota) as well as bad dreams and mriyu are called Yama’s messengers.

**Two Dogs**

Amongst his messengers a comparatively fuller account of the two dogs is available (RV, 10.14.10-12). The epithets which describe the two dogs are:

- (1) Sarameyau (RV, 10.14.10)
- (2) Udumbalau (RV, 10.14.12)
- (3) Urunasau (RV, 10.14.12)
- (4) Caturaksau (RV, 10.14.11)
- (5) Asutrpau (RV, 10.14.12)

**Sarmeyau**

These dogs are sons of Sṁãṁḍṁ and hence they got this metronymic (RV, 10.14.10). Sarama was a messenger of Indra (RV, 10.108.2) who had been sent to the Panis with his message. Panis are a group of 'demons of the upper air’and primarily the enemy of Indra. They captured Indra’s cows and hid them in the mountain (RV, 10.108.11) Brhaspati found out their whereabouts and Sarama was assigned the job of carrying Indra's message. The R V, 10.108 contains a dialogue between the Panis and Sarama. At first she was mocked at and then persuaded by the Panis to become their sister and stay with them sharing their kine.

This proposal was immediately turned down by Sarama who warns the Panis of Indra's deadly weapons.

There is nothing in the RV showing directly that Sarama was a bitch. The Nir, 11.25 calls it the ‘bitch of gods’ Sayana also describes Sarama as deva-suni (Sayana on the RV, 10.14.10).

In the AV, 4.20.7 a four-eyed bitch is mentioned and according to M. Bloomfield this four-eyed bitch is Sarama. the mother of the two four-eyed dogs of Yama.

The JBr, 2.438.440 also contains the Sarama-Pani story. Here too her connection with the gods as Indra's messenger is clear but it is not expressed that Sarama is a bitch.

In the RV, 7.55.2-3 this vocable occurs in its singular form as Sarameya, but in this context it does not stand Ãîã Yama’s messenger-dog. Sayana explains the word as 'born in the race of Sarama’. It shows that gradually Sarameya became a synonym of dog in general.

Yama's dogs possess a ferocious appearance. Both of them have four-eyes and a broad nose (RV. 10.14.12). They are Sabala, i.e., brindled In colour. In the AV. 8.1.9 one of them Is spoken of as Syama, while the other one as Sabala. M. Bloomfield considers Syama and Sabala to be the proper names of these dogs.In that case here a word which is an adjective in dual (RV, 10.14.11) becomes, in singular, the proper name of one of the dogs (AV, 8.1.9).

**Udumbalau**

Another epithet used for Yama’s dogs is udumbalau (RV, 10.14.12; AV, 18.2.3) Sayana explains it as Vistirnabalau Monier Williams and Suryakantatranslate it as ‘copper-coloured’, Macdonell,Hillebrandtand Oldenbergdo not translate it and Macdonell comments that there is no means of throwing light on its sense.

Usually the word udumbalau is translated as ‘brown’ or 'copper-coloured' on account of its supposed identity with Udumbara—i.e., the fig-tree, the fruit of which is light brown in colour.

F.B.J. Kuiper observes that taking udumbala for udumbarais purely conjectural; Sayana’s explanation is also not satisfactory, and since dogs are called sabalauin the RV, 10.14.10 there is no reason to suppose that udumbalau at this place denotes a colour at all. He further says that this word can be connected with the Munda word udumbara meaning ‘fat, obese’, on the basis of dum-dum, meaning having protruded ‘stomach’.

The Kathaka Samhita (8.2) identifies udumbara with urjas, i.e., vigour, and according to this text, wherever the gods distributed *u*rjas (i.e., the vital power) there arose udumbara. Thus according to the Vedic tradition udumbara signifies vigour, and so Sayana’s translation as vistirnabalau cannot be dismissed altogether.

**Caturaksau**

On Caturaksah which is another epithet of the Yama's dogs. Bhatta Bhaskara comments — "having two marks above the two normal eyes".This ingenious explanation of the epithet 'four-eyed' accounts for the number 'four' by the two marks and two normal eyes which together give an impression of four eyes. (Thus according to him the epithet caturaksah appears to be fictitious). Sayana explains this epithet as having another pair of eyes above his (normal) eyes'.According to M. Bloomfield the term Caturaksah indicates here the capacity (of the dogs) to see both In day and night.

This epithet seems to be a figurative expression suggesting the sharp sightedness and alertness of the dogs. Agni is also called caturaksah (RV, 1.31.13), which can be satisfactorily interpreted only as ‘sharp-sighted’, and is a suitable poetic epithet for Agni.

**Asutrpau**

These dogs are also called asutipau (RV, 10.14.12), Venkatamadhava and Sayana explain it as ‘pleased in taking away the lives’. This explanation is acceptable to Keithwho translates is as ‘delighting in lives'. Velankar translates It thus — “those who feel happy in seizing the asus (spirits) of men."Macdonell also comments that the literal meaning of the term asutipau, delighting in lives implies delighting in taking them while they wander among men as Yama’s messengers.

**Functions Of The Dogs**

Guards

These two dogs are the guards of Yama’s house and also of the path (RV, 10.14.11), which leads to his realm. They sit on this path (AV, 18.2.12) as watchful guards, and a deceased is advised to hasten past them and join, without hindrance, the Fathers who rejoice in the company of Yama (RV, 10.14.10).

In connection with their role as guards, Macdonell remarks, that though there is a possibility, there is not sufficient evidence for supposing that they keep out the soul of the evil-doers.

**Guide of the Souls**

It can be deduced from the RV, 10.14.11 that they are guides to a soul who is proceeding on Yama’s path. Their help is sought for reaching the blessed dead who are enjoying the company of their lord Yama. Here a contradiction with the RV, 10.14.10 is seen where they appear to be stopping the souls from entering the realm of Yama, but this lack of harmony in their functions is not actual. In this connection Velankarrightly remarks that here the deceased is not advised to run past these dogs in order to avoid an encounter with them, on the contrary, he is advised to run to themand seek their help on his way to Yama’s kingdom. The next rc also renders support to this interpretation of atidrava sarameyau (RV, 10.14.10), where Yama is requested to entrust the dead man to these dogs, till he reaches his destination mentioned in the RV, 10.14.10cd.

Thus the main function of these dogs is to see that the souls of the dead reach Yama’s kingdom without any obstruction or difficulty. This is similar to the duty of their Avestan counterpart who frightens the fiends trying to snatch away the souls of the dead.That the fiends can harm the dead is also suggested in the RV. It is stated that apeta uita (RV, 10.14.9) should be recited at the time of cleaning a place for cremation (ASS, 4.2.10). The object is to drive the fiends away from the cremation place. According to Sayana this rc is addressed to the fiends who might be present there at the time of cremation.

There is no clear evidence of any such function of the Yama’s dogs as the divider of souls as good and bad.

The task of these dogs as messengers is also not very clear. The RV, 10.14.12 states that they move among people. The object of the AV, 8.1 is to enhance the life of a dying person and it is wished: “May these two dogs of Yama, one Syama and the other Sabala not come nearyou.” (AV, 8.1.9).

This suggests that, at the bidding of their lord Yama, these dogs move among the people and approach a person whose death is near. In the PGS, 1.10.24 there is a dog-spell, requesting the dogs (evidently of Yama) to leave the child alone “verily, your mother is Sarama, your father is Sisara and Syama and Sabala are your brothers". In the HGS we have mention of a dog-demon Si sarama (clearly a compound of Sisara and Sarama). The fact that he is a demon whose sons, according to the PGS-passage are Yama’s messengers connects the dogs with sinister association. Even today the typical mournful baying of a dog is considered omnious. It is believed to be a portent of the impending death of someone living nearby. Thus the arrival of death is connected with the wail of a dog and hence a wailing dog is driven away to avert the arrival of death.

The function of these dogs, therefore, seems to consist of tracking out among men those who are about to die, and in keeping guard on the path of Yama. They also guide a soul on his way to Yama’s world.

**Identity of the Dogs**

The Kathaka Samhita, 33.14 takes these two dogs of Yama as day and night.Dayananda Sarasvati also holds the same view.S.D. Satvalekartakes them as denoting day and night, as they also move and have access in all the four directions. The four sections of day and night are their eyes. The day is brown (sabala) and the night is black (syama). It is important to note that the words syama and sabala are used for the night and the day respectively in the JaimUpBr, 2.6, as well as the Kaustitaki Brahmana (2.9).

Western scholars hold a variety of opinions. According to Bergaignethey are the forms of Yama (as Agni) and Yami. Ehni takes them as south-wind and the north-wind.Bloomfield identifies them with the sun and the moon.This idea seems to be based on the few references, e.g., AV, 6.80, TBr, 11.2, where the sun and the moon are called heavenly dogs. In MadhSB, 11.1.5.1 also moon is called a heavenly dog.

The AV (Paip, 3.22.5) makes a notable statement that the four-eyed dog overlooks, by night, the sphere of night. Here also moon is called a heavenly dog. It Is observed that the sun and moon course across the sky. The abode of light and the blessed is situated beyond that. As they constantly watch the race of men dying day-by-day they are regarded as daily choosing people for the final journey. In connection with the sun and the moon the term mithu-drsa (i.e., seen alternately - RV, 1.29.3) can be easily understood as sun and moon alternate their presence.

The TBr, 1.1.2 states that two asuras flew up in the sky and became two heavenly dogs ofYama. It explicitly tells that there are two dogs In the heaven, and they are Yama’s dogs. It also tells that they were originally the asuras named Kalakanjas. In order to reach heaven they piled up a fire- altar. With the intention of foiling their attempt, Indra disguised himself as a priest and placed a brick in the fire-altar. But when the asuras climbed up to heaven, Indra pulled out this brick which resulted in the fall of the asuras. The asuras who fell down became spiders, but two of them flew up and became the two heavenly dogs of Yama. The Kalakanjas also appear in the AV, 6.80.2 where they are mentioned as having attained godship. There they are mentioned as three in number.

Max Muller considers these dogs as representing the mythological conception ofTime in its two aspects as morning and evening.Bruce Lincoln observes that hell-hounds in both Greek and Vedic mythology hold an important intermediary position at the border of this world and the next, between life and death, hope and fear, good and evil. Thus, according to him, the hell-hound is the moment of death, the great crossing over, the ultimate turning point.

Yama's dogs appear to be of lndo-European origin, because in other ancient mythologies too dogs are connected with the ‘lord of the other World’. In the Greek mythology, Hades, who is the god of the underworld and a brother of Zeus, has a three-headed hound named Kerberos, whom Zeus appointed as the messenger of the gods to the dark kingdom of Hades.Kerberos is a three-headed dog while Syama and Sabala (the dogs of Yama) are two, but they are mentioned as having four eyes. ‘Kerberos’ is allied to the word Karbura meaning spotted and variegated. It is significant that 'Sabala' the name which describes one of Yama's dogs also means the same. Kerberos is described as having serpents entwined around his body, which would naturally make him look variegated.Kerberos was the watch dog of Hades. Stationed at the gates, Kerberos allowed people to enter but devoured them, if they tried to leave.

'Sarameyah' is an epithet of Yama’s dogs which has some similarity with Hermes who in the Greek mythology is the messenger of the gods and is called Mercury by the Romans. He was the son of Zeus and Maia, and patron of travellers and shepherds. He wore a winged cap and had wings attached to his ankles, enabling him to travel from place to place with the speed of the wind.

Max Muller observes that the word sarameya approaches very near, linguistically as well as philologically, to Hermeres or Hermes, who is the guide of the travellers and particularly of the souls who travel on their last journey, he is the psychopompos.In this functional aspect also Hermes can be compared with the dogs of Yama who have a similar responsibility.

In the Avesta also a dog guards the path to the next world. The Kinvat bridge, which leads to the next world is said to be guarded by a four-eyed and yellow-eyed dog. He scares away the fiends and saves the souls of the holy ones to be dragged to the hell. According to the Vendidad VIII. 14-22, when a man dies and his soul parts from the body, the evil corpse-demon (Druz-Nasu) falls upon the dead.This demonis driven away by the means of the look of the dog'. In practise a yellow dog with a spot over his each eye, or a white dog with yellow ears (because the dog who guards the path to the next world is described as 'four-eyed and yellow-eared'), is brought near the dead body. It is made to look at the dead whereupon the demon flees back to hell.An allowance of bread is also made for the dogs three times in summer and two times in winter. On this account it is wished that they should come to assist the soul at the Kinvat bridge.Vendidad ÕIII.9 states if two dogs, i.e., the shepherd-dog and the house-dog passes by the house, they should not be driven away from there.

In the Egyptian mythology also ‘Anubis', a Jackal-faced dog Is the conductor of the dead.

**Birds**

The RV. 10.165 contains several verses about the pigeon and the owl. Here the pigeon is clearly mentioned as the messenger of Yama, who is identified with mrtyu (death) itself. The pigeon is called Nirrti's messenger too (RV, 10.165.4). Hooting of an owl as well as a pigeon placing Its foot near fire (settling near Agni, i.e., reaching well inside the house) are considered bad portents (RV, 10.165.4). According to the AGS, 3.7.7 this hymn (RV, 10.165) is recited If a pigeon enters the house. In this hymn the pigeon is called paksini hetihi, i.e., a winged missile (RV, 10.165.2.3).

In AV, 6.29.2 both the pigeon and the owl are called the messengers of Nirrti They are sent by their master, but sometimes they come on their own. Gods are prayed earnestly that the kapota be expelled from the house and the ill-omen be rendered inefficacious. According to the Kausika Sutra, 46.7, the AV, 6.27 and the following two stanzas are recited while the great consecration (maha santi) is being poured. These stanzas are used for averting the ill-omen when a pigeon or an owl enters the house. In AV, 6.28.3 Yama is identified with mrtyu and hence the presence of a pigeon who is Yama's messenger, is feared. In the Brahmanas and Grhya Sutras also a pigeon's entry in the house is considered ominous (PB, 5.8; AGS, 4.15; KS, 5.5).

According to the AV, 10.3.6 the ominous shreak of a bird is rendered futile by wearing a particular amulet. If a person sees some evil dream, if a wild beast runs an evil course (papam srtirri), if there is too much sneezing, if the evil utterance of bird is heard, the varana-amulet is said to protect him from all these ill-omens.

Commenting on these stanzas S.D. Satvalekar observes that they are obscure and it is difficult to say what forthcoming misfortune these birds indicate. He further suggests that since pigeons are used for sending messages, it may be that, it is prayed here that the pigeons who carry messages should bring good news, and not a death or defeat of their warriors.In the Mbh the pigeons along with vultures, flre-flies and bees are considered to bring ill-luck (Mbh, 13.104.14 f.) Red-footed birds and pigeons are particularly inauspicious (Mbh, 5.143.18 f; 7.6.56). It is a horrible portent for a person if a pigeon alights on him.It is also said that a red-brown owl with green eyes attacking a crow foretells misfortune (Mbh, 5.143.18 f; Ram, 7.6.56). In the Matsya-Purana also kapota and uluka are stated to be precursors of ill-fate. A pigeon entering the house forebodes death of the house-holder (MP, 237.6), while the hooting of an owl forebodes the loss of property as well as death of the family head (MP, 237.13). To nullify this portant it is advised to recite stanza devah kapotah (RV, 10.165).

**Dream**

In the AV, 16.5.1 dream is identified with death itself. It is also called 'Son of Yama' (AV, 6.46. l).This stanza is addressed to svapna (dream). It is important to note that it is not duhsvapna or the evil dream who is addressed here. The text states that the name of svapna is araruand his mother is Varunani. According to Sayana on the RV, 7.87.1 Varuna is connected with darkness.The TS, 2.1.7.4 also says that the day is said to belong to Mitra and night to Varuna.

In the present stanza (AV, 6.46.1) Varunani, i.e., wife of darkness is said to be the mother of dream. In the next stanza (AV, 6.46.2) the dream is prayed to save the worshipper from evil dreams.

Dream is also called karana, (instrument) of Yama. He comes from the region of Yama and enters the mortals and causes (brings) evil dreams (AV. 19.56.1).

Dream is also called the son of Grahi (disease),Nirrti (destruction), Nirbhuti (poverty) Parabhuti (defeat) etc. (AV, 16.5).

The dream mentioned as son of Yama (AV, 6.46.1) and of Grahi. and others (AV. 16.5) only reflects the belief that a bad dream forebodes these undesirable situations. It is considered to be a portent of ill-luck, disease, poverty, destruction and also of death. Similarly a bad dream is not a son of Yama in the literal sense of the word. Yama presides over the evil dreams (duhsuapnadhidevo Vaivasvato — Sayana on RV, 10.164.2) and therefore Yama's auspicious sight is sought by the worshipper for the dissipation of the evil dreams, The god who Is addressed in the hymns about dream or evil dream is. therefore, Yama. The AV, 16.5,6,7,8 are connected with the evil dreams. The rsi of these verses is Yama and they are addressed to svapna (i.e., the dream). The refrain of the poem Is the prayer to svapna urging him to save the worshipper from the evil dreams— sa nah suapnaduhsvapnal pahl.

**Mrtyu**

Mrtyu is called a messenger of Yama (AV, 18.2.27). He takes the life of the dead to the Fathers (TS, 18.27; AV, 18.2.27). Like Yama, he is also mentioned as holding a padbisa (foot- fetter) (AV, 16.8.32). Though Yama is not considered to be a god of death in the RV. there are a few references in the later Vedic literature which almost identify Yama and Mrtyu. In the KathaUp Yama is repeatedly called Mrtyu establishing their complete identification.

Mrtyu is conceived to be death, i.e., the phenomenon of cessation of life, or Death as personified. As a divinity representing death he is different from Yama, who is the regent of the departed. Yama is offered oblations of ghi (ghrtaj with a pray er that he should grant a long life to the worshippers (RV, 10.14.14). Death is sought to be warded off through Yama's help and the worshipper desires assurance (AV, 3.14.4)The ḌÂ, 1.8.6 has a prayer for deliverance from death and it is addressed to Yama. This suggests that Yama is not considered a killer but a savior.

In the RV mrtyu is primarily the phenomenon of death. Gradually he came to be apotheosised as a divinity presiding over death. There are four res, RV, 10. 18.1-4, addressed to Mrtyu and Sayana observes that Mrtyu is the ñṁmṛ of a god who kills everybody.In the RV. 10.18.1 Mrtyu is prayed to go away by a different path. i.e., a path which Is his own and is distinct from the path of the gods. He is also prayed not to harm the progeny.

In the next stanza the relatives of the dead are addressed. It says that after effacing Mrtyu’s foot-step and thereby avoiding the path of death they would now have a longer and better life: "May you Î sacrificersi enriched with progeny and affluence be cleansed (suddha) and pure (puta)."It is wished that those who are living should remain separate from the dead: “may our Invocation to gods today be successful: let us go forward to merriment and laughter, assuming a longer and better life. I erect this circle of stones for the protection of the living, so that none other of them may approach this limit. May they live a hundred years, occupied in many holy works, and keep death hidden by this mound.”

In the RV, 10.18.1 Mrtyu is prayed to go away on his own path which is distinct from that of the gods (itaro devayanat) and to spare the progeny of the sacriflcer (RV, 10.18.1; VS, 35.7; AV, 2.2.21). This suggests that the path of Mrtyu is the pitiyana. It is significant to note that he is called caksusmat (one who has eyes, i.e., one who sees properly) and srnvan (one who hears properly)with a wish that he would see his own path and go away by it and also listen to the prayers of the worshippers. In the AV, 18.2.27, Mrtyu is called pracetas. i.e., wise, and there he is mentioned as a wise messenger of Yama.

Though Yama is not considered to be a god of death in the RV, he is regarded with a certain amount of fear due to his indirect connection with death, so much so that it is tempting to identify him with Mrtyu in the RV, 10.165.4d (also AV, 6.28.3; 63.3).

As a pigeon, who is Nirrti’s messenger, comes near the place of the sacrifice, the sacrificer wants to make atonement for this and prays to gods for the well-being of his “bipeds and quadrupeds” (i.e.. his people and his cattle). As seen earlier the hooting of an owl as well as pigeon's entry in the house is considered to be very inauspicious because it forebodes ill-fate for the house-head. It is earnestly prayed that the bird who has entered the house be auspicious: “may Agni accept our oblations and may this pigeon depart from here". It is also wished that this bird, who is as dreadful as a winged weapon (paksini hetih), should not harm anybody. It is prayed that ‘may the shriek of the owl be in vain, may the pigeon's entry in the house be in vain". Salutations are offered to Yama who is called the god of death and who has sent his messengers, i.e., the pigeon and the owl. (RV, 10.165.1-4)

In the RV, 10.165.4d Yama comes to be identified with Mrtyu, who is at an other place called his messenger. Yama has no direct connection with death, while Mrtyu is the divinity presiding over death, but here these distinct personalities are getting mixed up.

Sayana renders mrtyave as maryitre (i.e., a killer) and takes it as an adjective of Yama. S.D. Satvalekar follows Sayana and renders this pada as tasmai mrtyave marakaya Yamaya namah astu.

Griffith translates it (i.e., the RV, 10.165.4 cd) thus — 'To him who sent it as an envoy, to him be reverence paid, to Death, to Yama."This rendering of tasmai Yamaya namo astu mrtyave (RV, 10.165.4 cd) creates an impression that here reverence is being paid to two deities, i.e. Death (mrtyu) and Yama, but the context does not permit this because a dove is mentioned to be a messenger of Yama and Nirrti and not of Mrtyu. Griffith does not take mrtyu in this rc to be an adjective of Yama as Sayana does. Possibly he is addressing a single deity, i.e., Yama by two names, i.e., Yama and Death. This is not a sole instance where Griffith is taking Yama as Death. He calls a dove “an ill-omened bird, the messenger of Mrtyu"instead of that of Yama. Here in 10.165.4 too Griffith seems to be addressing Yama by two names by translating “to Death, to Yama".

Antaka is said to be the son of Mrtyu in the AV, 8.10.(4) 7.In the VS, 39.13 Yama is mentioned by the side of Mrtyu and Antaka.

Mrtyu is mentioned as having a number of weapons. He holds a noose (pasa) which is a special weapon of Varuna. Through sacrifice men seek to free themselves from the thousands of nooses of Mrtyu.Like Yama, Mrtyu also holds the foot-fetters (padbisa).He is said to be making people motionless by using the thunderbolt (vajra).,Thus Mrtyu is credited with holding the noose, the foot-fetters and the thunderbolt which are the special weapons held by Varuna, Yama and Indra respectively. Apart from these weapons, Mrtyu is also mentioned as holding wooden maces (sthanu) (AB, 12.3).

Mrtyu is, as noted above, a wise messenger of Yama and he takes the deceased to the Fathers (TS, 18.27; AV, 18.2.27). In the AV he is mentioned as the lord of the creatures (prajananij while Yama is mentioned as the lord of the Fathers (pitrnam) (AV, 5.24.13; 14).

Thus, Mrtyu rules over all living beings. The causes of death such as diseases prevail all over the world and so there are no restrictions over his activities (SB, 13.3.5.1).He himself is immortal (SB, 10.5.2.4). In fact he was created by Prajapati above all mortal beings as their consumer (SB, 10.1.3.1). Hunger is also called death. The SB, 10.6.5.1 reads as follows;

Verily there was nothing here in the beginning, by Death (this) (Universe) was covered, by hunger because Death is hunger.

From the ritualistic point of view of the Brahmanas, the holy works, i.e., sacrifices, are necessary for a man to escape recurrent deaths. If one does not offer oblations Death would get hold of him in every world and if he offers oblations to Death, he wards off in every world Death, even hunger (SB, 13.5.1.2).

Even gods were freed from the fear of death after performing sacrifices. Afraid of death they had rushed to Prajapati who instituted the Dasaratra and Visvajit sacrifice for them. By these sacrifices the gods defeated death and thus they became immortal (JB, II.329-31).

Death separates the world of living beings from that of the dead. In the event of a death It Is even more strongly wished that death should not come again and kill others. The Adhvaryu who Is performing the santikarman after the cremation of the deceased is done, puts an obstruction (paridhi RV. 10.18.4), a rampart — a line of demarcation between the living and the dead, which no one should cross. This rcis employed by AGS, 4.6.9 In the santi-karman which is performed after the collection of the bones. The Adhvaryu places the obstruction saying: “I place (here) this barrier (stone) for the (protection of) other living beings (relatives, sons and the like) so that none of them may go this goal (that the departed went). May they live for a hundred prosperous autumnsl May they keep off death by means of mountain (the stone).

According to Sayana, with the uttering of this verse a stone is placed as an obstruction for the safety of others’ lives anayajiuaraksartham pasanamparidhi-riipena sthapayanti (Sayana on RV, 10.18.4) Wilson translates paridhi as ‘a circle of stones’.

P.V. Kane comments that paridhis here are the encircling sticks of the sacrificial wood such as palasa, khadiraplaced round the Are. With imamJivebhyah (RV, 10.18.4) the fire Is surrounded on three sides by wooden sticks and a stone is placed on the north with the reciting of the last quarter of the rc as stated in the AGS. 4.6.10.

The mountain (parvata) mentioned in the RV, 10.18.4d is. according to Sayana ‘a heap of stones' symbolising a mountain placed in order to obstruct death, the killer, from coming again.

Mahidhara refers to KaSS, 21.4.24 and explains that the Adhvaryu deposits a huge lump of soil (mrt-khandam) between the village and the cremation ground.

Thus parvata which is explained as a heap of stones by Sayana and as a huge lump of soil by Mahidharasymbolises a mountain which is difficult for Mrtyu to cross and strike the people again.

Though Yama's jurisdiction, in the RV, is beyond death, he gradually came to be identified with mrtyu (death). In the KathaUp Yama appears as a preacher of the secret of the life after death. Here a complete identification of Yama and Mrtyu Is seen because Yama Is repeatedly addressed as Mrtyu.At some places mrtyu Is used as just another name of Yama.Vajasravas angrily says — "To Death I give thee" and Naciketas proceeds towards the abode of Yama pondering “Is there any work of Yama which he will perform today through me" (KathaUp, 1.1.5). Thus it Is seen here that on being given to mrtyu Naciketas understands that he is given to Yama. Sankaracarya, identifying Mrtyu with Valvasvata Yama, comments mrtyave Vaiuasvataya tvarh dadami iti.Thus it is clear that Mrtyu is considered to be Yama. This is further established by Yama being addressed as Mrtyu at several places.

**Fathers**

Fathers are called Yama's companions (RV, 10.15.8; AV. 19.9.51). He is also their king, with whom they live in the third heaven (AV, 5.24.14). They enjoy in the company of Yama (Yameria ye sadhamadam madanti — RV, 10.14.10) and Yama is also mentioned as enjoying the company of the Fathers (RV, 10.15.8), Yama is their lord (Yamah pitmam adhipatih.—AV, 5.24.14), and he is frequently called pitrmat. The SB, 7.1.14 also states that Yama is the king and the Fathers are his people.

The term pitarah is generally used for the ancestors. Originally pitr means a protector or a guardianand hence it denotes 'father'. It can be assumed that gradually the plural form, i.e.. pitarah came to be used for the dead fore-fathers, and later still for all the dead. Though the term pitarah is extensively used for the ancestors, it is also sometimes used for denoting a different sense e.g., (1) wisemen (RV, 10.88.18), (2) men sitting in assembly (sabhasadah, AV. 7.12.1), (3) warriors (RV, 6.75.9; VS, 29.46), (4) prana (or the life-breath) (RV, 10.130.1), (5) seasons (VS, 2.32). In the Brahmanas the term pitarah used for denoting seasons (SB, 2.6.1.4:32,7B, 1.4.10.8; 1.3.10.5. etc.).

All the dead souls who have gathered in the world of Yama form a special community and this is called pitarah. In this connection the rites from cremation to sraddha are important because they enable the deceased to join the rank of the pitarah (Fathers). It is said that through the samskaras after birth one conquers earth and through the sarhskaras after death, heaven (Baudhayana, Pitnnedha, 3.1.4). Great importance is given to the rites to the Manes. ManuS (3.203) says that the rite in honour of the manes is more important than the rite in honour of the gods.

The term preta refers to the dead for whom obsequial rites have not been performed.According to P.V. Kane the word pitarah is used in two senses, viz. (1) a man’s three deceased ancestors, (2) the early or ancient ancestors of the human race that were supposed to inhabit a separate world (loka) by themselves.

**Creation of Fathers**

The RV, 10.130.6 indicates that the Fathers along with rsis and manusyas were made at the time of the primeval sacrifice. In the VS, 14.29 also the creation of the Fathers is mentioned (navabhir astuvatapitaro as rjyanta— VS, 14.29).

Mahidhara explains that Prajapati prayed with the nine pranas (vital breaths) and then the Fathers like Agnisvatta etc. were created. He explains the nine pranas mentioned here by quoting SB, 8.4.3.7 which states that there are nine pranas—seven belonging to the head region and two to the lower region.The creation of the Fathers is found in SB, 8.4.3.7 with more details.

In AV, 10.10.26 and also in A V, 11.7.27itisindicated that the Fathers were considered to be a separate class of people like gods, gandharvas, asuras and men.

According to the ḌÂ, 2.3.8.2 Prajapati first made the asuras and afterwards he made the Fathers. Thus here also they are spoken of as a separate class because they were created separately. Obviously here pitarah is not a collective name for all the dead.

According to ḌÂ, 1.3.10.4 there are two kinds of Fathers: (1) divine and (2) human. Sayana explains that divine Fathers are those who are the lords of the pitrloka. Human pitrs are those who being dead attain that world through repast.Thus it can be said that those who passed of old and those who follow the countless ancient Fathers constitute a group of pitrs who are the invisible, powerful, immortal, divine beings. According to the SB, 13.8.4.12 the Father and men never mix together. They lead separate lives and they are not seen together here. The Fathers are said to be rejoicing in the company of Yama (RV, 10.14.10). Yama is called pitrmat. i.e., a companion of the Fathers (RV, 10.14.4). At the mahapitryajna Yama and the Fathers are invited conjointly to accept the oblations (RV, 10.15.8). It was Yama who after himself dying first found out a new path for the dead souls to follow (RV, 10.14.2). Yama is the lord of the Fathers (AV, 18.2.25; 5.24.14). The SB, 13.4.3.6 states that Yama is the king and the Fathers are his people. At the sacrifices the Fathers are also invited and honoured. Like Yama they too are offered oblations with the call of svadha(RV, 10.15.14, AV, 18.2.49; ḌÂ. 3.3.6.4).

**Abode of the Fathers**

The Fathers reside in the midst of heaven (RV, 10.15.14) and their place is in the highest heaven (RV, 10.14.8) which is also the abode of their lord Yama (VS, 19.45, ḌÂ, 2.6.3.4, SB, 18.1.18-19, JaiUp, 1.12.2). Their region is situated in the third heaven which is nearer to the sun. According to the AV, 18.2.48 the Fathers live in the third heaven.

According to PB, 9.8.5, ḌÂ, 1.3.10.5 (trfiye ua itah lake pitarah) also the Fathers live in the third world from here. The author of the GB, 1.2.4 has them placed in the svarga-loka (heaven). As noted earlier it is not a matter of fact that the deceased went to the abode of the Fathers, because the AV, 12.2.45 implores Agni to let those who are dead reach to the region of the manes. The funeral rites are performed In order to enable the deceased to reach this region.Being the subject of Yama, the Fathers are also connected with Yama’s direction, i.e., the south direction (MS, 4.9.5). The SB, 3.1.1.7 states that south direction is the direction of the Fathers (ya dakstria dik sa pitmam, SB, 3.1.1.7).

The SB, 13.8.1.5 tells that the door of the pitr-loka is in the south-east, while that of heaven is said to be in the north-east (SB, 6.6.2.4). Death is the dividing line between the Fathers and men and so they do not mix together. They lead separate lives. Fathers are invited to the sacrifices, where they receive great honour. Like Indra and other goods they came to the sacrifice in chariots (RV, 10.15.10). They are often spoken of as somyasah, i.e., Soma-loving (RV, 10.15.1; 5; 8) and are fondly Invited at the time of drinking Soma, and to witness how Immortal (Soma) has entered the mortals (men). At the sacrifice the Fathers sit to the right side of the altar. A.A. Macdonell, observes that the Fathers sits to the right of the altar. I.e., In the south direction because the south Is the region of Yama and the Fathers.They sit in a peculiar way with their knees on the earth.They are told by Prajapati to keep their sacred thread on the right shoulder and bend the left knee while sitting. When they asked Prajapati the manner in which they were expected to live, he told them they would eat the food offered every month with the accomplishment of svadha. He also told the Fathers that the moon shall be their light.

Food is offered to the Fathers at least once a month. It is believed that in the sraddha-paksa, i.e., the dark half of the month Asvina they come to the houses of their kin to seek food.

**Attitude Towards the Living**

The attitude of the Fathers towards the living beings is that of friendship. They do not possess a hostile nature and so prayers to them are not due to any fear.

People look upon their ancestors with affection and esteem. Even after death they are considered a part of the family. They are supposed to come to a wedding in the family to see and bless the bride (AV, 14.2.73), like the living elders do. It emphasises the interest the dead are supposed to take in the affairs of the living and the sense of continuity that was felt to bind the living with the dead.The Fathers are said to possess power to Influence the life of their descendents on earth for good or evil. Hence there are passionate appeals to the Fathers to help their sons and grandsons, etc. on earth and not to Injure them In any way. In the AV. 18.4.82, 83 salutations are offered to the wrath of the Fathers (nomah vah pltaro manyave) as well as to their dreadful and cruel deeds. It is Intended here that the anger of Fathers would not be directed to the worshipper.

**Prayers to the Fathers**

The Fathers who are kind, gentle and righteous, beneflcient and harmless are prayed for success, for victory over enemies, for the gift of rains and abundance of food. They are prayed to show their goodwill, to be gracious towards men, to bless them with health and strength and protect them against dangers (RV, 1.106.3, 6.52.4, 75.9, 7.35.17; AV, 2.12.4, 18.1.51; 2.11, 30; 3.14, 45,48, 58). They are prayed for the gift of rains and abundance of food. They are requested to protect the sacrificers (RV, 10.15.1:5). They are propitiated to avert their anger for any sin the descendents might commit through human weakenesses (RV, 10.15.6) and not to injure (punish) them for the faults which they, being (erring) humans, have done.

The Fathers wish to continue their line of progeny and they endow the human beings with the power of procreation (RV, 10.56.60). In this they have a special interest, as the continuation of the rites to the Fathers can only be kept up by continuing the family line. In the SB, 13.8.1.6, the Fathers are said to keep their contact alive with this world through the offsprings.

The Fathers are also connected with light. They are said to pervade the whole world and illumine it (RV, 10.56.5).They adorned the heaven with the constellations (RV, 10.68. 11).The RV, 3.38.3 states that the Fathers measured the two world with measuring rods and made them broad. They found out the hidden Agni and brought forth the dawn (RV, 7.76.4). In battles they are invoked as warriors (RV, 6.75.9).

In the VS, 2.33 the Fathers are worshipped to secure the prosperity and children. They are requested to grant (handsome) sons like Asvins who put on lotus garlands'. In the VS, 19.58 the Fathers are requested to come to the sacrifice and enjoy the offerings. They are also requested to pronounce blessings (adhi bruvantu) and extend their protection on their descendents. They are prayed to make the sacrificers lords of wealth (vayam syama patayo rayinam).

In the AV the Fathers are prayed to save sacrificers from sins (AV. 11.6.11). They are propitiated to bestow riches, sons and longevity to their progeny (AV, 18.3.14; 4.62). Yama along with the Fathers protects the vessel of the food cooked for sacrificial purpose (AV, 12.3.8). They bestow a happy and long life to people (AV, 12.3.8).

**Different Classes of the Fathers**

Three grades of the Fathers are mentioned In the RV, 10.15.1 as lower (auara), medium (madhyamah) and high (parasah). Sayana explains that those who have performed sacrifices according to Sruti are uttama, those who have performed according to Smrti are madhyama and those devoid of any sarnskaras are adhama. In the AV, 18.4.80, 81. 82 svadha oblations are offered to the Fathers who are sitting (1) On the earth (prthivi-sadbhyah). (2) On the mid-region (antariksa-sadbhyah) and in the heaven (divisadbhyah). This suggests that the Fathers had access to all the three worlds. They are classified as ancient (purvaih) and recent (paraih) (AV, 18.3.48, also 72).

The RV, 10.15.4 mentions two classes of Fathers. They are; (1) those who are cremated, and (2) those who are not cremated. In the VS, 19.60 this stanza appears with only a little difference. Here the term agnidagdhah and anagnidagdah replaced by agnisvattah and anagnisvattah. This shows that the meaning of agnisvatta in the following way — yan agnir eva dahan svadayati te pitaro agnisvattah. It tells that the Fathers who are tasted by Agni at the time of cremation are agnisvattas. Others being those who are not cremated. These modes of disposal of the dead are referred to in the RV as well as the A V. In the RV. 10.18.11,12 the dead is told to go to the mother earth which Is to enclose him, as a mother covers her child with a garment. Thus It Is a clear reference to the burial of the dead. These words are also applied to another rite by which the bones of the cremated, collected in an um, are deposited in earth, and hence Oldenberg holds the view that these verses are in connected with the burial of such as urn.

According to A.B. Keith who disagrees with Oldenberg, both these modes of the disposal of the dead. i.e.. cremation and burial were practised in the Rgvedic age as two alternative methods. Later when burial was given up for cremation these verses were used for the burial of the bones, “but the fact remains that the verses are burial verses, that the RV treats burial and burning on the same footing."

Besides cremation the other modes for the disposal of the dead are mentioned in the AV, 18.2.34. The dead might be buried (nikhatah), they might be cast away (paroptati) or they might be exposed (uddhitah). Those cast away may refer to a practise, as in Iran, of throwing the dead body to the beasts to devouruddhitah seems to mean exposed on trees, which is referred to in SB, 4.5.2.13 for the expiation of a particular sin.

Since modes of the disposal of the dead other than cremation were also practised, anagnisvatta is used to denote such Fathers who were not cremated. It may also include those whose cremation could not be done due to certain reason like loss of their dead bodies as in some cases of drowning.

In the VS, 19.58 agnisvatta Fathers are invited to come to the sacrifice by devayana, enjoy the oblations offered to them with the utterance of svadha.

Fathers come to sacrifice and sit on the strewn grass (barhis) spread out for them, so they are called barhisadah. They are prayed to come and accept the svadha oblations offered to them (RV, 10.15.3; VS, 19.56, AV, 18.1.45).

Angiras is a class of the Fathers which is frequently mentioned in the hymns connected with Yama. They are mentioned as special companions of Yama. Yama is said to prosper with the Angiras (RV, 10.14.3; AV, 18.1.4, with some variation). Yama is invited to come with Angiras and sit on the asanas spread out for them.

**Two Paths**

The concept of the two paths, i.e., the devayana and the pitryana, mentioned in the later Vedic literature is not very clear in the family-books of the RV. The RV, 1.109.7 however records that, “by the sun's rays, the Father attained a heavenly region". It is sufficient to suggest that this path is the devayana which passes through the sun and leads to the total emancipation. The RV, 10.18.1 is a prayer to Mrtyu. This stanza mentions devayana and indirectly tells about another path which is different from the devayana (itaro devayanat) and which is here said to be the path of Mrtyu (i.e., that of repeated births and deaths!).

The RV. 10.88.15 (also VS. 19.47; SB. 12.8.1.21) tells about the two paths for the mortals — one is that of the gods and the other one is that of the Fathers. The path by which the Fathers come to sacrifices is called the pitryana, whereas the other path by which the gods come to sacrifices is called the devayana. Sayana while commenting on this rc quotes the Bhagavadgita, 8.24-26 to explain the two paths; one leading to the liberation and the other to the recurrent births.

After his death everybody passes through either of these paths. The SB, 12.8.1.21 states that by means of these two paths the Fathers go to the world of heaven.

In the MS. 1.3.37 it is said that the deceased has gone by the path of Rta through the moon to the gods. It also states that moon is the eye of the Fathers, which lights up their path. Thus it is clear that the path which takes the mortals to the gods through moon is devayana.

The RV does not mention the recurrent births. Though the germs of the doctrine of transmigration can be seen even in RV, the doctrine of rebirth was not yet formulated.

Gradually the pitryana began to be considered the path of rebirth while devayana that of the total liberation. (SB, 10.4.3.1-10).

The Upanisads mention two paths open to the mortals after their death:

One of them Is the bright path, i.e., devayana and the other is the dark path, i.e., pitryana. They are also conceived as arci-marga, and dhuma-marga. Those who perform charitable deeds in their lives follow the pitiyana after their death, while those who lead a life of piety and austerity enter the path of the gods (ChaUp, V. 10.1-2). The souls taking up the devayana are said to reach the region of Brahman, (ChaUp, V. 10.1; BrUp, 6.2.15) while the pitiyana leads to rebirth (ChaUp, 5.10.3-6).

The ChaUp, (V. 10.1-6) provides in full details the intermittent stages of both the paths. According to it he who follows the pitiyana enters first into smoke, then into night, the dark half of the month, six months of the year when the sun is in the southern course, the sky and finally it reaches the moon. He remains there till the remnant of his good deeds remain. After that he descends through ether, wind, smoke, mist, cloud, rain, herbs, grains and seeds. Those who eat the food and procreate, become manifold. Through the assimilation of the food by men he enters a womb and is born again.

Those souls, who go by devayana enter successively into flame, day, bright half of month, six months of the year when the sun in its northern course, sun, moon, lightening, and finally into Brahman never to return (ChaUp, 4.15.5-6).

It is clear here that though all the dead go to moon, those coming by pitiyana stay there and after receiving the fruit of their good deeds return to earth to be born again. But it is different for those coming by devayana. For them the moon is a transit station.

According to KauBr all those who depart from this world go, without exception, to the moon. For reaching up to moon no separate paths like devayana or pitiyana are mentioned. From moon they (according to their knowledge and action) go either by devayana which leads to Brahman, or (the name pitryana is not used) they enter upon a new birth; whether as worm or a fly, or a fish, or a bird, or a lion, or a boar, or serpent, or a tiger, or a man, or something else.

Thus it is clear that according to the KauUp the dead do not take the devayana or pitryana right from the earth. All the dead go to the moon first and from there take up devayana to reach Brahman. Others come down to earth in a renewed birth.

KauUp, 1.4 presents a development in the concept of devayana.It does away with the relays of the devayana which are recognised in the ChaUp, and substitutes new ones. It tells that when the soul comes to the path of the gods he first goes to the world of Fire, then successively to that of wind, Varuna, Sun, Indra and Prajapati and then finally to the world of Brahman.

From the above discussion it is evident that while the word devayana is categorically mentioned in the RV, the other path which is the path of the Fathers is hinted by itaro devayanat. It is in the later Vedic literature that the idea of these two paths was crystellized.

## 3. Yama and Other Gods

On the basis of the statistical classification of the gods (suggested by A.A. Macdonnell), Yama cannot be regarded a major god since he belongs to the fifth category of the gods.In the ṚV only three hymns are addressed to him (viz. 10.10; 14; 135) and his name appears about fifty times, mostly in the 10th Maṇḍala. Yama appears in the other Maṇḍalas too e.g., 1.38.5; 85.5; 7.33.9; 9.113.8.2These stray and scattered reference are also important for getting a glimpse of the true character of this god.

Yama is seen to be closely associated with other gods. He is mentioned as reveling in the company of other gods (ṚV, 7.76.4; 10.135.1). He is also mentioned with individual gods like Varuṇa (ṚV, 10.14.7) and Bṛhaspati (ṚV, 10.13.4; 14.3). As a conductor of the dead Agni has a close relation with Yama. Agni, Yama and Mātariśvan are mentioned together as different names of the Supreme-being (ṚV, 1.164.46).

**Yama and Varuṇa**

Among all the Vedic gods Varuṇa seems to be closest to Yama. Due to similarities between them in more than one respect. Varuṇa is also considered to be a duplicate of Yama.

Like Yama, Varuṇa is also called a king (ṚV, 1.24.7, 7.87.5).

He is the lord of all beings (ṚV, 2.27.10) and of all the universe (ṚV, 5.85.3). Varuṇa and Yama are mentioned as two kings, who can be seen in the realm of the dead. A man who has recently died, is told to hasten to the other world, where he would see both, i.e., Yama and Varuṇa (ṚV, 10.14.7; AV, 18.34.6). In the ṚV, 10.14.7, Varuṇa along with Yama is mentioned as enjoying the svadhā oblations which are otherwise exclusively for Yama and the Fathers.

Varuṇa is frequently called asura, i.e., one who possesses the largest amount of asu (the magical power-substance) and therefore, has the greatest power.He is the magician- creator of the world as well as its 'Supreme Sovereign'.

Varuṇa who is the upholder of the cosmic law (i.e., ṛta) is also by logical extension a moral governor who employs his noose and puts the evil-doers in bondage by way of punishment (ṚV, 7.84.2,AV, 4.166.9). The TS, 2.3.13 says that Varuṇa binds with his noose a person who is involved with evil deeds.Similar is the purport of the TB, 1.7.2.6 which says that Varuṇa grabs a person doing unlawful deeds.Obviously the noose of Varuṇa, the supreme binder-god, performs a dual function. Varuṇa binds down all the phenomena of the universe with his noose and thereby controls the universe, making it a well-regulated whole.On the other hand, he also uses his noose to punish the sinner. Thus Varuṇa controls with his noose the cosmic as well as the moral order. Varuṇa also watches the sacrificial rites and punishes people for the inaccuracies therein, if any, with his knots and cords which are repeatedly mentioned in the Brahmanas.

According to the ṚV, 1.122.9 Mitra and Varuṇa inflict disease of the heart on a man who does not offer oblations to them.The ṚV, 1.122.9 says

A man who does you wrong, Mitra and Varuṇa! Who injures you in any way or does not present you with oblations, contracts for himself sickness in his heart.

According to H.H. Wilson, yakṣmā is a disease in general. V.S. Apt understands it as a pulmonary disease and yaksma-graha as “attack of consumption”.

Can be inferred that diseases are the pāśa of Varuṇa. H.H. Wilson observes that a kind of dropsy seems to be referred to as Varuṇa’s noose in the AV, 4.16.7. The ṚV, 7.89.2 refers to this disease(i.e., dropsy) as inflicted by Varuṇa. This disease (also mentioned in the AB, 7.15) is supposed to cause water-retention in body resulting in enlargement of the belly and the condition of a man sick with dropsy is vividly expressed here in the following way:

I move along tremulous like a wind-blown skin.

According to a story in the AB, 7.13-18 Varuṇa is said to have punished the king Hariścandra for the breach of his promise by making him sick with dropsy. There is no deed, good or bad, of which Varuṇa is ignorant. The all-seeing sun who is the eye of Varuṇa (ṚV, 1.115.1;6.51.1)by which he sees the deeds of a man as well as his truthfulness and falsehood. The sun is mentioned as the 'golden-winged messenger of Varuṇa’ (ṚV, 10.123.6).In the ṚV, 7.34.10 Varuṇa is mentioned as thousand-eyed; he watches everything that is secret, every deed done or to be done (ṚV, 1.25.11)the worshippers pray Varuṇa and seek deliverance from his noose (ṚV, 1.25.21;6.74.4).

Varuṇa’s function as a punisher brings him close to Yama. Though Yama is not explicitly mentioned as a punisher or as a judge in the ṚV, but a parallel to Varuṇa’s noose can be seen in Yama’s foot-fetter (padbīśa). The worshipper prays to be freed from the pāśa and padbīśa of Varuṇa and Yama respectively (ṚV, 10.97.16). In the ŚB Yama is clearly mentioned as a punisher.

Varuṇa gets the epithets satya dharman and dharmapati. (TS, 1.8.10.1: TB, 1.7.10.3). In this connection it may be noted that Yama holds the epithet of dharmarāja in the Epics.

The VS, 24.28 mentions buffalo as the animal to be sacrificed to Varuṇa.In the Epics Yama has a buffalo as his mount. Varuṇa’s emissary, a bird with golden wings, is said to be residing in the region of Yama.The AV, 1.14 is addressed to “Varuṇa, or Yama” (Varuṇo Yamo vā), thus equating or at least connecting Varuṇa with Yama.

In the AV. 6.46.1 the dream or the evil-dream is called Yama's son by Varuṇāni. i.e., Varuṇa’s consort. It is significant that Yami is not stated to be the mother of Yama’s son. This is one of the few references which indicate that Yami was not originally considered to be Yama’s wife, and for that matter no wife of Yama is mentioned in the ṚV. Yami is mentioned in the ṚV as Yama’s twin-sister but she is not mentioned as Yama's wife.

At another place a bad dream is called a son of Grāhi and also of Nirṛti, Abhūti, Nirbhūti, Parabhūti and Deva-jāmi in the subsequent lines AV 16.5.

Another connection of Yama and Varuṇa is said to be through Nirṛti.Nirṛti is conceived as a malevolent goddess, who brings diseases and destruction. Varuṇa is the lord of ṛta or the cosmic order and Nirṛti, who is the negation of ṛti (which is probably another form of ṛta)is associated with death and destruction and in this capacity she is considered to be the female counterpart of Yama. At a few places Yama is identified with death (ṚV. 10.165.4,AV, 6.28.3: 93.1).In the MS, 2.5.6 it is said that Mṛtyu indeed is Yama (mṛtur vai Yamaḥ — MS, 2.5.6), while Kauśikasūtra, 8.23 states that Yama is mṛtyu. It is important to note that Varuṇa is also identified with death. The KS, 13.2 says that, while practicing witchcraft, one should sacrifice a black ram, which belongs to Yama, because ’Varuṇa is death’.

Varuṇa is also said to be the earlier name of Prajāpati, the lord of (pro-) creation.Prajāpati is identified the Primeval Man (Puruṣa) who was offered in the first sacrifice and whose dismembered limbs became this universe.

Varuṇa is the greatest asura because he possesses the largest amount of asu or magical power-substance. With his magical powers, he creates the cosmos. He is the magical creator of the world as well as its Supreme Sovereign.Thus it is seen that Yama and Varuṇa both share an important attribute of being the creator of the Universe. The ṚV, 10.13.4 gives a vague account of the sacrifice of a divine being for the cause of progeny, in which it is said that Yama sacrificed his body for the cause of progeny. This stanza provides a rudimentary and obscure account of the sacrifice of a Cosmic Man which fined a detailed expression in the Puruṣasūkta. On this basis, an equation can be formed, i.e., Varuṇa = Prajāpati = Puruṣa = Yama, which equates Varuṇa with Yama in the capacity of a creator, but it is difficult to establish an identity between Yama and Varuṇa. Varuṇa is sometimes represented as being identical with the other Vedic deities, but Varuṇa is not identical with any of them. Actually he 'includes and transcends them all’.

**Yama and Agni**

Agni is called 'a dear friend Yama' priyo yamasya kāmyo (ṚV,10.21.5). Yama's connection with Agni is mainly through the funeral rites as well as through the sacrifices to the Fathers. These are three forms of Agni as mentioned in the ṚV. They are (1) Kravyād (2) Kravyavāhana and (3) Havyavāhana.

**Kravyād Agni**

This form of fire is used for the cremation of the deceased. It consumes the corpse and takes the deceased to the other world, i.e., the world of the Fathers (ṚV, 10.16.1-4) which is according to AV, 18.3.71 the world of the righteous people. Hence this corpse-burning fire is called kravyād, i.e., consumer of flesh. Yama is connected with kravyād through its function of consuming the dead body and also of taking the dead to the world of the Fathers which is the realm of Yama. In the AV, 12.2.54, kravyād Agni is called Yama's fire who belongs to the south direction which is considered to be Yama’s direction.

After the cremation, this fire is left at a cross-road in the south direction because it is not used for any other purpose e.g., sacrifices to the gods or to the Fathers.

For obvious reason, the kravyād fire is considered inauspicious and unfit for sacrificial purpose. It is also strongly wished that this fire should not come again (i.e., no death should occur in the family). Therefore after the cremation is done, this fire is sent away and another fire is procured for performing the rest of the rites, i.e., the sacrifices to the Fathers.Compare here “I send away the flesh-devouring Agni to a distance: let him, bearing away sins, repair to the realm of king Yama; but let this other Agni \[itaro jātavedāḥ) convey the oblation to gods \[ṚV, 10.16.9).The next stanza states that before performing the worship of the manes, the kravyād Agni is turned out (ṚV, 10.16.10).The Sūtras also Instruct that kravyād Agni is to be discarded after the cremation. With the recitation of the ṚV, 10.16.9 the fire is taken to the south and discarded at a crossing there. Another fire is prepared from the north direction, for performing the śanti-karman (post-cremation rites) and also the sacrifices to the Fathers (ĀŚS,2.219).

**Kravyavāhana**

In the ṚV the fire carrying oblations to the manes is called kravyavāhana (10.16.11) or the carrier of kravya. Commenting on this stanza Sāyaṇa takes kravya as kavya. Kavyas are the offerings made to the Fathers (Sāyaṇa on the ṚV, 10.15.9) therefore kravyavāhana is explained as kavyasya voḍha agniḥ.Udgitha, a predecessor of Sāyaṇa (whose commentary on the ṚV, 10.15.9 is available) retains the word kravya and explains the word kravyavāhana as kravyāsya voḍha agniḥ.

Basically kravya means flesh. So kravyavāhana is a 'fire carrying the flesh-oblations’.S.D. Satvalekar points at the offering of flesh to the Fathers in the VS, 35.20.

In the post-Ṛgvedic literature the term kavyavāhana instead of kravyavāhana is extensively used for the “fire carrying oblations to the Fathers".According to Hillebrandt the word kavyavāhana is a new form of the older word kravyavāhana caused "probably by a real change in sound or formed for euphemistic reasons".Hillebrandt refers to the views of M. Bloomfield (Festgabe fur, Streitberg, p. 13) explaining the word to be “a product of contamination of kavyavāhana with much more common Havyavāhana."A.B. Keith, however believes that kravyād, kravyavāhana and kavyavāhana are different names of one form of Agni. He further comments that the term kravyād alternates with kravyavāhana and later with kavyavāhana.

It should be noted here that the term Jātavedas is used as a general name of Agni and that it is used at different places as a name of any of the three forms of Agni. In the ṚV, 10.16.9 the priest says, “I drive away this kravyād Agni to a distant place. Let this other fire (Itaro Jātavedāḥ) carry oblations to the gods." Verily, here Jātavedas stands for the havyavāhana Agni, while in an earlier stanza (ṚV, 10.16.1) Jātavedas stands for the kravyād Agni, where he is told to burn the corpse properly.

In the AV also the term Jātavedas is used to denote various forms of Agni. For example, in the AV, 18.3.42 Jātavedas stands for the kavyavāhana Agni while in the AV, 18.3.71 it denotes the kravyād Agni.

The VS, 1.17 distinguishes the three forms of Agni in the following way:

- (1) Āmāt
- (2) Kravyād — corpse-devouring fire
- (3) Devayāja — sacrificial fire

Āmāt—In the vocable, āmāt, āma means "raw, uncooked". According to A.B. Keith āmāt means the 'eater of raw flesh'.Since the Agni who consumes flesh (corpse) is spoken of as kravyād here, it is unlikely that āmāt also refers to the flesh- devouring Agni. A plausible meaning of āmāt can be the fire who consumes the rawness of food (by cooking it). Thus āmāt can be the kitchen fire used for cooking the food. Uvvaṭa and Mahidhara seem to be referring to this meaning of āmāt in their commentary of this stanza (i.e., VS, 1.17).

In TS, VI. 5.9.1 Agni tells Indra imperatively “thou shalt not offer what is raw (āmaṁ) in me".A consumer of rawness' can, therefore, be a figurative expression for the fire that cooks.

But according to the MS Agni has two forms.He carries oblations to gods by one and to the Fathers by the other. Here no separate Agni as the consumer of the corpse is mentioned. It seems that the functions of both these fires have got assimilated here. According to ĀŚS the kravyād Is left after the cremation and another fire should be prepared for further rites (i.e., pitṛmedha). The fire carrying the oblations to Fathers must be other than the eater of corpses which Is left aside after the cremation. This confirms that kravyād and kavyavāhana are two separate forms of Agni performing two separate functions.

The TS, 2.5.8.6 mentions the three forms of Agni (1) Havyavāhana, (2) Kavyavāhana and (3) Saharākṣas. According to it havyavāhana belongs to the gods, the kavyavāhana belongs to the Fathers and the saharākṣas is associated with goblins and belongs to the asuras.

The TS, 3.3.8 tells that Yama Is Agni and the fire-altar is Yami.According to the TS, 1.8.5 one can attain Yama's kingdom by performing the Agniṣṭoma sacrifice.

**Yama and Havyavāhana**

The ṚV, 10.51 contains a legend connected with the havyavāhana Agni, Yama and other gods. The story is about the disappearance of Agni who concealed himself in water. Eventually all oblations to the gods came to a stand-still. It was Yama who found out the hidden Agni. The gods Implored Agni to come from his hiding place. They told Agni “We all looked for you and it was Yama who recognized you blazing from your ten hiding places”. (ṚV, 10.51.3) These ten hiding places are, according to Sāyaṇa, the three worlds: (1) heaven, (2) mid-region and (3) earth; three divinities: (4) Agni, (5) Vayu and (6) Āditya; (7) water, (8) shrubs, (9) trees and (10) bodies of living beings.R.T.H. Griffith observes that the meaning of ten hiding places appears to be that Yama knew that Agni would appear again from the fire-sticks worked out by fingers of both the hands.Gods requested Agni to come out and become the conveyer of the oblations offered to them. Agni expressed his fear and said that his elder brothers were also engaged in this work of oblation bearing and they were killed while performing their duty. On seeing his brothers’ fate, Agni, trembling with fear, had concealed himself (ṚV,10.51.6). Sāyaṇa comments that the three brothers of Agni, viz. Bhūpati, Bhuvanapati and Bhūtānāmpati were killed while performing their duty as oblation-bearers of the gods.

The gods assure Agni of a fair deal. They grant him Immortality (ṚV, 10.51.7) and also a portion in the oblations (ṚV, 10.51.8).

W.E. Webster remarks that the legend contained in this hymn was probably invented to account for the triumph of Agni 'the youngest of the gods' over the older deities Mitra and Varuṇa.

This story also occurs in the TS, 2.6.6 and the TB, 7.13. The TS-version tells that three elder brothers of Agni died while carrying oblations to the gods. Afraid of meeting the same fate he escaped and entered the waters. When the gods looking for Agni reached there, the fish told them about his hiding place. At this, Agni cursed the fish that they would be killed by men. The gods requested Agni to come out and become their oblation-bearer. Agni finally agreed when he got the assurance that the offerings which fall outside the altar shall go to his brothers (TS, 2.6.6).

Another version, with some variation, is found in the TB, 7.13. According to it when Agni fled away from gods, he assumed the form of a mole and entered the earth. The story of Agni’s flight also appears in TS, 6.2.8; Bṛhaddevatā 7.6.1.77 and ŚB, 1.2.3.1.

Apart from being called a dear friend of Yama (ṚV, 10.21.5) Agni along with Yama is adorned with praises (ṚV, 10.64.3). The AV, 3.31 is addressed to pāpmahā Agni and here Agni is prayed to unbind the cords of death from which a brahmacārin seeks to be released.

At some places the word Yama is used as a name for Agni (ṚV, 1.66.8). Sāyaṇa also points out that here Agni is called Yama.Here the word Yama which also means ‘a twin’ is used for Agni, because he and Indra are said to be twins. Agni Is also called Yama from yam ɱ yaccha = to give, because he grants the worshipper all his cherished desires.

The ŚB, 7.1.1.1 even establishes an identity between Yama and Agni and where it is said that king Yama is the same as the Gārhapatya fire. The place of the Gārhapatya fire is swept with a palāśa-branch and palāśa is believed to be an emblem of Yama.The ŚB, 7.2.1.10 also says that Yama indeed is Agni and according to the JB Agni is death (JB 1.332: III. 348).

**Yama and Soma**

The Fathers are said to belong to Soma and hence Soma comes to be indirectly connected with Yama, the lord of the Fathers. In the ṚV, 8.48.15 where Soma is prayed, he is said to be in the company of the Fathers.In the ŚB, II.6.1.4 the Fathers are repeatedly called Somavantaḥ Here Soma is called pitṛmat. The TB. 2.6.16.2 also connects Soma with the Fathers. According to ŚB, 2.4.2.12 Soma is the god of the Fathers. The Fathers are also called somyāsaḥ, i.e., Soma- loving or connected with Soma(ṚV, 10.14.6: 15.1; AV,2.12.5) .

In the ṚV, 10.57.3-5 Soma-nārāśaṁsa and the Fathers are mentioned together in connection with the sacrifice to the Fathers. According to Sāyaṇa the nārāśaṁsa Soma is the Soma connected with the Fathers.Soma is always invoked in the rituals for the Fathers and he is especially the deity at the pitṛmedha (VS, III.56).At the offering of the piṇḍa- piṛtyajña, Soma Is invoked as kavyavāhana.It is to be noted here that the kavyavāhana a form of Agni, especially connected with the Fathers as their oblation-bearer.

The MS, 1.10.18 calls Soma as the god of the Fathers. Here, he is also called pitṛmat.It is said here that the offering made to Soma are considered as offered to the Fathers because Soma indeed is the god of the Fathers. In the VS, 2.29 also Soma is called pitṛmat. Thus Soma, who is often spoken of as god of the Fathers, and Yama who is the lord of the Fathers pitṛmam adhipatiḥ — (AV, 5.24.14) are connected with each other through the Fathers. Though it is difficult to see an identification between Soma and Yama,there is a distinct relationship between Soma, the god of the Father and Yama, the lord of the Fathers.

Soma, conjointly with Yama, Varuṇa, Aśvins and others is prayed to save the worshipper from death (mṛtyu) (AV, 19.20.1). Another verse says that one, who prepares viṣṭārin brahmodana for the priest, lives happily with Yama in the company of the Gandharvas, who are connected with Soma (AV, 4.34.3). Soma is believed to be the extract of the Soma-plantmixed with milk or honey, which was offered to the gods. The sacrificer too partook this sacrificial drink after offering it to the gods, Soma was deemed as the best offering to the gods (uttamaṁ haviḥ, ṚV, 9.86.10).

It is quite clear from the Vedic reference that Soma is primarily a sacred ritual drink. Gradually it became so closely associated with the moon to have become another name for Candramas (moon).

The ṚV, 9.41.5 says that Soma fills the earth and sky with its rays.In the AV, 11.6.7 the identity of Soma with candramas (moon) is established.The ŚB, 2.4.2.7 states that this indeed is Soma who is called Candramas, the food of the gods.It is clear that the moon came to be regarded as the drink of the gods. It diminishes in size, when it is being consumed and then gradually regains its original size after a given time.

According to Hillebrandt Soma in the ṚV always stands for Candramasand also that these five gods viz. Varuṇa, Āpāṁnapāt, Bṛhaspati, Yama and Soma are closely connected with Candramas. This view is, however, strongly criticized by many other scholarsR.N. Dandekar observes that Varuṇa ’s apparent Identity with Soma which constitutes one of the main planks in Hillebrandt’s hypothesis can be more adequately explained by assuming that it was perhaps a conscious attempt on the part of later Soma-priests to glorify Soma by bringing him in contact with Varuṇa, the world-sovereign.Soma is also identified with Viṣṇu, Śiva, Kuber, etc.R.N. Dandekar asserts that Soma is primarily the sacred ritual drink which fact would be quite clear from the Vedic references. The identification of Soma with the moon can only be a secondary character and may be regarded as a product of poetical fancy.Thus, according to R.N. Dandekar Hillebrandt's assertion that Soma is essentially identical with the moon (HVM, 1.282 ff.) is quite unconvincing.

Yama’s direct association with Soma (as candramas) is seen in JB, 1.246 where Yama is said to be residing in the heart of the moon. Also, the JB, 1.28 equates the mythological hare in the moon with Yama — “This indeed is a hare in the moon.... This indeed is Yama that is seen in the moon. He indeed controls everything" (JB, 1.28).

**Yama and Nirṛti**

A consistent account of Nirṛti's personality and her bearings is found in the rv down to the Brāhmaṇas.
