She is depicted as a goddess having malevolent qualities and hence is regarded with fear and apprehension. It is prayed that Nirṛti should not harm the worshipper or his kith and kin. The ṚV, 10.59 is addressed to Nirṛti imploring her to leave the sacrifice alone and depart from the place of the sacrifice so that no harm might come. The refrain of the hymn is a prayer to Nirṛti asking her to leave the sacrifice and go to distant places.

According to Sāyaṇa Nirṛti is pāpa-devatā, i.e., an evil-goddessand rakṣojāti-devatā,Sāyaṇa has also mentioned her as Mṛtyu-devatā, the goddess of death.The ṚV, 10.16.1 (AV, 3.1.1) is used to bring a patient back from Nirṛti’s lap. If one is said to be sleeping in Nirṛti’s lap it is meant that he is dead (ṚV, 10.95.14). This shows that she is connected with diseases and death. In the ṚV, 1.38.6 Nirṛti’s name is accompanied by the word durhaṇā, which is a substantive for harm or mischief,and also by durvidātra,meaning ill-disposed; envious. J. Gonda comments that there is a tendency in the ṚV that generally no epithets are given to the words for unfavorable happenings, formidable beings, terrible concepts and subjects; in short, all manifestations of evil are generally speaking left without epithetical attributes. It was considered wise, J. Gondabelieves, to conceal the attributes of their qualities and properties, not to ascribe any honor to them and not to add to their powers and influence by using epithets which could be regarded as praise. But one of the exceptions to this tendency is Nirṛti, the goddess of disorder and destruction who is styled as durvidātra (ṚV, 10.36.2) which is used to typify the inimical behavior of the injurious or malicious (arātim durvidātram—ṚV, 10.63.12) and a wicked person (manyumdurvidatrasya—ṚV, 10.35.4).

Nirṛti is also portrayed as terrible (VS, 12.64) and wicked (ŚB, 7.2.1.3). She is described as black, horrible, holding a noose (TS, 4.2.5.3) — an iron-noose (VS, 12.63) and also sharp weapons.She is connected with black color. The TB, 1.6.1.4 states that Nirṛti’s garments are black and black chaff is her sacrificial fee.

Nirṛti is described as ever-increasing in might (ṚV,1,38.6). She is a great and all-pervading goddess having golden hair (AV, 5.7.9) and wearing dark clothes (TB, 1.6.1.4). With her connection with black color it is natural that the factors of chaos and disorderliness are attributed to Nirṛti. When the Vedic Aryans realized that the universe was governed by some definite law and order, owing to which it was a cosmos and not a chaos, this realization gave birth to the concept of ṛta and Varuṇa was conceived as the upholder of ṛta.

Later on while applying this cosmic law to a limited sphere, i.e., the world, it was observed that some happenings seem to be breaking the cosmic law and Oder (ṛta). These happenings of un-orderliness are seen in the form of diseases and disasters. Death (i.e., untimely death), difficulties, poverty, barrenness are regarded as abnormalconditions. On the analogy of Varuṇa, who is the upholder of ṛta, Nirṛti was conceived as personifying such un-orderliness or aberration of order.Thus Nirṛti can be regarded as a linguistic feminine negative counterpart of ṛta.

Fatah Singh obseRVes that Nirṛti obviously appears to be the negation of ṛtiḥ which is probably another form of ṛtam and it would mean cessation or negation of ṛta.But S.K. Lai finds no connection between ṛta and Nirṛti. According to him “Nirṛti has no connection with Ṛta, not even negatively or as its opposite element. It appears that the concept of Ṛta and Nirṛti developed independently”.

Nirṛti is also connected with sudden death. The AB, 4.10 states "the hotṛ wrests from the hands of Nirṛti her cords and puts them down while repeating the Dvipadā verse". The verse says: “May she who is the ruler of all that is born and moves not be angry (with us), not visit us (with destruction)." He does so for attaining his full age. He, who has such a knowledge, attains his full age. This passage shows that Nirṛti’s displeasure can cause destruction as well as untimely death.

If the oblation meant for any god gets burnt, it would show that Nirṛti has taken hold of that sacrifice. She takes over the offerings made in that sacrifice. If, therefore, the offering gets burnt, one should suspend the sacrifice and again prepare the offerings for the original deity. The burnt portion of the oblation should be given away, by way of dakṣiṇā, to the sacrificer’s enemy; thereby Nirṛti seizes the enemy.

Moreover, Nirṛti is said to take possession of a wife, who has no sons (ŚB, 5.3.1.13). She also seizes upon that part of soil in which no plants are produced. This shows that Nirṛti is connected with the barrenness of land as well as that of women.

According to another view Nirṛti is not the presiding deity of lawlessness, chaos or disorder. She is ‘the evil spirit that grapples men when they violate Ṛta. She is nowhere associated with Varuṇa even as a negation of Rta”.

̮̮

But Nirṛti cannot be taken as only a punisher, because a punisher Inflicts punishment when the person has committed some crime, while Nirṛti is said to be causing harm even otherwise as she brings death, destruction and calamity. Moreover, Nirṛti cannot be regarded as a punisher only, because in that case neither her dark bearings nor the repeated prayers of the sacrificer’s imploring her to depart to the distant places can be explained

Nirṛti is also seen as a goddess of the earth. According to Fatah Singh “Nirṛti derived from ni + ṛ is PrthivīYāska gives two derivations of the word Nirrti.They are:

(1) ni + ram, and (2) ni + ṛ.

̮̮

̮̮

Durgācārya’s commentary is helpful in understanding the full derivation from both the roots. According to him Nirṛti is the Earth and it is derived from ram = 'to enjoy', because creatures living on the earth rejoice here. The other derivation of Nirṛti is from ṛ which would mean calamity, pain and sorrow.It has been rightly pointed out that derivation of Nirṛti from + ram is loose due to the disregard to the vowels, since r in ram could have no correspondence to ṛ in old Indo-Āryan.

The second derivation of Nirṛti from ni + ṛ means misery, absence of prosperity.Mahīdhara in his commentary on the VS, 12.63 expresses Nirṛti as calamity and also as Earth.V.K. Rajwade renders Nirṛti as ‘piercing povertywhich, according to Siddheshwar Varma, may be corrected into ‘degenerating’.

The traditionally handed down meanings of the word Nirṛti show a dichotomy in the nature of Nirṛti as (1) blissful because Nirṛti is 'the earth' on which creatures live happily and (2) as a source of calamity. Thus Nirṛti is said to be blissful as well as a source of misery. This contradiction can be explained in the context of geological calamities. The earth undergoes upheavals due to earthquakes and volcanoes. Due to these events the same blissful earth becomes the cause of death and destruction. Thus the two meanings of Nirṛti (i.e., (1) the earth and (2) calamity) are not contradictory.

**Direction of Nirṛti**

Nirṛti is connected with the south-west direction of which she is the presiding deity. The VS, 9.35 refers to the oblations to be offered to Nirṛti in the south-west and in other direction for their presiding deities. The TB, 1.6.1.2 states that the south-west is her quarter where she reigns. A place where the soil is saline by nature (svakṛta-iriṇa)or a cleft (pradara) is her abode (TS, 5.2.33). She is offered oblations in the Nairṛtya (south-west) direction at an iriṇa (i.e., a place where the soil in saline) (ŚB, 5.2.3.2-3). because the saline land belongs to Nirṛti.The Kauśika Sūtra, XIII.28 recommends propitiatory rites in case of sudden cleft in ground in the

Yama — The Glorious Lord of the Other World village, or house, on fire-house or meeting place.

**Yamī and Nirṛti**

Yamī and Nirṛti are both equated with the Earth. In the TS, 3.3.8 it Is said Yama is Agni, this earth (i.e., the altar) is Yamī,while the TS, 4.2.5 states "As earth men know thee, Nirṛti, I know thee on every side.” But it is difficult to find an identification between Yamī and Nirṛti. The malevolent features of Nirṛti’s nature are obvious, while this cannot be said of Yamī. In the Brāhmaṇas also Nirṛti is not directly equated with YamīIt is also noteworthy that Nirṛti does not represent the earth as a whole, but only a part of it which is barren and uncultivable. The ŚB, 7.2.1.8 says “on whatever part of earth there is a cleaving or in whatever part of it plants do not grow, indeed, that part of earth is Nirṛti. In the context of the Rājasūya sacrifice in the ŚB, 5.2.3.3 and 7.2.10.8, the sacrificer takes a fire-brand from the anvāhārya-pacana (e.g., the southern) fire and therewith goes southwards. And where he finds a natural cleft, having made up a fire there he offers with the VS, 9.35. “This, Nirṛti! is thy portion, accept it gracefully, hail! For Nirṛti is this (earth), whomsoever she seizes upon with evil him she seizes upon with destruction (Nirṛti), hence whatever part of this (earth) is of Nirṛti nature that he thereby propitiates and thus Nirṛti does not seize upon him while being consecrated. And the reason why he offers in a self-produced hollow or cleft is that, that much of this (earth) is possessed by Nirṛti."

Some element of similarity is noticeable in the personalities of Yama and Nirṛti. The lap of Nirṛti is considered to be death (ṚV, 10.161.2; 10.95.14; AV, 3.11.2). The ṚV, 10.161.2; AV, 3.11.2 are used to bring back a sick man from the lap of Nirṛti. Nirṛti is punisher who binds the offender with a rope around his neck (AV, 6.63.1). Like Yama she holds a noose (TS, 4.2.53) which is an iron-noose (AV, 6.63.1). Freedom from the rope of Nirṛti means a long life, strength and vigor (AV, 6.63.1).

Nirṛti and Yama are both connected with evil dreams. In the ṚV, 10.164, Yama is the god invoked for dispelling evil dreams. The AV. 6.27-29 are employed for dispelling evil dreams and they are addressed to Yama and Nirṛti conjointly.

The dove and the owl, who are the messengers of Yama are also said to be Nirṛti’s messengers (AV, 6.27.1; 6.29.2).

In spite of Nirṛti having attributes like a noose, dove and owl as her messengers and her connection with evil dreams which are same as Yama’s, there is no similarity in the function of the two. Nirṛti is basically a goddess of death, destruction and disorder, while Yama is the ruler of the departed souls. Therefore, Nirṛti cannot be safely called a female counterpart of Yama.It is only in the later Vedic literature and more fully in the Epic and the Purāṇic literature that Yama came to be connected with death, one of his main tasks being that of taking away the lives. This later aspect of Yama is nearer to that of Nirṛti.

In the Vedic literature where Yama is equated with Mṛtyu (he is even called mārayitre which means ‘destroyer, killer’) it is quite possible to connected her with Yama, possibly as a female counterpart from the functional point of view.

## 4. Yama’s Personality and His Functions

**Yama's Sovereignty**

In the ṚV as well as in later Vedic texts Yama is constantly called rājan (a king). This suggests that he was the king of a particular region (ṚV,9.113.8; 10.14.1). This region is situated In parame vyoman (ṚV, 10.14.8) and according to Sāyaṇa parame vyoman refers to svarga (heaven). Yama's region is the abode of the Fathers too, where they live blissfully in the company of their lord Yama (AV, 5.2.14; ŚB, 7.1.14). Thus Yama is the king of the dead who reach his kingdom after death.

Yama is fondly remembered as the 'first to die' (AV, 18.3.13). After passing through death himself, he found out a new path and finally secured a settlement for all the people who died (ṚV, 10.14.2). The concept of a pioneer of anew path and finder of a new realm has its natural extension in the concept of his sovereignty over that newly found region.

Yama's association with Varuṇa is significant for establishing the former's own sovereignty. When the deceased reaches the highest heaven, he finds king Yama and Varuṇa rejoicing in the svadhā oblations offered to them (ṚV, 10.14.7). Varuṇa, the typical sovereign in the Vedic mythology, guards the cosmic order (ṚV, 7.86.1; 6.70.1; 5.69.4). It is said that by the law of Varuṇa heaven and earth are held apart (ṚV. 7.86.1; 6.70.1); he made a path for sun (ṚV, 1.24.8); he (with Mitra) supports heaven and earth (ṚV, 5.69.4). As a righteous king Varuṇa punishes the evil-doers (ṚV, 7.85.3). Yama’s association with Varuṇa suggests his equally high status of a sovereign. Yama is mentioned as holding a padbīśa (ṚV, 10.97.16) which can be regarded as a symbol of his sovereignty just as the pāśa is of Varuṇa (ṚV, 1.24.15:6.74.4; 10.85.24). A.A. Macdonell observes that Yama's foot-fetter (padbīśa) is spoken of as parallel to the bond of Varuṇa.

It is said that Varuṇa grabs a person who breaks the sacred order (TB, 1,7.2.6)and it is earnestly prayed: “May I be retrieved from the pāśa of Varuṇa and the padbīśa of Yama" (ṚV, 10.97.16, also VS, 7.112.2; 9.96.2). This suggests that Yama too punished the defaulters with his padbīśa as Varuṇa did with the pāśa. In the capacity of a punisher he is close to Varuṇa. Punishing the guilty, being one of the most important duties of a sovereign, Yama's sovereignty is evident. R.N. Dandekar observes that the possession by Yama of padbīśa corresponds to the pāśa of Varuṇa. This may be regarded as a symbol of sovereignty and seems to be one of the remnants of Yama's kingship.

A padbīśa is a pada-bandhanam,i.e., something by which one of confined by foot. The padbīśa is mentioned more frequently in connection with the trying of a horse (ṚV, 1.162.14; VS, 25.28) and it may presumably be a rope/ chain/noose mentioned in the Purāṇas with which Yama or his assistants binds/bind the evil-doers for taking them away to Yama’s region.In the Purāṇas Yama is described as holding a pāśa which he or (on some occasions) his dūtasuse for binding the people, whose period of life on earth is over, and taking them to Yama's kingdom.

**Lord of the Earth**

Yama, who is basically the king of the other world where the Fathers are his subject, is also mentioned as the overlord of the earth. Some scattered pieces of evidence surely suggest his original sovereignty over earth.

According to the ṚV, 10.14.9, Yama grants the required piece of land for performing the cremation of the deceased. (ṚV, 10.14.9. VS, 35.1, TS, 4.2.3), because he is the overlord of the earth. The AGS, 4.2.10 states that for preparing the place for cremation, the Adhvaryu priest sweeps the cremation place with a branch of śamī tree, while reciting apeta vīta (ṚV. 10.14.7). This ṛc is addressed to the fiends, etc. who might be residing at that place. They are told to leave the ground and move to far-off places since that ground is given to the deceased by Yama himself.

This stanza (i.e., 10.14.9) also appears in the VS with a slight variation (VA, 35.1). The second hemistich of the VS, 35.1 is same as the ṛc with only one change of dadāti to dadātu The first hemistich begins with apeta vīta as the ṚV, 10.14.9 with the rest part of it being variant. With the recitation of this VS-stanza the Panis who are the enemies of the gods (devapiyavaḥ) are told to leave the place which is being prepared for the pitṛmedha. According to the Kātyāyana- \`rauta-Sūtra, 21.2.32, the ground is to be swept with a palāśa branch or with śamī branch according to AGS, 4.2.10, with the recitation of apeta vīta

The VS, 35.1 and following stanzas are in connection with the 'pitṛmedha rites' and according to Mahīdhara this ritual is called varṣā-smaraṇam(Mahīdhara's comm, on VS, 35.1). Commenting on this group of mantras Mahīdhara quotes the Kātyāyana- \`rauta-Sūtra and the Yajus-pariśiṣṭa. He explains the details of the ritual connected with the making of a samādhi of the deceased, in which a pot containing the bones of the deceased is deposited in a pit specially prepared, in accordance with the prescribed measurement, for the purpose and these mantras are recited during this ritual. The VS, 35.1, apeta vīta is recited at the time of cleaning the place for funeral rites, but Mahīdhara explains it in a different context. While the ṛc is for the cremation of a dead body, the VS, 35.1 is according to Mahīdhara, for preparing a samādhi by placing the bones of the deceased in a special pit prepared for this purpose, according to the prescription of the śūlva- sūtra.

Thus it is seen that the stanza apeta vīta (ṚV, 10.14.9 as well as VS, 35.1) is recited at the time of the ritual-cleaning of the place of cremation-rites. It Is Interesting to note that this stanza also occurs in the VS (12.45) with a different background. The context here. i.e. VS, 12.45 is that of preparation of the Gārhapatya fire and the stanza is as under:

*apeta vīta vl ca sarpatāta ye atrā sthā purāṇa ye ca nūtanāḥ./*  
*adāt yamo avasānaṁ pṛthivya akrḥ imaṁ pitaro lokaṁ asmai/*

— VS, 12.45

The similarity of this stanza with the ṚV, 10.14.9 and VS, 35.1 apparent, and even where the words are different, the purport is more or less the same as that of the aforesaid stanzas. But it is important to note that the context is absolutely different here. While the ṚV, 10.14.9 and the VS, 35.1 both are in connection with the cremation rites, the present stanza VS. 12.45 is connected with the preparation for a sacrifice. This shows that Yama grants permission for using the required portion of land, be it for the cremation of the dead or for the sacrificial purposes. The VS, 12.45 is recited at the gārhapatya-cayana (Kātyāyana- \`rauta-Sūtra,17.1.1).

Mahīdhara comments that this stanza is addressed to the servants of Yama who might be present there. According to him Yama being the lord of the whole earth, his servants move at every placeand with this stanza they are told to move away, because Yama gave this place to the yajamāna (sacrificer).

Yama's overlordship over the earth is mentioned at several places. The TS, 3.45.1 mentions that Yama is the overlord of the earth just as Agni is of the creatures, Indra of the lokapālas, Vayu of the atmosphere and Sūrya of the sky.

The MS, 3.2.3 states that Yama is the god of this terrestrial region.Thus the whole expanse of earth belongs to Yama. So his permission is necessary for performing any rites in which a section of earth would be used: be it the cremation of a dead person, or the preparation of the fire- altar for performing sacrifices. One, who performs sacrifice without taking Yama’s permission, is not entitled to get the fruit of that sacrifice. If a negligent sacrificer piles up the fire- altar without seeking Yama's permission, he plies it up for Yama. Only after reciting the mantra — apeta vīta(VS, 12.45) and having asked Yama's permission thereby to use that portion for the sacrificial purpose, a sacrificer piles the altar for himself (TS, 5.3.2; MS, 3.2.3; ŚB, 7.1.1.2-33). Furthermore, by granting his permission Yama grants a debt to the sacrificer. If a sacrificer, therefore, occupies a place for the sacrifice and prepares a fire-altar there, he owes a debt to Yama. This debt can be repaid by completing the sacrifice properly. If he fails to do so, and leaves altar without kindling the fire, Yama's assistants bind him by neck and take him away to the other world (TS, 3.3.8.3-4).

**Strife for Supremacy**

The TS, 2.4.2 gives an account of a strife, for gaining supremacy, between the gods and Yama. Yama deprived the gods of their power and he himself gained supremacy. The gods reflected that Yama has become here what we are. From this account it can be deduced that Yama, who was till then the lord of the yonder world only, gained the over-lordship of the earth too after the struggle with the gods which Yama won.

In the ṚV there is no record of such a strife. S. Bhattacharji i observes that Yama was not initially immortal like other gods, but after some struggle with the established gods he wrested power and was raised to the rank of a god.But there is nothing to suggest here (TS, 2.4.2) that initially Yama was not a god and he gained the godhood after defeating the gods. This account only tells that Yama gained more power after defeating the gods. It is possible that his gaining the over-lordship is suggested here. Thus it can be said that a god who was already the lord of the yonder world and the king of the Fathers defeated other gods and gained the over-lordship of the earth too.

The MS, 25.11 records Yama’s attainment of the over-lordship of the earth.The Brāhmana-texts highlight the fruit of the sacrifices. The supremacy attained by Yama is attributed to the sacrifice. The TMB, 11.10.20 states that by this (sacrifice) Yama unconquerably won the over-lordship of the world.

Thus Yama’s gaining supremacy is attributed to two factors:

- (1) the struggle which he fought with the gods and won, and
- (2) by performing some special sacrifice.

**Lord of the South Direction**

The ṚV contains no clear record of Yama’s connection with the southern quarter: but in the AV he is explicitly connected with the south (AV, 9.7.20) of which he is the lord. His abode is also said to be in the southern direction from where he is invited along with the Fathers to receive the oblations offered to them.

But at a few places Indra is mentioned as the lord of the southern quarter, of which at other places Yama is mentioned as the lord (AV. 3.27.2, ŚB, 3.1.1.7). In the TS, 5.5.10 Indra is called the lord of the south and Yama of the vaśinī. Sāyaṇa explains vaśinī as the down-direction (adho dik)which he interprets as bhūmi—the earth. According to this explanation Yama is here regarded as the lord of the earth.

In AV, 6.28.3 Yama is mentioned as the master of all two-footed and four-footed creatures: here Yama is identified with Mṛtyu (death).

In the Brāhmaṇas too, as noted earlier. Yama rules over the settlements of the earth and he grants it to the sacrificer (ŚB. 7.1.1.1). Seeking his permission before preparing the fire-altar is obligatory.

According to the ŚB, 13.8.2.4 Yama rules over the earth and grants abode to the creatures. The ŚB, 2.3.2.1 states that Yama is the ruler of the dead, as well as one of the deities that, 'reside in him that exists’.

Thus it is seen that Yama, who was a lord of the yonder world (heaven), became the lord of earth also. Later he is the lord of southern direction only. Thus a god who Is regarded as the lord of the earth, gradually became the lord of south quarter only.

**Yama’s Connection with Sacrifice**

Yama is connected with sacrifice in a special way. Though Manu is called the first sacrificer (ṚV, 8.43.13; 10.63.7), the ṚV, 10.13.4 contains an account of Yama having performed a sacrifice and, having offered in it, for the cause of progeny, his own body as the final offering.

In the ṚV, 10.14.16 Yama is mentioned in connection with the three special Soma-sacrifices, which according to Sāyaṇa are Jyotiṣṭoma, Gauṣṭoma and Ayuṣṭoma sacrifices. This stanza is translated in the following way — “Yama acquires the three Trikadruka sacrifices, the six earths, the one great (moving world); the Triṣṭubh, Gāyatrī, all the sacred metres converge in Yama."

The expression patati in this ṛc signifies that Yama reaches these sacrifices to protect them. A.A. Macdonell takes the vocable trikadruka to mean the 'three Soma-vats' and Soma is described as flying through the three Soma- vats. The subject of the verb patati here is Soma according to A.A. Macdonell, Yama according to Sāyaṇa and the dead according to Geldner.

S.S. Dangeobserves that the idea of Yama being invoked to rush to protect the trikadruka does not seem quite convincing, because Yama does not figure at all in other places where the word trikadruka occurs (i.e., ṚV, 1.32.2; 2.11.7; 2.55.1; 22.1; 8.13.18; 92.21). In taking Yama as the subject of the verb patati Sāyaṇa appears to be influenced by the reference of Yama in the previous stanzas of this hymn (i.e., ṚV, 10.14). Furthermore this verse clearly shows two different ideas contained in the first and the second hemistich. The second hemistich states that the metres are established in (the abode of) Yama. With this also goes the second part of the first hemistich which describes the way to Yama’s world and it is said here that the unit of the six worldshas to be crossed to reach the abode of Yama.

In the AV, 18.1.32 Yama is said to have entered the sacrifice. This Sāyaṇa explains as “the sacrifice pleased Yama”, while Whitney translates it as ‘this sacrifice of mine is based on Yama'.

**Yama the Judge**

In the ṚV Yama is mentioned as holding a padbīśa(foot-fetter) which like the pāśa of Varuṇa can be regarded as a means of according punishment. A worshipper prays to be retrieved from the noose of Varuṇa and the fetters of Yama (ṚV, 10.97.16; VS, 12.90). Here is an indication that Yama, like Varuṇa punished the evil-doers. Yama is prayed to make the worshippers free from sins (AV, 3.2.11). The nature of this punishment, however, is not clear. The Vedic texts do not provide anything to suggest that Yama used his foot-fetters for binding people at the hour of death for carrying their lives away. This idea is expressed in the Puranas and there a rope or chain (nigada—GP, 2.23.38) is mentioned. With this rope Yama or his assistant binds (the soul of) a dying person.

In the Vedas Yama is principally the king of the Fathers and not the judge of good of evil deeds of men. However, the ŚB, 11.2.7.33 says that after his death a man’s deeds are mounted on a balance in the yonder world and he attains a good or bad region according to his deeds.It further says that one who realises this fact mounts the balance even in this world; i.e., he is careful about doing good deeds only, and is thus saved from being mounted on balance in the yonder world. The yonder world mentioned here refers to the region of Yama. The ŚB, 11.7.3.3 says that the separation of good and evil deeds is done by fire.

The TA, 6.5.13has the first clear statement that people are classified as truthful and untruthful in front of Yama. But here too, Yama’s role in separating them or judging their actions and awarding punishments accordingly is not very clear. It is significant that the TA, 6.5.13 states that the truthful and the liars are separated in the presence of Yama, the king. This may be considered as the earliest reference where Yama is depicted as supervising the separation of the good and the evil. Here the development from the ŚB, 11.2.7.33 is clear. In the tulādhāna(mounting on a balance), i.e., judging people as good or bad on the basis of their good or evil deeds, Yama’s involvement is absent, which is suggested in this later reference (TA, 6.5.13). Here it is said that the truthful and the liars (i.e., pious and the evil-doers) are separated before Yama; it can be assumed that this separation is done in order to punish the guilty (sinner) and reward the pious. It may be inferred here that since Yama watches the separation, he also pronounces the reward and punishment to people according to their good or bad deeds. This seems to be a precursor of Yama’s role as a judge of the dead, which is well established in the Epics and the Purāṇas.

**Yama’s Benevolence**

Like any other Vedic god Yama too is prayed to grant various things to the people. Though Yama is connected with the dead, of whom he is the king, it is significant that he is not considered to be a fearful god. The prayers to him are not offered out of fear as to Rudra.On the contrary Yama is considered to be a kind and benevolent god who himself passed through death, i.e., surrendered his immortality for the cause of progeny and became immortal in a new region. He grants longevity to the living beings; and to the dead a proper place in his kingdom.

In the ṚV. 10.14.14 Yama is besought for happiness and long life and also for prosperity and good health (ṚV, 10.14.11). The same idea is also found in the ṚV. 9.113.7.

In the AV, 19.20.1 Yama along with Soma, Varuṇa, Aśvins and Pūṣan is prayed to grant immortality. In the AV, 18.3.63; 18.4.54 Yama is prayed for an abundant life. A person praying to become immortal actuality wishes to enjoy a long life on earthand after death, a permanent place in Yama's region (ṚV, 9.113.7).

In the AV, 16.4.4 Yama is prayed for protection against the difficulties caused by men. In the AV, 3.2.11 Yama is prayed for the safe conduct, apparently from this life to the next, and also for deliverance from sins. According to the TS. 6.5.13 the righteous and evil-doers are separated under the supervision of Yama. It is, therefore justified that the worshipper prays Yama to free him from the sins. Death is also sought to be warded off with Yama's help. Yama is also a protector of the sacrifices. It is stated in the AV. 12.3.8 that Yama and the Fathers protect the vessel of food cooked for the sacrificial purpose.

In the Vedic literature Yama is mainly a king of the departed souls and he is not directly connected with the phenomenon of death. Hence, hardly any element of fear is seen in the hymns addressed to Yama. He is considered to be a king, a protector and a well-wisher of all the dead who reach his region. Though the occasion of death is of profound sadness, and though there are frequent prayers to Yama requesting him to grant a long life, even then Yama is not a feared-god simply because he has no direct connection with death. Though people reach his region after death, he does not cause death. He takes under his wings the soul who reaches his region. Yama announces that by reaching there the dead had became his own. Yama grants him a place in his region and announces that then onwards he, i.e., the dead would receive a portion from the oblations which are offered to him (i.e.. Yama) (AV, 18.2.37).

The Kaṭha Up also provides the details of Yama's personality which is far from fearful. He exhibits extreme politeness and reverence towards a guest by offering him, here Naciketas, three boons for the three nights he had spent at Yama’s palace-gate,waiting for him.

It is also significant that when Naciketas’ father angrily told him that he had given him (Naciketas) to Mṛtyu, Naciketas was not at all afraid of an encounter with Yama. This proves the point that since Yama does not cause death; he is not a feared god. He is regarded with high esteem and reverence, because he is the lord and protector of people after their death, which is considered to be the surest reality in the earthly life.

**Yama: The Creator**

According to the ṚV. 10.10.4 Yamī is a twin-sister of Yama and they are the offspring of a Gandharva and apyā-yoṣā. Sāyaṇa, Udgitha and Mahīdhara take Gandharva and apyā-yoṣā to be Vivasvat and Saraṇyū. F. Max Mūller holds a similar view.

In the ṚV Yamī appears only in the 10th Mandala. The ṚV. 10.10 contains a dialogue between Yama and Yamī. which appears also in the AV. 18.1.1-16. In this dialogue- hymn Yamī, with a desire to procreate asks Yama to oblige her by uniting with her as with a wife. Yama refuses saying that he does not want such relation, since they are brother and sister. Yamī persuades him and says that gods take pleasure in a union like this which is forbidden for the mortals (ṚV. 10.10.3).She further adds that no one would know of it (ṚV, 10.10.6). Yama, not the least influenced by these arguments, asks her that it is not proper for them to do something which was never done before (ṚV, 10.10.4).He further states that no one can escape the watchful eyes of the spies of the gods (ṚV, 10.10.8) and that the gods would be offended by such an immoral act. In the AV, 18.1.14 Yama says that the idea of a brother and a sister sleeping together is something to which his mind revolts. Yamī argues that they have lain together once in the mother’s womb (it would not be a sin to do so again), since the gods have created them to make a conjugal pair even in their mother's womb. (ṚV, 10.10.5; AV, 18.1.1). But Yama remains unfaltered and rebuffs her saying that such intimacy is considered immoral in which a salakṣmā (i.e., akin, a sister) becomes viṣurūpā (i.e., non-akin), i.e., a sister becomes a wife.The subsequent ages would come when such deeds would be done. She should, therefore, approach some other man (ṚV, 10.10.10). Yamī speaks comtemptuously, when Yama rejects her suit altogether.

The theme of this dialogue has led to an assumption that Yama and Yamī were the parents of humanity and Yamī is thus considered both, ‘a sister and a wife of Yama’.In this hymn Yamī, though a jāmi to Yama wants to behave like an ajāmi and approaches Yama, the only martya (i.e., mortal), with a desire for sexual union in order to procreate and start a human progeny.Yamī says, that the gods also desire off¬springs from one alone of mankind.Yama gently but firmly turns down this proposal calling such a relation between a brother and a sister as a highly immoral act. Yama's firm and strong denial here gives a glimpse of an important aspect of Yama's character, which later developed in the Purāṇas where he is called Dharmarāja, i.e., the upholder of the moral and ethical order. In spite of Yamī ’s various arguments Yama rejects her request on moral grounds and suggests her to approach some other man: therefore, this hymn does not imply that the incest actually took place, and the humanity originated from it, making Yama and Yamī the parents of the human race. Furthermore, there is no other reference connecting Yamī with the creation of humanity. Though ṚV, 7.33.9: 10.13.4 suggest Yama’s role as a procreator. Yamī has no place there. Had the incest actually taken place, the Vedic poets would not have left it without mentioning or suggesting. The hypothesis that the two formed the first human couple whence came the whole race of humanshas been rightly challenged by Max Mūller and has been discarded.

R.N. Dandekar holds the view that the idea of the origin of the mankind through an incest was very offending to the morality of that age. The poet-seers did not mention it and even left the hymn (ṚV, 10.10) incomplete, because if completed, it would have nullified their purpose. “This hymn is evidently a protest against a union that was unobjectionable to the older generation."

The assumption of Yama and Yamī as the parents of humanity is mainly based on similar myths in other primitive mythologies which record an incest and the origin of mankind through it.There is nothing in the Vedas to support it.

Though Yama is an Indo-Iranian divinity, there is no warranty for saying that Yamī is also the same, because Yimak who is considered to be the Iranian counterpart of Yamī appears only in the post-Avestan writings.The part of a myth which developed in later writings cannot be foisted upon the earlier writings.Hence the myth of an incest and the origin of the human race, therefrom cannot be considered Indo-Iranian. it seems to have developed independently in the later Iranian literature.

The ṚV. 10.10 and the AV, 18.1.1-16 close without giving the slightest indication of Yamī 's success in accomplishing her desire. But the hymn looks complete even without it and conveys its meaning emphatically. It depicts Yama speaking again and again against the immoral act in spite of receiving contemptuous remarks from Yamī; so the hymn looks complete and not a torso. The hymn would appear to have ended abruptly and inconclusively only if read with a prejudiced concept of the incest virtually having taken place, as is found in other primitive mythologies. But the Vedic text does not provide any such hint. In the AV also this hymn, which occurs there with slight variations, ends at the same juncture as in the ṚV.

The interpretation of myths dealing with incest or awkward sex relations demand extreme care. There are a number of such myths other than that of Yama and Yamī like, the myth of Vṛṣākapi, or Brahmā and Sarasvatī, etc. They do not necessarily indicate a social behaviour.In many cases already floating myths go enacted for rain and prosperity getting Vedic names. On the other hand many such rituals get turned into awkward myths. According to Ulrisch Schneider,in olden times the birth of a twin of opposite sexes was considered evil. Since they although being brother and sister were laid together in the mother's womb (cf. ṚV. 10.10.5. 7). It may, therefore.be said that the ṚV. 10.10 was considered as a part of the ritual to avert the inauspiciousness believed to be brought by the birth of the twins, especially the twins of the opposite sex. Thus this hymn had a ritual application of being recited for the expiation on the birth of such a twin. S.A. Dange observes that an important aspect of the Vedic ritual accounts is the element of sex in it. When such rituals fell back, the mere account that was a word frame for the ritual lost its meaning. According to him the Yama-Yarm or Vṛṣākapi and others such myths were developed from original rituals and these are the cases where ritual produced wrong myths. Hence what appears to be a mythical account was never a myth but only a description and the guidelines to the ritualists and “though somewhat elliptical to later generations it was a formula — like indication for the ancient people." These ritual accounts became myths at a stage when the original tradition was lost, as in the case of Yama-Yami hymn (ṚV, 10.10). In some cases the ritual was not completely lost. e.g. the Ṛgvidhāna (3.119.127) enjoins the two verses from the Vṛṣākapi hymn (ṚV, X.86.16-17) to be recited at the special bath to be administered to an issueless women, or to one who is not happy with her husband. In the case of Yama-Yamī hymn the ritual is forgotten, only the account of the rituals is preserved in this hymn.

In the introduction to the AV, 3.28.4, which is used to avert the evil effect of a twin’s birth, Sāyaṇa remarks that the birth of a twin to a cow, a mare, a she-donkey or a woman is an unusual occurrence, and this hymn is used as an expiatory recitation for averting the evil.

The ṚV, 10.13.4 contains an account of Yama having performed a sacrifice in which he, as the chief priest and the sacrificer offered his own body as the sacrificial oblation. This sacrifice was performed for an onset of progeny and it can be called the primeval sacrifice. Out of the self-immolation of Yama in this sacrifice the human progeny originated. The ṛc under consideration is as follows:

*devebhyaḥ kam avṛṇīta mṛtyuṁ,*  
*prajāyai kam amṛtaṁ nāvṛṇita/*  
*bṛhaspatiṁ yajñam akṛṇvata ṛṣiṁ,*  
*priyāṁ yamas tanvaṁ prārirecīt./*  
*(ṚV, 10.13.4)*

According to Sāyaṇa this ṛc can be rendered as follows:

Whom has he (i.e., Yama) selected as the killer of the gods? Whom has he not selected as the non-slayer of the human beings? (Yama does not send a killer at a time when the process of havir-dhāraṇa is taking place). The priests performed the sacrifice with the mantras. Yama saves our dear body from death.

Sāyaṇa 's interpretation does not help to get a clear import of the stanza. Following Sāyaṇa H.H. Wilson translates —  
Whom has he selected as the slayer of the gods? Whom has he not selected as the non¬slayer of the human race? They have made ṛṣi Bṛhaspati the sacrifice. Yama preserves our dear bodies.

According to Geldner’s interpretation, which holds a diversity, a portion of the ṛc can be translated as — for gods (Yama) took death, for the progeny he did not take immortality. The gods made rsi Bṛhaspati the offering. An altogether different interpretation is suggested by N.G. Chapekar — Yama has taken away the dear body (of this dead man). Why did he choose mortality for the people? Why did he kill this man in spite of his having performed the sacrifice with mantras (Bṛhaspati).

The present ṛc is considered to be exceedingly obscure,but it surely contains one of those few stray and scattered references connected with a Vedic god which have escaped priestly revision and, therefore, retain the traces of the original character of that god.It also contains one of the two versions of the mythological motif of the primeval sacrifice, the other version is found in the Puruṣa-Sūkta (ṚV, 10.90).

Strictly following the text this ṛc (ṚV, 10.13.4) may be rendered as follows:

̮̮

For the sake of the gods indeed (he) chose death; (he) did not choose indeed in the interest of the progeny a life immortal. They made Bṛhaspati, the rsi, a sacrificial offering. Yama gave up (his) dear body.

The middle voice of uṛ in the first hemistich suggests that Yama chose mortality for himselfand he did not prefer immortality for himself. According to the first hemistich of the ṛc the gods had arranged this sacrifice for (the birth of) the human progeny. The second hemistich concluded that Bṛhaspati, the rsi was offered as the oblation and Yama gave up his dear body. This indicates that Yama was himself the bṛhaspati, i.e., the principal priest and the sacrificer. The chief priest is also called bṛhaspati, because he is the representative of the Bṛhaspati, the divine priest. In the VS, 2.12 the Brahman priest is called bṛhaspati. Commenting on the VS, 2.12 Uvvaṭa and Mahidhara state that Bṛhaspati is designated as Brahman priest in the sacrifice performed by the gods, and even men attain this title by taking resort to Bṛhaspati.

Here in the ṛc under discussion (ṚV, 10.13.4) they (i.e., the gods) made the priest (i.e., the bṛhaspati) the offering (yajña).In the light of this study the purport of the ṚV, 10.13.4 is intelligible. It is referring to a primeval sacrifice arranged by the gods with the definite purpose, i.e., beginning of a progeny. In the sacrifice they offered the sacrificer, i.e., Yama as the oblation because he chose to give up his body in order to accomplish the desire of the gods. Thus, this ṛc records the immolation of a divine being who was immortal like other gods. This explains why Yama is called a mortal in spite of his divine birth.

The Puruṣa-Sūkta (ṚV, 10.90) gives a detailed account of a sacrifice in which a cosmic man Purusa is sacrificed. The basic motif in the case of Yama (ṚV, 10.13.4) and Purusa (ṚV, 10.90) is thus the same, i.e., the immolation of a divine being for the sake of creations.On this basis Yama is considered to be Father of the mankind.

According to the Puruṣa-Sūkta the sacrifice is arranged by the gods (ṚV, 10.90.6) in which a Cosmic Man Puruṣa is sacrificed (ṚV, 10.90.15) and the creation originated from the different parts of his body.The ṚV, 10.13.4 presents a similar account, though very vague and incomplete, of a sacrifice and the self-immolation of a divine being, i.e., Yama, for the cause of the gods and progeny. It is, however, not mentioned how Yama’s sacrifice helped in starting the human progeny, a point which is mentioned in the Puruṣa-sūkta. It can be said that the ṚV, 10.13.4 and ṚV, 10.90 are two versions of mythological cosmogonic motif of the primeval sacrifice.

R.N. Dandekar also notices similarity in the character and Yama and Purusa. According to him, both of them are hermophrodites. Puruṣa creates Virāj out of his own self (ṚV, 10.90.2). Though Yama is not directly expressed as a bisexual being, Gūntert has pointed out that his name Yama' can be linguistically connected with Lat. Geminus, Gemullus, and GK didumos which mean both the twin and a hermaphrodite.From the similarity of sacrifices mentioned in the ṚV, 10.13.4 and the ṚV, 10.90, and also on the basis of the similarity in the character and the functions of Yama and Puruṣa, it can be concluded that the Puruṣa of the Puruṣa-sūkta is only a more detailed priestly modification of the original mythical conception of Yama.

It is suggested in the ṚV 10.13.4 that out of the primeval sacrifice in which Yama immolated himself the progeny originated. On this basis Yama is considered to be the father of mankind. It is also important to note that Yama is said to have started progeny through a primeval sacrifice and Yamī plays no part in it. The idea of incest is not supported by the text.Yama and Puruṣa are similar in character as divine beings sacrificed in the primeval sacrifice, but Puruṣa not only started a human progeny, he is also the creator of the universe. Origin of the various kinds of creatures as well as the sun. moon, sky, ether, air, fire and earth is attributed to Puruṣa (ṚV. 10.90.8, 10, 13, 14).

Manu too is called the progenitor of mankind (ṚV, 1.80.16) and the first sacrificer (ṚV, 8.43.13; 10.63.7). He is also a son of Vivasvat. Due to these aspects Manu is a 'double' of Yama. He came to be regarded as the father of mankind when Yama was allocated the kingdom of the dead. Yama’s role as the first sacrificer was also transferred to Manu.

R.N. Dandekar holds the view that the incestuous union of Yama and Yamī produced the mankind, but the idea of humanity having originated from an incest was highly objectionable to the morality of that age and so the Yama-Yamī-sūkta is left incomplete. This motif of incest in Yama- mythology seems to have been particularly responsible for the withdrawal of Yama from the Vedic cosmogonic mythology and the priestly emphasis laid on Yama’s double, Manu. There was a considerable interaction between the Yama-myth and the Manu-myth and Manu-myth overshadowed the Yama-myth. Yama's character as the progenitor of human race was forgotten and only a few stray references reveal it. The parallels between the Yama-myth and the Puruṣa -myth helped to bring the Manu-myth and Puruṣa-myth together. Yama and Puruṣa having identical characters, while Puruṣa and Manu, both signifying ‘man’ being more or less synonyms, there was a considerable intermixture of these three—Yama, Puruṣa and Manu-myths.

The idea of Yama having created the human progeny also finds expression in the ṚV, 7.33.9; 12 where it is said that Yama spread out the ‘garment of creation'.The theory which is very vaguely and indirectly mentioned in the ṚV, 10.13.6, that Yama created a progeny, is explicitly mentioned here in the ṚV, 7.33.9.

*te it niṇyam hṛdayasya praketaiḥ, sahasra-valśaṁ abhi saṁ caranti/*  
*yamena tataC paridhiC uayantaḥ apsarasaḥ upaseduḥ vasiṣṭhāḥ.*

They alone move about fearlessly owing to the innate knowledge of their heart, in that secret of the thousand branches intending to weave that garment, first woven by Yama, Vasiṣṭha approaches the celestial nymph (for birth).

According to this ṛc Vasiṣṭha approached the celestial nymph for being born from her. It was due to a strong urge to extend the garment which was (first) woven by Yama, that Vasiṣṭha wanted to be born.He desired to enter the thousand-branched and thousand-threaded secret namely the mundane existence which was started by Yama. Saṁsāra is conceived here as a tree of a thousand branches (like the aśvattha of the BG' 15-16) and a garment of a thousand threads in the ṚV. 7.33.9c.

The vocable paridhi in this stanza is taken to be a cloth, a garment.But paridhi also denoted a sacrifice since tantu stands for yajña at several places. In the ṚV, 10.53.6; 9.22.6, 7, Sāyaṇa takes tantu in the sense of 'a sacrifice’. Following Sāyaṇa, Wilson translates tantum tanvan rajaso bhānum anvihi as “spreading out the sacrifice follow the Illuminator of the world".

On the other hand the word tantu is also used to denote a thread, which can also metaphorically denote the thread of progeny, continuation of the family line. The ṚV, 10.56.6 has tantum ātatam which Sāyaṇa translates as — prajām vitata kṛtavantaḥ iti śeṣaḥ putra-pautrādi rupeṇa anavacchinnām prajām ityarthaḥ. The AB, 3.38 says prajā vai tantuḥ. In the TA, 7.11.1 also tantu signifies the continuity of progeny and it is said here prajā tantum mā vyavachhetsīḥ; thus tantu signifies the continuity of progeny. It is interesting to note that the word tanaya denoting 'son' also, like the word tantu, is derived fromtan

The two views regarding the meaning of tantu are discussed by Sāyaṇa in his commentary on the ṚV, 6.92. Here he presents both the views. According to saṁpradāya-vidaḥ, ie., the knowers of the tradition, here is a figurative allusion to the mysteries of the sacrifice where the threads of warp, (tantu) are the metres of the Vedas, and those of the woof, (otu) are the Yajus (i.e., liturgic prayers and rituals and the combination of the two is the cloth, i.e., the sacrifice.) Tantus are the various threads of the cloth which it is made up of. The Vedic metres, e.g., Gāyatrī, etc. are also tantus of a sacrifice which it is made up of. The other view is of the ātmavidaḥ or the Vedantins who understand tantu as alluding to the mystery of creation. Thus ‘spreading out the garment’ denotes creation of the progeny, which, the ṚV, 7.33.9; 12 state, was started by Yama. It is clear that Yamena tatam paridhim highlights Yama’s function as a creator which is only vaguely suggested in the ṚV, 10.13.4.

## 5. Yama: The Preacher and Eschatology

In the Upaniṣads Yama’s connection with death is clearly expressed. At several places he is even identified with mṛtyu (death). In the KatḥaUp Yama emerges as a preacher of the Brahman-philosophy. He imparts the knowledge of the highest principle to Naciketas, who chose it as the third boon which Yama had promised him. It is only after testing Naciketas for the firmness of his determination as against all allurements of worldly pleasures (KatḥaUp, 1.22; 1.29; 1.23-25) and being satisfied with the worthiness of this recipient, Yama happily disclosed to young Naciketas the knowledge which is difficult to be obtained even for the gods.

According to the KatḥaUp Naciketas, the son of Vājaśravas was deeply troubled on seeing his father giving away old cows as the sacrificial gifts which he offered after the completion of a sacrifice. Those cows were no longer able to drink, to eat, to give milk or to calve (KatḥaUp, 1.37) Naciketas, truthful as he was, could not reconcile himself to this deception which he thought was the path to a joyless hellish existence. He approached his father and asked ‘whom will you give me?' In asking this he revealed his boldness proceeding from the innate virtue of śraddhā. When he repeated his question a second and then a third time, the father got infuriated and said, 'I give you over to Death’. Young Naciketas was neither disturbed nor angry. With his passion for truth and fearlessness he reached the doors of Yama. Yama was away from his abode and Naciketas waited there for his return. Yama returned after three nights and considered it a lapse on his part for having kept a holy guest waiting. He decided to make amends and asked Naciketas to choose three boons.

With the first boon Naciketas secured his return to his father who with his anger bygone, would welcome him back. As the second boon he learnt from Yama the details of a fire (i.e., sacrifice) which leads to heaven. At this juncture Yama conferred an additional boon that that fire would thenceforth bear Naciketas’ name. As the third boon, Naciketas, with utmost preciseness, asked for the secret of the life after death to be disclosed to him. He said that there was a doubt in regard to a man who had died. Some said he existed, while others said he did not exist. Yama reflected that the mystery of the life after death was difficult to be understood. Even the gods had not been sure on that subject and Naciketas was too young to assimilate that knowledge; therefore Yama offered various attractive gifts, i.e., centenarian sons and grandsons, beautiful women, herds of cattle, elephants, horses, gold, a very long life for himself and told him not to ask about the existence of man after death. But Naciketas insisted on his boon because where else he could get a better instructor than Yama on a subject regarding the life after death. Naciketas was not lured by wealth and long life or other-worldly pleasures and he refused to choose something else as a boon. He wanted the secret of death to be revealed to him by the Lord of Death himself. Here Yama was pleased that between the ‘good’ (śreyas) and the 'pleasant' (preyas) Naciketas had chosen the 'good'. He was now convinced that Naciketas was a true seeker of knowledge. He expressed a desire, viz. "May there be for us an inquirer like you, Naciketas" (1.2.9). Ignorant men under delusion of wealth think that “there is nothing beyond this world and they came again and again under my (i.e., death’s) way” (1.2.6).

Yama spoke to Naciketas on the mystic symbol Oṁ as synonymous with Brahman. He also preached the doctrine of the immortality of the soul (I.e., the Self, the Ātman) — The Self is not born, nor does It die; it sprang from nothing and nothing sprang from it; it is unborn, constant, eternal and primeval; it is not slain when the body is slain. If a killer thinks that he kills, or if the killed thinks that he is killed, both of them do not understand that this Self neither slays, nor is slain (1.2.19). This self cannot be obtained by mere academic pursuits, nor by intellect. He is to be obtained only by the one whom he chooses (1.2.23).

Narrating a parable of the chariot, Yama further explains the body to be a chariot and Self to be sitting in it. In this chariot intelligence (buddhi) is the charioteer and the mind the reins, while the senses are the horses (1.3.3-4). A wise man's mind (i.e., the reins) are firmly held and therefore his senses (i.e., the horses) are under his total control (1.3.3-5).

Though Yama’s objective is to answer Naciketas' query about the life beyond death, he now teaches about the Supreme Self, of which the Individual Self is a part. While instructing about the survival of the finite Self, Yama dilates on Brahman solely with an object of enabling Naciketas see that the finite Self is non-different from the infinite Self.

The non-difference of jīva and Brahman forms the central teaching of all the Upaniṣads. The five Mahāvākyas (1) ahaṁ brahmāsmi — BṛUp, 1.4.10, (2) tat tvam asi— ChāUp, 6.87, (3) sarvam khalv idaṁ brahma— ChāUp, 3.14.1 (4) ayaṁ ātmā brahma — MāUp, 1.2, (5) satyaṁ jñānam anantaṁ brahma— TaitUp, 2.1.1 occurring in different Upaniṣads affirm this Identity in a direct and forthright manner.

After explaining to Naciketas the real nature of jīva, Yama takes up the original question and tells about the survival of the Self after death. According to their deeds and knowledge some enter a womb in order to be born again and some get insentient form and go into in organic matter. The Self is one but takes different forms, just as the fire takes different forms according to whatever it burns; in a similar way one Self within all things becomes different according to whatever it enters (2.5.9). Yama declares that one gets liberated from the clutches of death by discerning that Supreme Self.

**Eschatology**

Admission in the realm of Yama is considered to be the highest fate for the dead. He Joins there the category of the Fathers, who are not only worshipped like gods but are also believed to possess powers to Influence the life of those on earth. In the rites offered to the dead the main idea is to secure for him, the dignity of pitṛ (Fathers) (ṚV, 10.16.1; 2) and thus make him immortal in Yama's region.

**Heaven**

According to the Vedic thought the kingdom of Yama is heaven where the Fathers reside and revel in the company of their king Yama; therefore, Yama is called saṇgamanaṁ janānām (ṚV, 10.14.1) 'the gatherer of the people'. At the cremation the dead is told to go to Yama who collects all the dead in his realm, because that is the place which he discovered for all the dead to live in (ṚV, 10.14.2).

**Ritual Heaven**

There are some references in the later texts like the Brāhmaṇas which seem to speak of svarga (heaven) as explicitly distinguished from the abode of the dead. There are references of a sacrificer reaching heaven immediately after completing a sacrifice, i.e., even without dying. It will be an error if it is interpreted that the sacrificer died immediately after completing the sacrifice, and went to heaven. This shows that svarga was not always identical with the abode of the blessed dead. As observed by J. Gonda, such statements where the sacrificer is said to have attained heaven without having died refer to the ritual heaven, and the sacrificer's ritual birth therein.The idea of the ritual heaven, i.e., the earthly heaven, is made clear by such identification as in ŚB, 9.3.4.13 where the āhavanīya fire is said to be the svargaloka and when they annoint the sacrificer there it is said that they annoint him in the world of heaven. The ŚB, 13.2.8.5, quoted also by Mahīdhara at the VS, 23.20 states that ‘heaven is where they immolate the victim’.Mahīdhara adds that the term svarga-loka is synonymous with yajñabhūmi, i.e., the sacrificial place.

According to the PB, 10.4.5 the sacrificer after having reached the svarga-loka (heavenly abode) remains resplendent until old age and eats food suitable for brahmins. As the sacrificer here Is said to have (ideally) reached heaven and he is wished to live here on earth until old age, it is evident that the real heaven and the bliss of that world are not meant here, but the earthly welfare of the sacrificer after he has reached the ritual-heaven and has returned to this world.

**Location of Heaven**

Yama’s kingdom is situated in the midst of the sky (ṚV, 10.15.14), In the highest heaven (ṚV, 10.14.8). The worshipper earnestly desires to become immortal in Yama’s kingdom which Is a place of eternal light (ṚV, 9.113.7,9). According to the AV also Yama’s region Is in the highest (6.63.3) and luminous world (18.1.57), situated in the third firmament called pradyau and which is the dwelling-place of the Fathers (18.2.48). The other two firmaments are mentioned as undanvatī (watery) and pīḷumatī (starry). At the funeral the dead is told to acquire a place among Fathers and prosper in their company (18.2.25).The dead find rest in the third heaven. Heaven is said to be the world of light brightened by sun (AV, 9.5.18).

In the Yama-loka the pitṛs are said to enjoy in the company of Yama (ṚV, 10.14.10; 10.15.8); they eat the offerings made to them with svadhā (ṚV, 10.14.7). The pious drink soma with Yama (1.35.6). In the ṚV, 1.154.5b a place where all god-seeking people delightis also said to be the ‘favorite region of Viṣṇu, who is a friend of the pious".

**Heaven /Yama-loka**

Yama’s region bears the name Yama-loka (TB. 3.9.20.1, JB, 1.167, PB. 9.8.4, etc.). It is also called Yamasya- sādanam, i.e., ’the abode of Yama’ (AV, 18.2.56). In the kingdom of Yama the pious dead live in the companionship of the gods. In the AV, 18.2.55 Savitṛ is prayed to take the dead to the region where the pious dwell. The next stanza (AV, 18.2.56) states that this region is Yamasya sādanam, I.e., 'abode of Yama' where the dead is being carried to.This shows that Yamasya sādanam or the region of Yama is considered to be heaven, i.e., the region of the pious. The dead is told to go to the region where his ancestors have gone before; it is said that there he would see Yama and Varuṇa (ṚV, 10.14.6). The Jātavedas Agni is urged to take the deceased to the Fathers (ṚV, 10.16.1-2), to the region of the pious (sukṛtām lokam—ṚV, 10.16.4).

On the basis of these statements and other similar references viz. ṚV, 10.14.14,etc., it may be concluded that Yama’s region was considered to be the highest heaven which is the ultimate goal of all god-seeking people.

It may be noted that in the Brāhmaṇas Yama's region also bears the name pitṛ-loka. One who performs sacrifices cherishes a hope; “May there be a place for me in Yama's world" (ŚB, 4.3.4.27).

Life of the dead in this region, which is brightened by in — exhaustible light is full of joy. It is to be noted here that in the Vedic thought the ideas of sun, sunlight, celestial, light are inextricably mixed up with those of well-being, good fortune, happiness and glory, the light of the sun means the possibility of life,of activity and of human existence and 'to see the sun’ means ’to live’ (cf. ṚV, 10.14.12; 10.57.4).

The most detailed Ṛgvedic account of the pleasures of the life in heaven is found in the ṚV, 9.113.7-11. According to it the eternal light illuminates this region; abundant waters flow; all desires are fulfilled and people are satiated. There is joy and merriment everywhere and the place resounds with the music of flutes and songs (ṚV, 10.135.7). This region of eternal bliss can only be attained by those who perform meritorious deeds and hence it is called sukṛtāṁ lokaḥ(ṚV, 10.16.4, VS, 15.50). Here the pious dead get whatever they had sacrificed and had offered as gifts to the priests (ṚV, 10.154.3, etc.). They are also united with the fruit of their iṣṭāpūrta (the merits acquired by sacrifices and charitable acts); therefore, the sacrificers, who performed religious rites in conformity with the approved norms, expected their post-mortem existence to be happy. Here we have possibly the germ of the later doctrine of karma. In the ṚV the term karman means 'work' in general and specifically ‘the sacrificial work’. Such work constitute the merit which wins the pleasures of heaven for the departed souls.

In the heaven the dead unites with the Fathers and assumes a shining body (ṚV, 10.14.8). This body is free from diseases, frailties and imperfections (ṚV, 10.16.6). The AV also states that the dead assumes a special body in heaven which is free from imperfections. His limbs are not lame or crooked any longer (AV, 3.28.5; 6.120.3). He is said to assume a complete body (AV, 18.2.21). In the heaven the deity carrying his life (i.e., asunitī) shapes his body (AV, 18.3.59). Yama makes his body free from diseases (AV,18.2.12). In the heaven the new body is joined with mind, life and limbs (AV, 18.2.24). Similar ideas are expressed elsewhere in the AV (AV, 6.120.3).

According to this description heaven is a bright world where pious people live in joy and harmony. They cast aside their ailments and physical deformities like lameness and deformed limbs. They acquire a luminous body and meet their relatives. The AV describes, quite explicitly, the pleasures of heaven enjoyed by one who offers bṛhmodana. In the realm of Yama he enjoys the company of the Gandharvas, he moves on a chariot (AV. 4.34.4). He also finds sexual pleasures there. Lakes full of ghṛta, honey, wine, milk, water and curds are seen here (AV, 4.34.2-6).

On reaching heaven with their new faultless bodies the pious dead are welcomed by the gods and are loved by them (ṚV, 10.56.1). The dead beholds both Yama and Varuṇa there (ṚV, 10.14.7). Yama recognises him as his own and offers him a resting place (AV, 18.2.37). Here are divine cows who yield all the desired objects (AV, 4.34.8). Here the dead souls get food and water offerings bestowed by their descendants on the earth (AV, 18.4.36; ŚB, 13.8.1.4).

According to the KatḥaUp, 1.12.13,18 the svarga-loka is a place of delight. Here people with all their sorrows gone enjoy themselves.The KatḥaUp, 1.3 mentions several lokas, i.e., the worlds of different gods such as that of Agni, Vāyu, Varuṇa, Āditya, Indra, Prajāpati, and Brahmā. Śaṅkarācārya on the Vedānta-Sūtra, 4.3.4 comments that the word loka means a place for enjoying the fruit of one's actions.A gradation of the higher worlds is described in the BṛUp, 3.6 like this: Deva-loka, Prajāpati-loka, Brahma-loka. A combination of blissful human condition, bodily happiness, power, well-being, etc. with an attempt at systemizing in a certain order, the lokas of the Fathers, Gandharvas, Prajāpati, etc. is presented by the author of BṛUp, 4.3.33.

Though there are references in the ṚV to heaven, the kingdom of Yama where all blessed souls of those who had performed meritorious acts live happily, the doctrine of reward and punishment, according to which all actions of the people are evaluated, was not yet formulated. It is clear that the seers of the ṚV intended on a happy life on earth, where they wished to live for a hundred autumns and even more (ṚV, 7.66.16).

The general idea behind various mantras of the AV is also to enhance the happiness of this life by removing death, diseases and other difficulties and subduing the enemies. The thoughts of the Vedic seers rarely dwell upon the life after death: therefore, the reference to the pleasures enjoyed in the heaven are very few and those of the punishments and sufferings of hells are fewer. And the dead are said to be receiving in the heaven the reward of their iṣṭāpūrta, i.e., the religious merit earned by performing the sacrifices and by charitable acts.

Though in the ṚV heaven is repeatedly referred to as sukṛtāṁ lokaḥ (i.e., the region of the pious), which these people attain by their religious merit, but the germs of the doctrine of karman can also be seen in the ṚV. There is a clear reference that in heaven the dead is united with the fruit of his sacrificial and charitable acts (ṚV, 10.34.8).

It is also to be noted here that in the ṚV heaven is a place of permanent stay for a person who is granted entry into it. This is made clear by the expressions contained in the ṚV, 10.107.2; and ṚV, 9.113.7-11, etc. where the man is said to become immortal in Yama’s region. The concept of the religious merit being exhausted by enjoying pleasures in heaven was not yet developed. The religious merit is thus never exhausted and, therefore, he does not have to be born again on earth.

Thus heaven in the ṚVis more like mokṣa, i.e., the place of emancipation rather than a place for enjoying the heavenly pleasures for the given time which a man earns by performing meritorious deeds on the earth. According to the PB, 6.8.15 (also ŚB. 2.4.2.9) the 'yonder world’, (heaven) is granted once and for all away from here,The ŚB, 2.4.29 also states that “people going to heaven do not return” \[sakṛt parāñcaḥ pitaraḥ —ŚB. 2.4.29); such passages do not contemplate the idea of exhaustion of sakṛta. On the other hand people living in this world are born again and again. The ŚB, 13.2.4.1 clearly identifies asau lokaḥ, (yonder world) and the sphere of the gods. It also identifies this world as manuṣya-lokaḥ.

**Passage to Heaven**

For reaching heaven (the abode of Yama) the spirit of the dead takes up the path which was found by Yama. It is a steep path leading to the highest heaven (ṚV, 10.14.1-2). The Fathers are said to have taken up this path (for reaching Yama’s abode) (ṚV, 10.14.7). By this path the dead goes to the realm of eternal light (ṚV, 9.113.7). There he meets the Fathers who revel with Yama and Varuṇa in the yonder world where there is no hunger or thurst. He goes there in a divine car or on wings.

At the cremation of the dead Agni is urged to burn the corpse properly and carry the souls of the dead to sukṛtāṁ lokaḥ, (world of the pious).In the AV also, as in the ṚV, sukṛtā generally means the merit a sacrificer gains by performing the sacrificial duties correctly. For one who performs good acts, i.e., the sacrifices an entry in heaven becomes his prerogative. Performance of certain sacrifices precisely and faultlessly enables the sacrificer to ascend to heaven by fallowing the devayāna, i.e., the path of the gods {AV, 18.4.2).This luminous path of the gods is revealed to the man who performs good deeds (AV, 18.4.14).The sacrificers who performed the religious rites expected a happy post-mortem existence in heaven.

**Punishment**

As gods rewarded the pious souls by granting a permanent stay in heaven, they also punished the guilty by causing sufferings. The irregularity and inaccuracy in performing the sacrifices brought the wrath of the gods. There are earnest prayers to the concerned gods with which men want to be spared from being punished for their wrong actions. Sometimes the gods are requested to direct their anger towards the enemies. Rudra is prayed not to get angry over the faults which the worshippers might have done (ṚV, 10.15.6).

Unlike the rewards which are said to be received in heaven, punishment can be imposed on the guilty even in his present birth. This punishment came in the form of diseases and other sufferings.

Varuṇa’s displeasure is said to cause dropsy. The ṚV, 7.89.2 refers to dropsy as the disease inflicted upon by Varuṇa. In the AB, 7.13-18, Varuṇa is said to have punished king Hariścandra by making him sick with dropsy. It is significant to note here that the cause of Varuṇa 's wrath was king's failures in keeping his promise made to him. Besides, as the promise was made to Varuṇa, Hariścandra incurred Varuṇa ’s wrath also because this god is also the protector of ṛta, i.e., the moral order. By breaking his promise the king had also broken a moral rule. Mitra and Varuṇa punish the guilty by inflicting a heart disease on him (ṚV, 1.122.9).

**Concept of Hell**

As the term sukṛtāṁ lokaḥ clearly indicates a place where the virtuous go after death, it is natural that some kind of place was thought for the duṣkṛt, i.e., evil-doer, and that place was a natural compliment to heaven.

Regarding the fate of the wicked, obscure indications only are found in the ṚV. The ṚV contains a reference to a place where the evil-doers are punished. A deep place gabhīraiṁ padam is for the evil, false and untruthful (ṚV,4.5.5). Indra and Soma are urged to hurl the evil-doers into the abyss of bottomless darkness so that none of them may get out from there (ṚV, 7.104.3), and that the robbers and enemies may lie below all the three earths (ṚV, 7.104.11).Indra is prayed to consign to the lower darkness a man who injures a worshipper (ṚV, 10.152.4). These passages present an idea of the Ṛgvedic seers’ belief in the existence of a place where the wicked are punished by gods. According to H. Oldenberg gabhīraiṁ padam refers to hell.The ṚV, however, does not mention the tortures of hells as found in the later literature, but the sages of the ṚV had some faint glimmerings of a dark deep pit below earth to which the wicked persons were relegated by the gods.

According to the early Rgvedic belief it was the destiny of the wicked to fall into the dark depths and disappear.As the heaven was a permanent abode for the pious dead, this dark pit is for the sinners where they remained forever. The concept of the sins being exhausted by undergoing the prescribed punishment came much later and which, in contrast with the earlier belief, holds that man is subjected to punishments in hells and rewarded with the pleasures in heaven and after that he has to be reborn on earth. However the germs of the doctrine of karma and rebirth can be seen in 1st and 10th Maṇḍala of the ṚV.

In the idea of a dark pit for punishing the guilty, the germs can be seen of the later concept of naraka (hell) as a place for the sinners. It is to be noted here that this pit is also said to be situated below the three earths.

An early reference to man's soul, (jīvātman) attaining a higher or lower region according to his own good or evil deeds is found in the ṚV, 1.164.38. According to it the Self (ātman) is immortal but cognates with a mortal body doing various good or bad deeds and consequently moves to higher or lower regions to bear the fruit of those actions. By doing evil deeds he goes to the lower regions while the meritorious deeds take him to higher regions. These rewards or punishments granted to him are for ever. The pious, therefore, have no fear of falling down on earth after all their merits get exhausted by enjoying the pleasures of heaven. Similarly the evil-doers also do not exhaust their sins by undergoing the punishment which is, therefore, never over. Thus heaven or hell, whatever is granted once, is final and permanent.

The AV mentions naraka-loka as a place of female goblins and sorceresses. This place is said to be located in the lowest region and darkness (AV, 2.14.3), black darkness (AV, 5.30.11). It is also a place where a murderer has to go (VS, 30.5).

**Torments of Hell**

The torments of hell are described in the AV, 5.19 where it is said that those who insult a brahmin or take away his money have to sit in the stream of blood and eat hair (AV, 5 19.3) It is further said that if people injure a brahmin their country falls into calamities and sinks down like a broken boat in water (AV, 5.19.8).

In the ŚB, 11.6.1 a reference to the torments of hell is found. According to it and JB. 1.42.44, Bhṛgu thought very high of himself. He considered himself to be more learned than his father, Varuṇa.

When Varuṇa came to know this he asked Bhṛgu to go in each direction one by one, see whatever he finds there and report everything to Varuṇa. Thus, due to his pride, Bhṛgu was sent to see these directions. He saw there horrible scenes of torture. He saw men being cut into pieces and being eaten by others. Horrified Bhṛgu asked them as to why they were doing this gruesome act of cutting a human being and eating him up. The answer he was given here is significant. They said that this man had done the same to them during his earthly life. Now, they, the victims, settle the score by cutting him into pieces. Bhṛgu also saw the lord of that place as a yellow-eyed and black-complexioned man holding a staff.beside two maidens — one of them fair and the other described as atikalyāṇī, i.e., either 'very fair' or 'past her beauty'.

Another picture of hell is found in the KausBr. 11.2 where a man is, in the next world, eaten up by the animals which he had devoured during his life. Certain rituals are also suggested to save one from getting into such predicament.

The idea of reward or punishment awarded according to one's own deeds is found in ŚB, 6.2.2.27; 10.6.3.1. The ŚB, 1.9.3 states that the dead pass between two fires which bum the evil-doers but allow the virtuous go by. Moreover all the actions of a dead are weighed on a balance and he receives award or punishment according to his good or evil deeds (ŚB.11.2.7.33). It is to be noted here that in the Brāhmaṇic thought the good deeds were more often of the nature of sacrificial duties than ordinary good-work. One who reads the Veda in a particular way is said to be freed from dying again and attains oneness with Brahman (ŚB, 10.5.6.9). If the sacrifice is performed properly, it secures for the sacrificer a place in heaven (ŚB, 11.4.4.12).

The TA, 1.19 refers to four hells in four directions. They are:

- (1) Visarpin in the south-east;
- (2) Avisarpin in the south-west;
- (3) Viṣādi in the north-east;
- (4) Viṣādi in the north-west.

People fall in these hells as the result of their own bad actions. In the Viṣādi hell people are tormented by dejection due to repentence. Visarpi is the hell where people move here and there walking under tremendous pain, while Avisarpi is a hell where the dead are unable to move at all and in Aviṣādi the people live always under fear. The KatḥaUp, mentions the pleasureless regions which Naciketas fears that his father would get for giving away useless cows as gifts. The KatḥaUp says nothing about the torments of the hells.

The BṛUp, 4.4.11 speaks about an extremely dark region where there is no happiness. People who are ignorant and unenlightened go to these regions after their death.On the other hand the BṛUp also tells about a man attaining the merger of his soul in Brahman. The BṛUp, 4.9.9.7 says that when a man becomes free from all desires, he, though a mortal, becomes immortal and obtains Brahman. It further says that the vital air of a man with all his desires centered only in the Self, do not depart and such a man being Brahman (while he lives) goes to Brahman after his death and becomes pure light. The BṛUp, 4.4.11 and IśaUp, 3 both tell about a dark region where there is no sun-light. Demonic (asuryā)are the regions, covered with darkness where those who have destroyed their souls are obliged to go (IśaUp. 3).

Taking into account these scattered but clear references to a dark, pleasureless place are sufficient to conclude that the Vedic people entertained the idea of the existence of a place like hell where the guilty were punished after death. However, the lack of detailed descriptions of hell, as seen in the Purāṇas. indicate that these ideas developed extensively only in the time of the Epics and the Purāṇas.

**Transmigration**

In the major part of the ṚV the idea of the transmigration is conspicuously absent. The Vedic Aryans did not give much thought to death or the existence of man after death. The ṚV hymns are primarily prayers to the different gods and reference to death and post-mortem existence is only casual. Even then some glimmering of the Vedic seers’ thoughts on transmigration can be seen in the ṚV also.

It is often said that the idea of transmigration is of a very late origin and it did not exist in the time of the ṚV. It is also said that it was an un-Aryan belief.RD. Ranade strongly refutes the idea of A.A. Macdonell that the Aryan settlers might have received the first impulse in this direction from the aboriginal inhabitants of India, which later developed into a theory of chain of existences intimately connected with the moral principal of requital.

Though the idea of transmigration is not very explicit in the greater part of the ṚV, but that an approach is being made is not hard to discover. According to the Ṛgvedic belief death was not the ultimate end of one’s existence and that the living beings continue to exist even after their carnal death. The worshippers wish to enjoy a life of one hundred years and after the fall of their bodies want to become immortal in the realm of Yama. This confirms their belief in the continued existence of man even after his death.

The first stage in the evolution of the Ṛgvedic idea of transmigration consists in taking an animistic or hylozoistic view of the world. At the cremation of the dead his eye is asked to go to the sun, his vital breath to the wind, the spirit has been directed to go to heaven or earth in accordance to its dharma (merits),or even to move to waters or trees as it suited it (ṚV, 10.16.3). The word dharma in this stanza is very significant because the soul is asked to go to heaven or to earth according to its merit. This confirms that this stanza is definitely expressing transmigration and not just putting forth certain hints towards the animistic or hylozoistic view of the world. There is one more passage definitely connected with the idea of transmigration. In ṚV, 10.58.1-12 the poet says that he is going to recall the departed soul (of an apparently dead-man) so that the dead may get back his soul and continue to live. The departed soul is said to have gone to distant places. The seer calls back this soul which, he says, might have gone to heaven or earth, to the four- cornered globe, which may have diffused in the various quarters, which may have gone to the oceans or the beams of light, to waters or herbs, to the sun or to the dawn, to the mountains or which may have spread through the whole world. This hymn surely suggests that the soul does not perish along with the body in which it existed. It can separate itself from that body because it has an independent existence.

Sufficient evidence as to prove the existence of the idea of transmigration in the ṚV is also provided by the ṚV, 1.164.30 and 1.164.38. According to Roth, Bṭhtlingk and Geldner also these stanzas contain the early thoughts on transmigration.They tell that the soul is a moving, speedful life-principle which comes and goes, moves backward and forward, comes in the contact with the body and then moves from it in the opposite direction.
