The culminating point of this doctrine is reached in the ṚV, 1.164.31 where poet claims to have seen (probably with his mind's eyes) the gopā, i.e., the guardian of the body moving unerringly by forward and backward paths, clothed in collected and diffusive splendour and it kept on "returning frequently inside the mundane region" \[varīvarte bhuvaneṣu antarā iti — ṚV, 1.164.31). This interpretation leaves no place for doubt that it is referring to the repeated transmigrations of the soul. To support his point that gopā i.e., ‘guardian’ mentioned in this stanza is the 'soul', R.D. Ranade refers to the preceding stanza, (i.e., 1.164.30) and comments that gopā in the ṚV, 1.164.31 is no other than the soul may be seen from the manner in which the previous stanza mentions the “breathing, speedful, moving life-principle”. Moreover the frequentative varīvartī tells about the soul's frequent returns to the world.

It is important to note here that Sāyaṇa takes gopā to be the sun whose reappearance every morning is here expressed as his frequent visits. In this sense the frequentative varīvartī denotes the frequent (here daily) appearance of the sun in the morning, and not of the rebirth of the soul.

On the basis of this reference it can be said that the ideas of transmigration are implicitly found in the ṚV. They mention the passage of the soul from the body, souls inhabiting various forms of existence like water and plants and also the soul acquiring several births and thus present the early thought on transmigration as contained in the ṚV.

The ṚV, 10.16.6 states that after his death man gets a new body which is free from its previous imperfections. According to the ṚV, 10.14.8 the dead takes up a new luminous body. Following Sāyaṇa’s interpretation this stanza can be seen as suggesting the rebirth of a dead by saying: “leave your imperfections and come back again with a new shining body”.

The soul of the dead Is here asked to return to its chosen home “when the soul of the dead returns, he is said to go back home, as it were returning to his true home”.

H.D. Velankar interprets the term asta figuring in the ṚV, 7.12.10 as 'in the home of man'; here the gods have sent Agni to the asta for men's protection, whereas Agni’s home is called dama.Thus the ṚV, 10.14.8 does not suggest the rebirth of the dead.

On the basis of these references found in the first and tenth Mandalas of the ṚV it can be derived that the ideas of transmigration are implicitly found in the ṚV. This ultimately proves that the idea of transmigration existed in the Vedic thought and it was not borrowed from the non-Aryan aboriginals of India. As R.D. Ranade puts: "whenever there is recognised the possibility of the soul coming to inhabit a body as a godlike principle from without, wherever, it is supposed that they could likewise part from the body, wherever it is thought that the soul after parting from the body could lead a life of disembodied existence, and wherever, it is supposed to return again to the earth and inhabit any form of existence, there is a kind of undying life conceived for the soul from which the step to actual transmigration is not far removed.”

**Transmigration and Karma**

Recurrent births are mentioned at several places in the Brāhmaṇas. It is said that one who lacks in the knowledge of a sacrifice and performs it in a wrong way, i.e., making faulty fire-altar, etc., is born again and becomes the food of death again. On the other hand, one who performs sacrifices with the correct knowledge gains immortality (ŚB, 10.4.3.10). Pin-pointing the possible error which an ignorant or negligent sacrificer commits the ŚB, 10.2.6.19 and 10.5.1.4) states that the recurrent death is the destiny of man who does not prepare the fire-altar in a correct way. Here in the reply to a question as to who conquers the recurrent birth it is said that those who make the altar correctly become the deity Agni and as Agni, i.e., the Fire-element is immortal, he too becomes immortal.

These and other such statements suggest that only the defaulters in performing sacrifices are born again in this world in order to suffer from recurrent deaths, while others who perform it correctly become immortal after death.

According to another Brāhmaṇic view all are born again after death to recompense for their good or bad actions. The type of their next birth is based on the type of actions performed by a man during his life. It is said that after his death a man is born in the world which he has worked for (ŚB, 6.2.2.27).Thus man (or the personal principle in him) is born into the existence he made for himself.

According to the Brāhmaṇic transmigration theory the karman (i.e., good or bad actions of man) result in happiness or sufferings in his earthly life as well as in the hereafter life. The performance of the religious rites according to the prescribed norms and the knowledge of their import brings for the sacrificer an abundance of food in this life and imperishable sukṛt in the yonder world (ŚB, 1.6.4.16). At another instance the reward includes good eyesight till old age in this world and in the beyond (ŚB, 1.6.3.41).

In the ŚB every man is said to have three births, i.e., a man is born thrice (ŚB, 11.2.1.1). His first birth is when he is born as a child of his parents, the second by spiritual education when he begins performing sacrifices and the third after his death. The passage under reference is translated as follows:

Verily, man is born thrice, namely in this way: first he is born from his mother and father, then when to whom the sacrifice inclines performs offering he is born a second time; and when he dies, and they place him on the fire, and when he thereupon comes into existence he is born a third time.

According to the ŚB, 1.5.3.14 the rebirth in this world is a reward for some special knowledge. It is, therefore, looked upon as a blessing and not something wished to be spared from. The same text states that as the spring assuredly comes Into life again out of winter, for out of one other is born again; therefore, he who knows this is indeed born again in this world.

**Ritual Rebirth and Death**

At more than one place in the Brāhmaṇas there are statements about a sacrificer reaching heaven even without dying. Such statements suggest that svarga-loka or ‘heaven’ was not or not always, or not explicitly identified with the abode of the blessed dead, and therefore it would be an error to read the statements about svarga along with an un-expressed 'after death'.The earliest reference to this notion is seen in the TS, 6.9.9.2 that a man, by performing certain rites can reach heaven without dying. The PB, 21.4.3 states that the performer of the Aśvamedha reaches the yonder world with his body (saśarīraḥ) and elsewhere (PB, 11.8.14) it tells about a definite Sāman for 'beholding the world of heaven’. That this ritual enables a sacrificer to see the heaven straightaway (añjasā). The inference that the sacrificer died at once to go to the abode of the dead is not probable.

According to the ŚB, 7.3.1.12 the officiant priest causes the sacrificer to be reborn in the svarga-loka \[i.e., heaven); but he does not cause his sudden death or his post-mortem transference to heaven. In the ŚB it is said that Mṛtyu was assured by Brahman that all will have to pass through death in order to attain heaven. In this light, the rebirth without having to die indicates the ritual-rebirth of the sacrificer and the world which is said to be gained by singing certain hymn is certainly the ritual heaven (AB, 3.19.4; cf. AB, 6.4.11; 6.20.2).

In the Upaniṣads the doctrine of transmigration is seen well established. The idea of recompense in the other world is also combined with the doctrine of rebirth.

The BṛUp, 5.10.1 can be considered to contain the oldest Upaniṣadic thought on eschatology. According to it a soul, after death, ascends through the regions of wind, the sun, the moon and then reaches a region which is sorrowless and snowless (ahiman) and lives there for ever.The passage however, does not tell whether this is the fate of all the souls or of the virtuous souls only. No such distinctions are made as virtuous and sinful.

The ChāUp. (5.10.3.6) speaks about two types of fate for the dead. The two paths, i.e., the devayāna and the pitṛyāṇa also called arci-mārga and dhūma-mārga respectively are also described. It is believed that those who perform charitable deeds follow the pitṛyāṇa after their death. On this path the soul enters successively into smoke, night, dark fortnight, six months when the sun has southern inclination, the pitṛ-loka (region of the Fathers), sky and from sky to the moon. After having stayed in the moon for a specific length of time, i.e., till the effect of his actions lasts, the soul undertakes the return-journey. Heading towards the earth the soul moves from moon to sky from sky to wind, from wind it becomes smoke from smoke the cloud is formed and from that it becomes the rain cloud. The rain produces rice, corn, etc. Those, who eat these grains, procreate and the dead is thus reborn on the earth as the soul takes up a new body.

The other path is the devayāna or the path of the gods. The final destination of this path is Brahman. Those, who cultivate faith and penance enter, successively, after their death, in the regions of light, day, bright fortnight, six months when the sun has northern inclination. From there the soul enters the region of year (saṁvatsara), sun, moon and the light and ultimately enters Brahman, never to return (ChāUp. 5.10.1-2).

Thus it is clear that in contrast with the devayāna the pitṛyāṇa is a path to rebirth. Those who take up pitṛyāṇa are finally reborn on earth.

In the KatḥaUp (1.1.6) the fate of man is described as a cornfield, where the crop is grown and cut again and again. In a similar way man also takes birth and dies again and again. The germ of the theory of karman which later developed fully in the Purāṇas can be seen in the ChāUp, 5.10.7. According to it the next life of man as well as his fate and circumstances in that life solely depend upon his good or evil actions performed during the present life. The ChāUp, 5.10.7 remarks that those whose conduct has been good will attain some good birth, i.e., the birth of a brahmin, a kṣatriya, a vaiśya, but those whose conduct has been evil get a low birth, i.e., the birth of a dog, a hog, a cāṇḍāla (a low-caste).

In the KauUp, 1.2 it is clearly stated that the soul gets different bodies in accordance to his karman or knowledge. A variety of bodies are mentioned here which a soul may get. They are a worm or a moth, a fish or a bird, a leopard or a lion, a serpent or a man or any of the other creatures.

The BṛUp describes in details the manner in which a man dies and is reborn (BṛUp, 4.3.37-38; 4.4.1 -2). It also says that the knowledge, the works and the former consciousness of the dead follow him after his death.

The manner in which the soul leaves the present body and takes up another is very explicitly related with the example of a caterpillar. When a caterpillar moving on a blade of grass reaches the end of it, it looks for another support, and then draws itself towards this new support. Such is the transition of soul from one body to another. Giving another example the text states that as a goldsmith takes a piece of gold and out of that he frames another form which is newer and more beautiful. In a similar way the Self after having thrown off this body and dispelled ignorance, takes on another newer and more beautiful form. The law of karman is also seen here. BṛUp, 4.4 further says that the soul gets a holy body if his actions have been good and a sinful body if his actions have been bad.Ultimately it can be said that the germs of transmigration which are found in the ṚV have developed fully in the Upaniṣads.

## Part II Yama in the Purāṇas

## 6. Parentage, Abode, Companions and Messengers

The Epics and the Purāṇas constitute a link between the Vedic and the classical literature. The Epic literature comprises of Rāmāyaṇa (the Ādi-kāvya) and Mahābhārata. Eighteen Mahā-Purāṇas and several Upa-Purāṇas constitute the Purāṇic literature. Unlike the Vedas they could be read by common people which has added to their popularity. The importance of Purāṇic literature can never be over-estimated. In fact the Purāṇas “occupy a unique position In the sacred and secular literature of the Hindus, their importance being regarded as next to the Vedas only".The Mbh and the Purāṇas are also regarded as the fifth Veda.

The study of Epics and Purāṇas has been stressed as necessary for the interpretation of the Vedas. Apart from forming a link between the Vedic and the classical literature, they are also an important source of the cultural history of India. They are useful for a historical study of Indian society in general as well as for comparative studies in the field of religion, philosophy and mythology. The epics and the Purāṇas particularly serve as the chief source of information about the Indian mythology. Many a legend, already familiar from the Ṛgvedic hymns and from the Brāhmaṇas reappear in the Purāṇas.The stories/legends which are nothing more than rudimentary sketches get a better deal in the Purāṇas where they are narrated in great details. Some stories appear in more than one Purāṇa and In a Purāṇa devoted to the praise of a particular deity, the same myths are shown to highlight the supremacy or benevolence of that god. Numerous classical poets and dramatists have found in the Epics and the Purāṇas, the themes and plots for their literary works. Purāṇas are extremely valuable for the history of philosophy and religion. “Taken collectively they may be described as popular encyclopedia of ancient and medieval Hinduism religious, philosophical, historical, personal, social and political.’’

Yāska gives the etymology of the vocable Purana as purā navaṁ bhavati, i.e., that which is always new in spite of being old (Nir, 3.19). According to the VāyuP, 1.203, again on the basis of its etymology, the term Purāṇa means “that which lives from ancient times".The MatsyaP describes the Purāṇas as containing record of the past events.“Originally the term Purāṇa signified ‘ancient tales’ or ‘old narratives’ in the Brāhmaṇa literature, as compared with Itihāsa or Nārāsaṁsa before it became associated with Purāṇas as a class of literature.

**Date of the Purāṇas**

It is very difficult to determine the exact date of the Purāṇas. They “undoubtedly reach back to great antiquity and are rooted in Vedic literature.”Though in the AV there are references to the Purāṇas as independent form of literature it cannot be said that the term Purāṇa meant actual books at the time of the AV.The ChāUp however, indicates that the term Purāṇa denoted a particular work which is mentioned along with the four Vedas (ṚV, YV, SV and AV) and it is called the ‘fifth Veda!.

Most of the Purāṇas consist of portions which were written at different period of time. While dealing with the age of the Purāṇas, therefore, the date of the earliest portion should be considered and not the latest. The earlier versions of the Purāṇas existed even at the time of the Mbh, while the extant Purāṇas, however, can be assigned to the early centuries of the Christian era on the testimony of epigraphy and literature.As noted earlier “it is no doubt true that the Purāṇas as they exist cannot be assigned to any particular age, even the component parts of individual Purāṇas belonging to different periods, as the process of inflation, omission contamination, etc. have been continuously going on.”

According to the popular belief all the Purāṇas were the work of the great sage Vedavyāsa, the arranger of the Vedas and the author of the Mbh.

**Characteristics of Purāṇas**

The characteristics of the Purāṇas have been mentioned in several Purāṇas where they are defined as pañcalakṣaṇa.These five characteristics are sarga (creation), pratisarga (dissolution and recreation), vaṁśa (divine genealogy), manvantara (the ages of Manu) and vaṁśānucaritam (genealogy of kings). These characteristics indicate the real nature of the Purāṇas in their early, if not the earliest, form. Most of the Purāṇas contain much more than these topics, while some scarcely deal with those mentioned in it. Since the Pañcalakṣaṇa occupied a very insignificant part in the bulk of a Purāṇa, “It appears that the religious instructions was not one of their primary aims”.The dāna (gifts), vratas (religious observance) tīrthas (sacred places), śrāddha (rites in honour of the Manes) etc. constitute a major portion of the extant Purāṇas rendering the pañcalakṣaṇa definition only a theoretical definition.

**Number of Purāṇas**

There are 18 Mahā-Purāṇas and 18 Upa-Purāṇas according to the traditional view. The names of these 18 Purāṇas are enumerated in many Purāṇas. RC. Hazra divided these 18 Purāṇas in two categories (i) Major, (ii) Minor. He takes seven Purāṇas, i.e., the MāP, VāyuP, Brahmāṇda P, ViP, MP. Bhāg. P, KūrmaP as major Purāṇas and the other eleven (i.e., VāmanP, LiṅgaP, VarāhaP, PP, Nāradīya, Agni, Garuḍa, Brahma, Skanda, BVP and Bhaviṣya) as the minor Purāṇas.

In the ViṣṇuP, 3.6.20.29 and BhāgP, 12.13.3-8, the eighteen Mahā-Purāṇas are mentioned in a particular order which is maintained in the other Purāṇas also. They are Brahma, Padma, Viṣṇu, Śiva, Bhāg, Nāradīya, Mārk, Agni, Bhaviṣya Brahma-Vaivarta, Liṅga. Varāha, Skanda, Vāmana, Kūrma, Matsya, Garuḍa and Brahmāṇḍa. In some places the ŚivaP is substituted by VāyuP.

There is no uniformity in the enumeration of the Upa- Purāṇas. They are more sectarian in character, comparatively late and of composite nature. They are Sanatkumāra, Narasiṁha, Nanda, Śivadharma, Durvāsā, Naradīya, Kapila, Vāmana, Uśanas, Mānava, Varuṇa, Kāli, Maheśvara, Śāmba, Saura Parāśara, Marica and Bhārgava.

**Classification**

The Purāṇas are classified into ancient and later on the basis of their conformity to the Pañcalakṣaṇa definition. Less the number of addition which are foreign to the nature of Purāṇas, older is the Purāṇa On this basis Vāyu, Brahmāṇda, Matsya and Viṣṇu are considered ancient Purāṇas.

The Purāṇas are also divided on the basis of their preferential treatment to the particular deities. The PP, 163.81.84 divides them, evidently on the Vaiṣṇava stand-point, as Sāttvika, Rājasika, and Tāmasika Purāṇas —

- (1) Sāttvika — ViṣṇuP, Naradīya, Bhāg, GP, PP, and VarāhaP.
- (2) Tāmasika—MP, Kūrma, Liṅga, Śiva, Agni and Skanda
- (3) Rājasika — Brahmāṇḍa, Brahma-Vaivarta, MāP, BrahmaP, VāmanaP and BhauiṣyaP.

Basing his division on later Tamil works, Dikshitar classifies the Purāṇas into five groups.

Deity Name of the Purāṇa   Brahmā BrahmaP and PP   Sūrya Brahma-VaivartaP   Agni AgniP   Śiva Śiva, SkandaP, LiṅgaP, KūrmaP, VāmanaP, VarāhaP, BhavṣyaP, MP, MārkaṇḍeyaP, BrahmāṇḍaP   Viṣṇu NaradaP, BhāgP, GP and ViP

On the basis of the contents of the Purāṇas, they are divided in six groups. This classification is attempted by Haraprasad Shastri who divided them in the following six groups:

- (1) The Purāṇas in the first group are encyclopaedia of literature. They deal in addition to the usual Purāṇic material, with medicine, grammar, dramaturgy, music, astrology etc. GP, AgniP, and NaradīyaP fall in this category.
- (2) The second group deals mainly with tīrthas and vratas. These Purāṇas have undergone drastic changes and revision resulting in their original matter being lost e.g., SkandaP, BrahmaP.
- (3) The third group has BrahmaP, BhāgP and BrVaiP. These Purāṇas have undergone revision and the additional matter is added at both the ends of the original matter.
- (4) The fourth group, this group is called the historical group. BrahmāṇḍaP and VarāhaP fall in this category.
- (5) Sectarian works form the fifth group which consists of LiṅgaP, VāmanaP and MāP.
- (6) Purāṇas of the sixth group had been revised out of existence. They include Varāha, Kūrma and Matsya

The study of the post-Vedic literature has its own importance from the point of view of comparative mythology and the Purāṇas are said to be a store-house of religion and mythology. In the Purāṇas some Vedic diety have gained importance while some lost their prominence or even relegated to oblivion. Yama is one of those Vedic deities whose personality and character have undergone tremendous change in the Purāṇic pantheon and Purāṇic Hindu religion.

**Yama's Parentage**

The account of Yama’s parentage is found in many Purāṇas viz. ViP, MP, VāP, MārkP, LiṅgaP, BP. Its different versions are as follows.

**Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa Version**

According to the MārkP chapter 74, Viśvakarman’s daughter Saṁjñā was the wife of Mārtaṇḍa (also mentioned as Sūrya Vivasvat).After the birth of Manu, Saṁjñā found it difficult to bear the radiance of Vivasvat and once he saw her as she closed her eyes on seeing him. He became infuriated and pronounced a curse on Saṁjñā, “you control your eyes from me. O fool! so may you give birth to Yama, the controller of mankind! "He further cursed her to bear a river as a daughter \[MārkP, 74.4). After the birth of Yama and Yamunā it became impossible for Saṁjñā to bear the lustre of the Sun any more. She asked her own shadow, i.e., Chāyā to take her place and care for her children. Saṁjñā told Chāyā to act as Sūrya’s wife and never to disclose her identity to anybody.

Chāyā agreed to do so but clarified that if Sūrya became angry and was about to curse her, she would disclose her identity. Agreeing to this Saṁjñā left her father’s house leaving Chāyā behind to look after her home and children. Sūrya could not know this change. Three children— Sāvarṇi Manu, Śani and Tapatī were born to Chāyā. Chāyā started giving more attention and love to her own children than to those of Saṁjñā. Manu tolerated her behaviour but Yama could not and on one occasion he lifted his leg to strike her. Though he controlled himself in time and did not hit her, Chāyā was furious. She cursed Yama that as he had lifted his leg for hitting her, his leg would be eaten up by worms. A worried Yama went to Vivasvat and said, “It is surprising that a mother should curse her son. As Manu has said before, “this lady indeed is not our mother, because even if a son is wicked, the mother is never cruel to him” \[MarkP, 74.32).

When questioned by Sūrya, Chāyā maintained that she was Saṁjñā, the daughter of Tvaṣṭṛ, wife of Sūrya and mother of his children. When she did not tell the truth and repeated the same statement, Sūrya got angry and was about to pronounce a curse. At this, Chāyā disclosed the truth and said that Saṁjñā has left for her father’s place. Sūrya went to Viśvakarman and learnt from him that Saṁjñā had stayed there for some time but had left when she was told that it was not morally good for a married woman to stay at her father's place for long and therefore, she must return to her husband’s house. Sūrya through meditation saw that Saṁjñā, in the form of a mare, was practising penance in Uttara-Kuru.

He also knew the reason for her distress and the aim of her penance. She wanted to make his lustre bearable. Sūrya requested Viśvakarman to reduce his dazzle and make it bearable for others. Viśvakarman reduced Sun’s lustre to one-eighth. Out of the pared off lustre of Sūrya he made the trident of Śiva, discuss of Visnu, Śakti of Agni and many other weapons (MarkP, 75.17-18). With his diminished lustre Sūrya assumed the form of a horse and went to the Uttara-Kuru region and found Saṁjñā in the form of a mare. She could not recognise her husband. On meeting, their noses united and the mare gave birth to two sons through her nose. Another son Revanta was also born to them after some time.

Vivasvat lessened the severity of the curse to Yama by adding that when the worms from his foot would fall on the earth, it would be alright again. Seeing Yama as possessing a righteous visionand equal bearing towards friends and foes, Sūrya also granted him the authority of the controller (MarkP. 75.28; also 105.16-18).

**Vāyu Purāṇa Version**

According to the Vāp, 84.24, the daughter of Viśvakarman was Sareṇu. She was married to Vivasvat and then she was named Saṁjñā. The further story has no significant difference from the other versions. But here Śrutaśravas and Śrutakarman are mentioned as two sons of Chāyā who elsewhere are named as Sāvarṇi Manu, Śani respectively. When Yama got angry due to the ill-treatment meted out by Chāyā and was about to kick her, Chāyā cursed that Yama would lose his leg (VaP. 84.56-61).

**Viṣṇu Puraṇa Version**

In the ViP, 3.2.1 ff. Pārāsara, on being inquired by Maitreya about the forthcoming Marvantaras. states the story of Vivasvat's progeny. According to it Saṁjñā, the daughter of Viśvakarman, was the wife of Sūrya who bore him three children — viz. Manu, Yama and Yamunā. Unable to bear the dazzling lustre of Sūrya any more, Saṁjñā placed her shadow (chāyā)in the service of her husband Sūrya and repaired to the forest for practising penance. Due to the similarity of Chāyā 's appearance with that of Saṁjñā, Sūrya took her as Saṁjñā and begot in her three more children—viz. Śanaiścara, Sāvarṇi Manu and Tapati. On one occasion Chāyā got angry with Yama and pronounced an imprecation upon him and thereby revealed to him and Sūrya that she was not Saṁjñā, the real mother of the former.On being further informed by Chāyā that Saṁjñā had gone to the wilderness. Sūrya by meditation beheld her engaged in austerities in the form of a mare in the Uttara-Kuru region. Metamorphosing himself into a horse, he rejoined Saṁjñā and begot three other children — the two Aśvins and Revanta. He then brought Saṁjñā back to his house. To diminish the intensity of his lustre Viśvakarman placed Sūrya on his lathe to grind off some of his (i.e.. Sūrya's) effulgence, and in this manner he reduced it by one-eighth, since more than that was inseparable.From the filed off parts of Sūrya he fashioned the discus of Viṣṇu. trident of Śiva, the lance of Kārthikeya and other weapons.

Yama was the fifth descendant of Mahāvisnu. From Mahāvisnu descended, in the following order, Brahmā, Marīci, Kāśyapa, Sūrya, Yama (ViP, 1.13).

**Matsya Purāṇa Version**

According to the MP, 11.2-3 Vivasvat had three wives (1) Rājñī by whom he had a son named Revanta, (2) Prabhā —by whom he had Prabhāta. (3) Saṁjñā, the daughter of Tvaṣṭṛ by whom he had Manu, Yama and Yamunā. It also says that the latter two were twins.When Saṁjñā found herself unable to bear the dazzle of Sūrya she produced a lady from her own body who resembled her. Saṁjñā asked this lady named Chāyā, to serve her husband and take motherly care of her children, and went away.In due course Chāyā gave birth to Sāvarṇi Manu,Śani, Tapati and Viṣṭi. Chāyā was partial towards her own children and showered all her love on them. Manu endured this unfair treatment but Yama could not. One day he threatened her by raising his leg. At this Chāyā cursed that his leg would be eaten up by worms and would suffer from wounds emissing pus and blood. Yama got angry and reported this Incident to his father, i.e.. Vivasvat (MP, 11.13). Yama said that he had been cursed by the mother for no fault of his. In a childish freak he lifted up his foot at her at which in spite of the entreaties of Manu she pronounced a course on him. Yama further said that such an altitude of Chāyā showed that she was not his real mother. Vivasvat listened to Yama and expressed his inability to do anything in this matter. He further said that one has to bear the fruit of one's folly and also of the deeds done in the previous births and that even Lord Śiva had to reap the fruit of his action. Thus, Sun expressed his inability to do away with the effect of a mother's curseeven when Yama told him that she could not be his real mother. He however, gave him a cock who would eat up all the worms and clean the impurities of the wound.

Thereafter Sūrya frightened Chāyā and made her disclose her identity. Chāyā narrated what had happened and also that Saṁjñā had gone to her father’s house. Yama, on the other hand, after getting the effect of the curse minimized went to Gokarṇa-tīrtha where he performed penance for several years. By the grace of Śiva he got the overlordship of the Fathers.He also gained the faculty of distinguishing the good and bad deads of man (MP, 11.18).

When Saṁjñā reached her father's house, he reprimanded her for running away from her husband’s house. She was denied admittance in the house. From there Saṁjñā went to the Marudeśa where she roamed in the form of a mare (MP, 11.26). Viśvakarman reported this to Sun when the latter approached him to inquire about Saṁjñā. Viśvakarman offered to reduce Sūrya’s lustre and make him bearable. With his permission, Viśvakarman sliced off, by his instrument Bhrami (the wheel), some portion of Sūrya’s unbearable lustre. After getting his lustre reduced by Viśvakarman, Sūrya went out in the form of a horse and enjoyed himself in the company of Saṁjñā, who not recognising him as her husband was terrified but helpless. She ejected the semen virile through her nostrils out of which the two Aśvinīkumāras, also called Nātsatya and Dasra, were born Afterwards Saṁjñā recognised Sūrya and went with him.

**Liṅga Purāṇa Version**

The LP (1.65.10) provides similar story as that in the MP. After being cursed by Chāyā Yama went to Gokarṇa region and there he practised penance for thousands of years. At last, by the grace of Lord Śiva he became a guardian of the region (lokapala) and the overlord of the Fathers. Being a Śaivite Purāṇa the LP highlights the glory of Lord Śiva who freed Yama from the terrible curse too. Sūrya, on knowing that the woman whom he took as Saṁjñā was not Saṁjñā but Chāyā, set out to look for Saṁjñā. However, how he could know this is not given in the LP version. Sūrya found her in the form of a mare. He himself took the form of a horse and Aśvinīkumāras were born to them. Viśvakarman pared off one-eighth part of Sūrya’s lustre to make it bearable.

**Brahma Vaivarta Purāṇa Version**

The BVP, 1.47.12 relates that while the Sūrya called Mārtaṇḍa was yet in the cosmic egg, Dakṣa gave him his beautiful daughter Arūpā, as wife.Yama and Yamī were born to her. Arūpā was dazzled by Sūrya’s brilliance. Even her golden complexion turned dark due to Sūrya’s rays. Deeply dejected she left her shadow, i.e., Chāyā in the house and went to the Northern Kuru for practising penance. She lived there in the form of a mare. Sūrya got two children viz. Sani and Tapatī by Chāyā. Once Yamī and Tapatī quarrelled and turned into rivers by mutual curse. Yama was beaten severely by Chāyā and he angrily lifted his foot (for hitting her). Enraged at this Chāyā cursed, “You, fool! have raised your leg towards me; therefore, as long as there will be the sun and the moon, the gruesome task of taking away lives of creatures will undoubtedly be yours. If you place your foot on earth, worms will consume it polluted as it is with my curse." Meanwhile Sūrya came there and Yama intimated Chāyā ’s unfair treatment and added that she was only a 'Chāyā ', i.e., 'shadow'-image of his mother. Yama also informed him the unhappy incident concerning Yamunā and Tapati. In order to reduce the severity of the curse Sūrya said that the river Yamunā would be considered as holy as the Gaṅgā into which she would ultimately flow. Tapatī would be considered equal to the Narmadā in respect of sanctity. He also said that Yama would become a lokapāla and that Chāyā would thenceforth live in her own body.

**Varāha Purāṇa Version**

According to the VP version Yama and Yamunā were children of Vivasvat whom he got as a boon from Lord Viṣṇu. In the VP, 137.281. Varāha tells Vasundharā about the sanctity of the Vaivasvata- tīrtha where Vivasvat (Āditya) himself practised penance. Vivasvat was desirous of having a son by the grace of Lord Keśava. He practised severe austerities for thousands of years with the desire of getting a son. At last the Lord was pleased and asked Vivasvat about his desire which should be fulfilled. Vivasvat replied that he wanted a son by Lord's grace (137.285). The Lord granted this and said that Yama and Yamunā would be born to him (137.287).

**Discussion**

Common Features of the Story  
Saṁjñā, the daughter of Viśvakarman

Sūrya'swife was the daughter of Viśvakarman.In most of the Purāṇas her name is given as Saṁjñā (ViP, MāP, MP, LP). She bore three children; Manu, Yama and Yamunā of which the latter two were twins.

**Saṁjñā's Departure**

Unable to bear Sūrya’s radiance any longer, she fashioned a woman from her body who was exactly like herself. This woman was named ‘Chāyā ’. Saṁjñā asked Chāyā to take care of her husband and children without disclosing her real identity. After this Saṁjñā left for her father’s place in the form of a mare. Here it may be presumed that she transformed herself into a mare in order to be able to run away fast from the house before her husband Sūrya could know about her plan.

**Saṁjñā 's Penance**

Saṁjñā went to her father’s place first. From there she, in the form of a mare, proceeded to the Uttara-Kuru region for practising penance.

**Chāyā’s Children**

Saṁjñā had left the house after engaging her substitute there. This substitute was ’Chāyā ’, literally, her own ‘shadow’. Sūrya could not discern this replacement and from Chāyā he begot three children —Sāvarṇi Manu, Śani and Tapatī.

**Chāyā’s Curse**

After the birth of her own children Chāyā ill-treated Saṁjñā’s children. On one occasion Yama could not endure her behaviour and lifted his leg in order to strike her. This made Chāyā furious and she pronounced a curse upon Yama that he would lose his leg.

**Father's Reaction**

On being cursed by Chāyā Yama was very unhappy. He reported this incident to Sūrya. Sūrya expressed his inability to do away with the curse of a mother (it is noteworthy that in this case she is not even Yama's real mother), but minimised the effect of her curse.

**The Reunion**

Sūrya forced Chāyā to disclose the whereabouts of Saṁjñā and he then set out to look for her. Finding her in the form of a mare, Sūrya transformed himself into a horse. A pair of twins, i.e., the two Aśvīnīkumāras and Revanta were born to them.

The different versions of this story in different Purāṇas are very similar in their details; with some points of dissimilarity.

**Points of Dissimilarity in Different Versions**

While most of the versions record the name of Sūrya's wife as Saṁjñā,the VāP provides that the daughter of Viśvakarman was Sareṇu and she was called Saṁjñā after her marriage to Vivasvat. It is significant to note that this name, i.e., Sareṇu is very near to her Vedic name Saraṇyū. It is a possible development, having undergone a phonetic change, from the Vedic word ’ Saraṇyū’, (‘a' becoming ‘e’ here and ‘y’ being dropped. This change may be ascribed to a tendency for easy pronunciation). The BVP gives the name of Sūrya’s wife as Arūpā.

**Children of Saṁjñā**

Manu was the eldest son of Saṁjñā and Sūrya.The two other children were Yama and Yamunā and they were twins. According to the VāyuP, Sūrya in a fit of anger cursed Saṁjñā that she would give birth to the ‘controller of mankind’, and also that she would bear a river as a daughter. The birth of Yama and Yamunā followed these curses.

**Saṁjñā Secret Department**

Leaving Chāyā in her place, Saṁjñā left her husband’s house. According to the MāP she went to her father, i.e.. Viśvakarman’s house and stayed there for many days. Then Viśvakarman advised her to return to her husband’s house, since it was against the dharma of a married woman to stay at her relatives’ house for a long time. At this Saṁjñā left her father’s house. Reluctant to return to Sūrya she went to the Uttara-Kuru region and practised penance in the form of a mare.

**Chāyā's Children**

The VāyuP gives the names of the two sons of Chāyā as Śrutaśravas and Śrutakarman, who are called Sāvarṇi Manu and Śani respectively in all other versions. In the BVP there is no mention of either Manu or Sāvarṇi Manu (i. e., Śrutaśravas or Śrutakarman) among the sons of Sūrya. Furthermore, Yamunā and Tapatī are said to have quarrelled and turned into rivers by cursing each other. It may be recalled here that according to MāP Sūrya cursed his wife Saṁjñā that a river would be born to her and then she gave birth to Yamunā.

**Chāyā's Curse**

Chāyā ill-treated Saṁjñā’s children. On one of such occasions Yama did not conceal his anger and spontaneously revolted by lifting his leg. The added fuel to Chāyā’s anger and she pronounced a curse on him. The target was Yama's leg only, but a slight difference is seen in some Purāṇas. According to the MāP Chāyā cursed Yama that his leg would be eaten up by worms, that it would also suffer from wounds emissing pus and blood (MR); that he would lose his leg (VāyuP). According to the BVP Chāyā cursed Yama that the cruel task of killing people would be his. Thus Yama became a killer-god due to Chāyā ’s curse to him. It can be compared here that in the MāP Sūrya cursed Saṁjñā to bear a conrtroller of mankind and then Yama was born. Furthermore, when Sūrya lessened the severity of Chāyā ’s curse he also granted him the authority of a controller, pleased as he was due to Yama’s righteous visionand equal bearing towards friends and foes (MaP, 105, 15-19). The ViP records Chāyā’s curse but does not mention as to why she got angry.

On learning from Yama about Chāyā ’s curse to him Sūrya felt that he was wrongfully cursed. Yet, according to the MP, Sūrya showed his inability to do way with the effect of a mother's curse but gave him a cock who would eat up all the worms and clean the wound. According to the MāP the sun minimized the severity of the curse by adding that when the worms from his leg would fall on eath, it would be alright again (MāP)

**Yama’s Penance**

According to the MP Yama retreated to Gokarna-tīrthato practise penance and to please Lord Śiva. At last, Lord Śiva was pleased and Yama asked for (1) the power of protecting the world; (2) the kingdom of the Fathersand (3) the faculty of distinguishing virtue from vice. Śiva granted these to him (MP). According to the LP also Lord Śiva made Yama a lokapāla, but in the BVP it was Sūrya who made him a to lokapāla.

In the MāP Sūrya cursed his wife Saṁjñā to bear a river as a daughter, and Yamunā was born to her, while the BVP version of this story provides that Yamunā and Tapatī had turned into rivers due to mutual curses. Yamunā became a river in the northern region, while Tapatī in the western region. Thus the story has travelled from North to West, where Tapatī becomes a river in the western part of India (presently in Gujarat) now known as Tāpī.

A skeleton-sketch of this story is found in the ṚV, 10.17.1-2. It informs that:

- (1) Vivasvat’s wife was Tvaṣṭṛ's daughter and that her name was Saraṇyū.
- (2) Saraṇyū was the mother of Yama. She also bore Aśvins. It further says that she had given birth to two pairs of twins. The Nirukta explains that these two pairs of twins were (i) Yama and Yamī, and (ii) Aśvins (dual deities).
- (3) Vivasvat’s wife, Saraṇyū was hidden by the gods. They fashioned another woman who resembled Saraṇyū. This woman, called Savarṇā was given to Vivasvat as his wife.

The Ṛgvedic account has travelled to the Purāṇas retaining some original features and introduction further developments.

The names of most of the characters of this story are seen changed in the Purāṇas:

Vedic name Purāṇic name   Tvaṣṭṛ Viśvakarman   Vivasvat Sūrya/ Mārtaṇḍa /Vivasvat/Aditya\[457\]   Saraṇyū Saṁjñā/Sareṇu/Arūpā   Savarṇā Chāyā (the name of her son is Sāvarṇi Manu which reminds of her Vedic name Savarṇā)   Yamī Yamunā

According to the Vedic versions Saraṇyū was hidden by the gods. After hiding Saraṇyū they made another woman resembling her. This woman called Savarṇā was given to Vivasvat. Thus Saraṇyū’s disappearance was planned by the gods for the sake of the mortals. They hid this immortal bride, i.e., Saraṇyū in order to serve a special purpose which was beneficial to the mortals.

The Purāṇic version has a major diversity here. Here, she was not hidden by the gods. She left the house at her own will. She found it extremely difficult to bear Sūrya’s radiance. She had incurred Sūrya’s wrath due to her inability to look up in front of him. Sūrya’s wrath resulted in a curse to his wife Saṁjñā that she would give birth to the controller of mankind.

In the Vedic version gods hid Saraṇyū and made a similar woman for Vivasvat, but here Saṁjñā herself decided to leave the house. She fashioned a woman from her own body who resembled herself (MP). According to VP, MāP, BVP versions Saraṇyū called her own shadow (chāyā) and asked her to task care of her family in her absence. It is curious that in ṚV, 10.17.1-2 Manu is not mentioned as a son of Saraṇyū or of Sāvarṇiwhile Nir, 12.10 and Bṛhaddevatā, 6.162.7 say that Manu was born to Sāvarṇi (who is Chāyā in the Purāṇas). Max Mūller comments that "the fable of Manu might have come at a later stage’’.In the Purāṇas Manu is the eldest son of Saṁjñā and Sūrya. The twin Yama-Yamī were born after him.

V.S. Agrawala gives a symbolic interpretation of this story and associated the two wives of Sūrya with mortal and immortal creation. He takes Sūrya as a symbol of both immortality and death. Saraṇyū and Chāyā both are wives of Sūrya. the former is immortal or light and the latter is death or shadow or darkness. That which is light is called Saṁjñā (consciousness) and that which is Sāvarṇā or Chāyā is matter. He further comments that once Sūrya is conceived as aśva, the imagery of the Cosmic Tree as Aśvattha and its dynamic energy as aśva (mare) is easy. The cosmos is called Aśvattha, because a horse (the sun) is at its centre, or because it stands like a horse on three legs and is free on the fourth or because it itself depends on the power or movement of the archetypical horse, that is Sūrya. Thus the conception of Sūrya and Sūryā or Aśva and Aśvā as the parent of Yamaand Yamī on one hand and of the twin Aśvins on the other is consistent in the Vedic thought.

According to V.S. Agrawala it is the doctrine of light and shade (ātapa and chāyā— Kaṭha Up. 3.5.1) which is put into this story. Light corresponds to the immortal creation of Puruṣa and shadow to the mortal creation of Prakṛti. Sūrya is the symbol of immortality and death, that which is beyond or on the other side of Sūrya is amṛta, and that which is on this side is mṛtyu (cf. tad yad kiñca arvācinām Ādityād sarvam tad mṛtyunā āptam atha ya enarn at ūrdhvāC cinute sa punar mṛtyum apajayati — ŚB. 10.5.1.4).

**Yama's Family**

According to the details found in different PurāṇasManu was the eldest son of Sūrya. He was a co-uterine brother of Yama and Yamunā. Yama and Yamunā were twins.They had two more brothers and two more sisters who were born to Chāyā, the substitute of Saṁjñā. They were Sāvarṇi Manu, Śani,Tapatī and Visti. Aśvīnīkumāras and Revanta were also Yama’s brothers.

The Yama-Yamī dialogue in ṚV, 10.10 has led to an apprehension that Yamī was a sister-wife of Yama.A later version of this hymn is found in the thirteen chapter of Narasiṁha Purāṇa. It contains a dialogue between Yama and Yamī. The purport of this dialogue is similar to that of the ṚV, 10.10. It begins with an introduction and the fruit of reading it is stated at the end of the dialogue. It is to be noted that the dialogue in the ṚV, 10.10 has no introduction and it ends as abruptly as it begins.

The chapter 12 of the NP ends with Mārkaṇḍeya-Carita and Śukra requests Vyāsa to narrate to him another Puṇyā, pāpaharā kathā (NP, 13.1; 3,4). He also wants to know about the merit (puṇyā) a man of firm mind gets on his not doing a condemned deed. Here Vyāsa tells him the dialogue of high-souled Yama and his sister Yamī. Yama and Yamī were the children of Vivasvat, the son of Aditi, Yamī was younger of the two. Once Yamī approached Yama and spoke like this:

A brother is not a brother if he does not become the husband of his spouseless sister. A man, who turns down the passionate appeal of his sister to become her husband, is not worthy of being called a brother. It is a pity if the brother takes another girl as his wife while his own sister is tormented by passion. Therefore, he (Yama) should wish to do what she is wishing otherwise she would die.

She goes on to the extent of saying that he should unite his body with hers. (NP, 13.17). At this Yama gets annoyed and reprimands her for talking about something which is hated in the society. Anybody in this sense would not do such a sin of co-habiting with his own sister. It is a great sin \[mahāpātaka) which is practised only by animals (13.19). Here Yamī is ready with the argument that since their former union in their mother’s womb was not defiling, it would not be a sin then (13.20). Yama argues that Svayambhū would condemn such an act. The high personage of society should not do contemptible deeds because people tend to follow them. Not doing the condemned deed is a characteristic of dharma, and since Yamī 's proposal was highly sinful, it was against dharma. He points out that the sages call it a sin if one draws one’s sister unto oneself. Therefore, she should choose some other partner. Yamī wanted to lure him by referring to her beauty and youthful age. But Yama displays that he was really a dṛḍhavrata and dharmacitta who wished not to get involved in any sinful act (13.35).

Yamī persisted. but Yama did not succumb to the ellurement. As a reward for his firmness and not doing the condemned deed, he attained the godhood (13.36). The following verses state the greatness (māhātmya) of this dialogue and the fruit of reading it. It destroys all the sins of the listener. It further says that whoever reads this dialogue, his ancestors are satiated and do not enter the abode of Yama.

As stated in earlier stanzas of the NP chapter 13, this dialogue is narrated to Śuka by Vyāsa in reply to his query about the merit (puṇyā) earned by a man who is firm in his mind and never does any condemnable deed. Thus this dialogue is a laudatory of Yama’s who is not only firm-minded (dṛḍhacitta), a high soul (mahātman) but also upholder of morality and righteousness. Thus this story has an ethical and didactic message.

Although no wife of Yama is mentioned in the Vedas, there are some references to his wife and offsprings in the Mbh and the Purāṇas. According to the Mbh, 5.117.81; 13.165.11. Yama’s wife is Dhumorṇā. Another wife of Yama is Vijayā.

According to the BVP, 1.15.21 -22, Yama’s consort is the daughter of Mṛtyu (mṛtyu-kanyā) who is styled as beautiful, dark-complexioned and mahāsatī. She wears red clothes and has six arms. In an icon of Yama she is made standing, along with her sixty-four sons, on the left side of her husband Yama.In the BVP a legend connected with Yama's marriage is found.Yama was exceeding pleased with Vijayā the daughter of a brahmin. When she first saw him, she was greatly terrified at his appearance and more so on knowing his identity. Yama allayed her fears and she consented to become his wife.

After their marriage Yama brought Vijayā to his abode. He cautioned her against going to the southern quarter of his kingdom. But after some days, overpowered by curiosity she went to the forbidden region. There she saw people being tormented for their sins.

Sunīthā is the eldest daughter of Yama. Aṅga married her and the famous king Vena was their son (ViP, 1.13). However, according to the Mbh. 59.93 and the PP Bhumi-Khanda, 30.36, Sunitha was the mind-born daughter of Mrtyu. She was famous in the three worlds for her beauty and virtues. Here too she is mentioned as the wife of the king Anga and the mother of Vena. The MP describes her as a daughter of Mrtyu and as having a ferocious countenance. Other details are similar to those found in the ViP and PP.

Yama is also said to be the father of Upadanavi and Hima.The MP, 49.9 mentions Ilina as Yama’s daughter.

**Yama’s Kingdom**

Since Yama is the lord of the dead, his realm is called pretapateh puram, i.e., the city of the lord of the dead (VarahaP 194.2).

The Ramayana provides a lengthy description of the material splendour of Yama’s vast kingdom. Beyond his capital is great darkness. His capital is resplendent with golden pillars and seats of diamonds and cat’s-eyes, surrounded by bowers is Yama’s palace. On the judgement-seat sits lord Vaivasvata, the king (Ram, 4.41.45-46).

The name of Yama’s city is given as SamyamaniThe MaP, 3.18ff provides that Lord Siva, pleased with Yama’s penance at Gokarna made him a loka-pala his city being Samyamani. Yama is invariably connected with the south direction. His city is in the south. He is also the guardian of this direction. According to VP, the city of Yama is in the south. Varuna-nagar in the west and Kubera-nagar in the north. The MP states that Yama's city is in the south of Meru on the back side of Manasa.The BhagP, 5.26.2-5 also states that Yama’s abode is in the south.

Yama's city lies eightysix thousand leagues (yojanas) from the world of man. According to GP this distance is greater than this. It states that the dead soul walks 200 yojanas everyday for 47 days and nights and then he reaches the abode of Yama (GP, 2.5.78).

The area of Yama's city is a thousand yojanas in the VarahaP two rivers flowing in the city of Yama are beautifully described. One of them is Puspodaka (194.12) which abounds in the clusters of lotuses. It can destroy all the sins of the living-beings. The other river is called Vaivasvati (194.22). The water of this river is clean, fragrant and pleasant to taste. The river has a divine appearance. It flows through the city nurturing it like a mother her sons.

There is another river called Vaitarani flowing in Yama's city, but it is different from the other two since the dead have to cross this blazing river in order to reach Yama. The AgniP specially recommends that one who is on the verge of death should make gift of a cow (preferably a black one). This cow would enable him to cross the river Vaitarani in the world of Yama.

According to the GP, 2.6.47. There are 12 guards in the city of Yama. They are 12 Sravanas, the sons of Brahma.They declare the good and evil actions of the people to Yama and Citragupta (GP, 2.6.47).

The abode of Yama resounds with pleasing music. Sounds of drums, stringed instruments and flutes and the songs of beautiful maidens fill the atmosphere. The air is fragrant with perfumes and people are seen engaged in sports.

According to the description in the VarahaPthe city of Yama has three gates. They are made of copper. The southern and western gates are for the entry of the sinners, while the northern gate is meant for deities, sages and other virtuous people. The southern gate is frightening, very hot and difficult to look at. By Yama's command the entry of the wicked, the cruel, the flesh-eaters and sinners is through this (i.e., southern) gate.

The city of Yama is a place of justice. Even the weakest gets justice here for the wrongs done towards him. The weak take revenge for the injustice and atrocities done to them. Here the powerful cannot harrass the weak. Yama's city is called Samyamana because it controls the conduct of the people (Mbh, 13.102.16). Here nobody can speak untruth.

**Yama’s Sabha (The Court of Yama)**

The sabha of Yama is said to be luminous like gold and is of thousands of yojanas in size. According to the Mbh, 13.102.16 it is fashioned by Visvakarman. The Mbh, 13.71 contains the story of Naciketa. On being cursed by his father to go to Yama, Naciketa fell down as if thunder-struck. His father's tears bring him to consciousness and he tells what he had seen when he went to Yama. Yama welcomed him is he would an honoured guest (Mbh, 13.71.16) Naciketa saw the sabha of Yama which was shining like gold and was very big in size. Naciketa says that he found the Vaivasvati sabha, or the assembly hall of Yama, a very charming place. He also saw the world of the pious where rivers ran milk and the mountains were made of butter.

The sabha of Yama is more fully described in Mbh, 2.8. It is made by Visvakarman and is more than a hundred yojanas in extent, golden and sunny, where wishes are granted and there is no cold, hunger, sorrow, old age, etc. All good things to be relished are there. This palace contains royal priests and seers, saints and heroes. Kala, Mrtyu and the Pitrs who die during the southern course of the sun, etc. They are cheered by songs and dance. The fire-god is in the sabha too and all respect Dharmaraja (Yama).

The VarahaP describes the court of Yama in great details.According to it, in the beautiful city of Yama there is a grand and beautiful assembly-hall. It is inlaid with all types of gems and is governed by Yama. This assembly is attended by the pious and the truthful and by those who have conquered anger, desires and all types of allurements. This court decides all matters of good and evil deeds and lays down the code of Dharma (moral law).

In this assembly the scholars, well-versed in the laws of Dharma, judge the actions of the people in the light of the laws laid down by the scriptures. They carry out the task without any fear and without deviating from the righteous path. These scholars think over the matter strictly in accordance to the scriptures and report their decision at the right time.

Manu. Prajapati, Vyasa, Atri, Auddaliki, Apastamba, Brhaspati, Sukra, Gautama, Sankha, Likhita, Angliras, Bhrgu, Pulastya, Pulaha and other scholars of Dharma in addition to a large number of celestial and human beings are seen in the Yama-Sabha. Naciketa saw divine sages, all well-versed in the Vedas, seated on the sides of Yama. He also saw many sages and Manus in the court of Yama (VarahaP, 195.20.23).

**Yama's Messengers and Assistants**

Yama-dutas

Yama-dutas are Yama’s assistants. Their main task consists of approaching a dying person to take away his soul to the kingdom of Yama. An early reference in the TB, 1.5.15 provides that Yama’s assistants bring victims forcibly and torment them.

Yama’s assistants are of horrid appearance (Ma, 3.136). They carry a nooseand clubs with which they bind and bash the sinners. Generally the soul of a dying person is taken away by Yama’s assistants, but in rare cases this task is carried out by Yama himself as is done in the case of Satyavat (Mbh, 296.15, MP. 210.16).

According to the MaP, 10.59, at the time of a sinner’sdeath Yama-dutas, who are terribly frightening and foulsmelling, arrive near him carrying their noose and rods. The dying man starts trembling at their sight and dies under terrible pain.

The PP Bhumi-Khanda, 13.54.54 describes the appearance of Yama-dutas. According to it they have a frightening appearance with their crooked faces, fiery eyes and large bellies. Some of them are yellowish, some black and some are reddish-brown in colour. They are very rough and ruthless.Describing their terrible appearance the PP(Kriya-yoga-sara, chap. 6) mentions their deformed faces, fierce teeth and red eyes. They hold the noose and clubs. They are called mahavirah, (possessing tremendous strength). It is clear from these and other similar accounts that Yama’s 176 assistants are visible only to the dying man and not to others who might be present near him.

According to the GP, 2.9.12—The servants of Yama hold nooses and rods. They are 16000 in number. Each pura of Yama is guarded by one thousand Yama-dutas.

When a man dies the Yama-dutas take away the soul (i.e., the individual soul) to Yama’s region and his lifeless body is left behind on the earth. The individual soul is of the measure of the thumb and it resides in the heart of the man.The soul is above pains and pleasures, and it is considered to be obligatory that one is punished for the evil deeds committed by him during his earthly life. The soul, therefore, after the fall of his earthly body takes up a new body in order to experience the torments of the hells. This new body is similar in age and condition to his previous body and it is referred to as yatana-deha.The Yama-dutas quickly bind the body with the terrible-pasa.

Yama-dutas are very fearsome and violent,they beat the sinners with their rods and drag them towards the south direction.They make him move on the terrible path which leads to Yama's region and is full of torment. They also frighten them by describing the tortures which are in store for them in the different hells.

When the messengers of Yama came to take away Ajamila, the Visnu-dutas (the messengers of Visnu) immediately stepped into save him from the yamya-pasa. i.e., the noose of Yama.They tell Yama-dutas not to take Ajamila by the wrong way,since he has completely expiated all his sins by muttering the Lord's name at the time of his death.

**Story of Ajamila**

Ajamila was a learned brahmin. He possessed the knowledge of the scriptures. But driven by lust he abandoned the righteous path. He left his lawfully wedded wife and started living with a maid-servant. He even raised a family with her. He ignored all his religious duties and plunged himself fully in the worldly pleasures. He doted on his youngest son 'Narayana'. When the period of his life on the earth was over, Yama-dutas came to fetch him. Terrified by their frightening form, he called out to his son Narayana, who was playing at some distance. On hearing the name of Lord Visnu, the Visnu-dutas immediately rushed in and stopped the messengers of Yama who were taking out the soul of Ajamila from his heart (6.1.31). A dialogue between, the Yama-dutas and the Visnu-dutas ensued. Yama-dutas said that they intended to take Ajamila, who had done nothing for expiating his sins, to Yama. There he would get purified through undergoing the prescribed punishment.

The Visnu-dutas argued that by muttering the name of Lord Narayana he has expiated all his sins (6.2.13). He was no longer a sinner and hence it was unfair to carry him by the wrong path, i.e., the sinners’ path leading to Yama's kingdom. They reiterated that even one syllable of the Lord’s name was sufficient to destroy all the sins.

At this juncture the Yama-duta raised doubt that Ajamila had not uttered the Lord’s name, ’Narayana’ out of his devotion to him nor did he knew the efficiency of the Lord's name. Even the call of ’Narayana’ was not made for the Lord but for his own son named Narayana. Under such circumstances a question arises as to how he could be benefited from the utterance of the Lord’s name.

The Visnu-dutas cleared this doubt with the help of a beautiful simile. According to it a man uttering the Lord’s name even without knowing its power of purifying the sins, burns away all his sins just as fire though thrown ignorantly by a child on a heap of fuel burns the fuel completely (6.2.18).

Just as a medicinal drug even though taken by chance, without being aware of its effect, is bound to show its remedial effect; the Lord’s name, even when muttered by chance without knowing about its highest purifying power shows its efficacy in destroying all the sins.

On hearing these convincing words the Yama-dutas retreated without taking Ajamila with them. In this way. the Visnu-dutas got him released from the clutches of death and then they too disappeared.

Ajamila, who had been watching the happenings realised his mistake. He thereon resorted to a pious life. When his mind and intellect was established in Brahman, the Visnu-dutas appeared before him. He cast off his body and immediately assumed the angelic form of the Visnu-dutas (BhagP. 6.2.43).

The fate of the pious dead is described in the BhagP, 6.2.44. According to it the pious ascend the heavenly car, made of gold and proceed through heavens to the abode of Visnu.

The authority of Yama-dutas is put aside here in the case of Ajamila. In the VisnuP, 3.7.20 Yama advises his servants not to touch the Visnu-worshippers, one of whose characteristics is their devotion to the institution of varnasrama. By observing the rules of varnasrama one can attain Visnu, Siva or other gods. They can also be saved from hells and the power of Yama.

**Two Dogs**

The two messenger-dogs of Yama mentioned in the RV. 10.14.11 also appear in the Puranas. They are called sarameyau, i.e., sons of Sarama. It may however be noted that the term sarameya stands as a synonym of 'dog' meaning 'born in the clan of Sarama'. A description of these sons of Sarama is found in the BrP. 131.3. They are called caturaksau, i.e., four-eyed (sharp-sighted); pavanaharau, i.e., who consume air; Yama-priyau, i.e., dear to Yama; mahasresthau, i.e., most excellent and jananugaminau, i.e., followers of men.

Similarity of these epithets of Yama’s dogs with those mentioned in the RV is worth noticing. In the RV, 10.14 they are called sarameyau, i.e., sons of Sarama; asutrpau, i.e., delighting in taking away lives; caturaksah, i.e., four-eyed, sharp-sighted. A parallel to asutrpau can be seen in pavanaharau which may mean “consumer of the vital breath (prana)".

But these dogs are not mentioned as sitting on the path leading to the Yama-loka as in the RV, 10.14.11. Here their task consists of protecting the gods’ cows, and also the animals brought for the sacrifice.

It is important to note that dogs are connected with Yama and at sraddha (rituals for the manes) dogs as well as crows are given food.

The SkandaP, 2.4.9 states that the two dogs of Yama should be given lamp-offering on the fourteenth day of the dark half of the Karttika month. One should take a bath and with wet clothes one should give the dipa-dana (lampoffering) to the two dogs, the sons of Mrtyu. The lamp should be placed with the recitation of these lines "O Syama and Sabalal two brother-dogs! servants of Yamal sons of Mrtyu! May you be pleased with the offering of this lamp”. Thereafter he should again take a bath and perform Yama-tarpana by giving libations to the fourteen forms of Yama(MP. 102.22; PP. 4.124, 13-14. BvtP, 104.10).

**Birds**

In the Vedas, pigeons and owls are considered to be the messengers of Yama. In the MP they are described as connected with bad omen. Their entry in house is considered to be precursor of ill-fate. The MP, 237.6 says that if a pigeon enters the house it foretells the death of the householder. It is important to note that a pigeon's entry in the house is considered a bad portent and the RV, 10.165 (devah kapotah) is recited to remove the ill-effect of a pigeon putting its foot near the fire, i.e., coming well inside the house. MP, 237.6 further says that if a crow lands on one’s head or the bees 'make honey’, i.e., make a beehive, the death of the householder is predicted.Hooting of an owl predicts loss of property and death of the family-head.

The MP, 237.13 suggests that on such behaviour of the birds, etc. a havana must be performed and five brahmins should recite the hymn devah kapotah (RV, 10.165).

**Citragupta**

Judging man’s good and evil deeds and rewarding him by granting a passage to heaven or sending him to one or more torturous hells comprises the main function of Yama in the Puranic literature. Yama knows about all the sins of man. In some Puranas he has an assistant named Citragupta to help him in his task. In his record-book Citragupta keeps the account of all the good and bad deeds of men. Thus Citragupta, the scribe, is Yama’s chief assistant.

A brief description of Citragupta's house is found in the GP, 2.23.24-27. According to it the palace of Citragupta stands in the Yama-loka near its entrance. This divine palace is of twenty-five yojanas in size and enclosed by a strong iron-wall. This palace has one hundred lanes which are decorated with flags. Hundreds of lamps brighten the palace which is filled by sweet sound of music.It is beautifully painted by expert artists. In this palace there is a divine gem-studded throne. Sitting on this throne Citragupta calculates the lifespan of all the creatures. He also keeps an account of the their good and evil deeds. While carrying on this task he is not influenced by any factor and gives a proper thought to all good and evil deeds. He notes down a deed, if it is free from the eighteen faults, it is recorded as a good deed by Citragupta In this way Citragupta daily sits on his throne and writes down as to what action is done by whom (GP, 2.23-32).

In the court of Yama also where he pronounces the judgement, Citragupta is his chief assistant. He not only presents the account of man’s good and evil deeds he also recommends the fate that a dead man deserves for his actions. The VarahaP contain a dialogue between Yama and Citragupta. Citragupta says "this man attains total emancipation since he died in the battlefield after killing the enemy";“this other man who was killed for the cause of a brahmin, a cow or for his country should be immediately sent to Amaravati, the kingdom of Indra" (Varaha P, 205.9-11).

Agni Purana 371.12 states that sinners fall into horrible hells that are declared by Citragupta at the order of Yama.

On the second day of the bright half of Karttika, which is called Yama-duitiya people worship Citragupta, his pen and ink-pot. In later Hindu belief the Yama-duitiya comes to be regarded as a unique festival of brothers and sisters. The brother visits his sister's house. She makes kumkum-mark on his forehead and he gives her clothes, ornaments, etc. as gifts. According to the Puranic belief Yama and Yamuna (the twin) have become divine symbol of the selfless affection of brothers and sisters. Here another story connected with Yama-duitiya is worth mentioning. According to SkandaP, 2.4.11 Yami once invited her brother Yama to come to her place along with his ganas (assistants), and have food there. Yama being busy in his work could not come. Then one day Yamuna persuaded him to come on the second day of bright half of Karttika. She offered him food in gold-vessels. Yama asked her to choose a boon and Yamuna said — you must come to have food on this day every year. On this day you should release all creatures from the hells and whoever goes to his sister's house and eats there should be prosperous and happy.

**Kala**

In the Epics and the Puranas Kala and Mrtyu are mentioned as close associates of Yama. They are conceived as distinct personalities (Mbh, 9.45.17), though often identified with each other (cf 2.56.10).

Kala primarily means Time’. Kala ripens creatures forcibly by turning months and seasons, changes the names of months and seasons with ‘the fire of the sun and the fuel of the day and night’. In the Mbh, 12.309.90 Kala is shown as composed of day and night, months and seasons. The seasons are caused by rainfall, hence Kala (Time) is identified with death. The word kalyamana means ‘being killed’. It is to be noted here that the JB, 1.246 equates Yama with year (samvatsara). According to it Yama is time and the seasons are his faces.

The VarahaP describes Kala as the main attendant of Yama. It says that a dark-complexioned, high-chinned one with upraised hair was seated by his side. He held a massive stick in his left hand, had a deformed face, fierce teeth, and looked always angry and frightful. He is entrusted time and again by the Lord of Dharma (Yama) to administer punishment and he, the eternal Kala, obeys him diligently (VarahaP, 195.24-25).

**Mrtyu**

Mrtyu is mentioned as an assistant of Yama and he is Yama's constant companion. In the Yama-Ravana battle (Ram, 7.22) Mrtyu, armed with his pasa and deadly club accompanied Yama. The fierce battle went on for many days. When Yama was wounded by Ravana (Ram, 7.22.22) Mrtyu sought Yama's permission to kill him. Mrtyu is mentioned both as a god and a goddess in the Mbh. The goddess Mrtyu was created by Brahma out of his wrath and was ordered by him to kill the creatures. Unwilling to do such a cruel task, she protested. On being rebuked by Brahma for her disobedience, she wept. As her tears got collected in her cupped palms, Brahma transformed those tears into dreadful diseases which would kill people. Thus Mrtyu was saved from performing the gruesome task of a killer (Mbh, 12.250).

According to the Mbh, 7.54.17 the goddess Mrtyu dreaded the amount of sin she would collect for killing the creatures. She practised severe penance. Then Brahma assured her that any destruction by her would not be considered a sin (Mbh, 7.54.44). Terrible strength of goddess Mrtyu is described in Mbh, 12.8-9.

Mrtyu performed the task of taking away the life. In the Mbh, 13.1.50 Mrtyu confirms the snake’s statement that he was ordered by Mrtyu the god of death, to bite the boy. He consoles the people and says that the god has programmed all events beforehand and the boy’s death was not due to a mistake on anybody's part.

According to the VarahaP, 195.31 NaciketS sees Mrtyu in Yama’s court. He described him as haughty by nature (durvinita) extremely wrathful (mahakrodhah), terrible (sudarunah) extremely mighty (maha-sattvah) and vigorous (mahatejah) free from old age and death, unassailable (dur-dharsan and bearing celestial pastes and perfumes on his body.

In the BhagP, 4.8.4. Mrtyu is said to be born to Adharma (Brahma's son) and his wife Mrsa. In the PP, 30 a daughter of Mrtyu named Sunitha is mentioned. The cruel king Vena was born to her.

It is important to note that in the VS, 35.13 Yama is mentioned along with Antaka and Mrtyu (although till then Yama was not directly connected with death), who in the Puranas become close associates of Yama.

**Lady Assistant**

In the Varaha P, a lady is described as seated by the side of Yama in the latter's court. In the VarahaP, 195.27-28 Naciketa gives the description of this lady in the following way.

There, in Yama's court, I saw a beautiful lady endowed with lustre and honoured by Yama.  
She was the annihilator of all. All gods, demons sages fear her. All painful diseases come from her.  
(Varaha P, 195.30)

This reminds one of Nirrti in the Vedic literature. Nirrti is also described as having malevolent character who brings death, destruction and diseases to the people.

## 7. Personality and Functions of Yama

**Appearance of Yama**

The details of Yama's physical features are found in Mbh (3.281.16 ff.), MP(210. 5-7 ff), VarahaP (195.18 ff), PadmaP Kriya-Khanda, 22.74, etc. He is generally described as having a terrifying appearance.

According to the Mbh, 3.281.16 Savitri saw him as a dark figure with red eyes, holding noose and looking very fearsome.

The MP (210.5-7) also contains a description of Yama’s appearance as seen by Savitri when she saw him coming near. She had accompanied her husband Satyavan to the forest on that day, since she was aware of the forecast according to which the day of Satyavan's death had arrived. After some time Satyavan felt headache. He told Savitri about his headache and falling vision. He also expressed a desire to rest his head on her lap and sleep. As Satyavan slept on her lap, Savitri saw 'Dharmaraja'himself coming over there. His colour was like that of a blue lotus. He was wearing yellow clothes. His glittering crown and ear-rings made his dark body look like a dark rain-cloud illumined by the flashes of lightening. He was bedecked with garlands and armlets. Mrtyu and Kala followed him (MP, 210.2-8).

In the VarahaP, 195.18 Naciketa sees Yama the great ascetic (mahatapah) wearing many ornaments and a highly lustrous crown presented to him by Brahma. Due to his dazzling lustre he could be hardly be looked at. Here Yama appears to him as an amalgam of the collective energy of all powerful beings.

In the BVP Yama is also called Kala and is described as black in colour and shining like the summer-sun. Here he is mentioned as wearing red-clothes and having six faces, sixteen hands, six legs and twenty-four eyes. The mention of an unusual number of faces, eyes, hands, etc. indicates his unusual powers and capacities.

As Yama judges the good and the bad deeds of the people and grants punishment according to the sins committed during the life on earth, everybody has to go to Yama after his death. But the appearance of Yama is said to be different for the sinner and the pious.

On seeing a pious soul approaching Yama feels happy and assumes the form of Narayana. Thus, a pious soul finds Yama as four-armed, dark in complexion, having eyes like bloomed lotuses, holding the conch, the discus, the club and the lotus. He is seen with Garuda, the mount of Narayana, and wears the sacred thread made of gold. He also wears a crown, ear-rings and a vanamala (PP Kriya-Khanda, 22.74). It may be noted that all these assets belong to Narayana, i.e., Visnu. Hence this description shows that Yama completely transforms himself into Visnu and assumes his pleasing appearance. When these pious souls reach his palace, Yama, the presiding deity of Dharma, himself welcomes them happily as one would welcome one’s friends (PP, 23.78).

On the other hand his form is fierce for the sinners. His body takes a huge form of thirty yojanas. His eyes look like big lakes and his terrible voice sounds like a thunder at the time of the universal destruction. In the form of breath great winds blow from his nostrils. He looks fearsome with his very big teeth, and his ālip pressed by them. His nails are as big as winnowing baskets. He is seen wearing an animal-hlde, holding a danda and riding on a ferocious buffalo and thus looking very dreadful.

The MP gives some directions regarding the icon of Yama. According to it the image of Dharmaraja (i.e., Yama) should be made as holding a mace and a noose, riding a huge buffalo. He should be black like soot with eyes blazing like fire.

According to the ViDhP, 3.47 the image of Yama should be made dark as a rain-cloud, while the colour of his garments should be like that of hot gold.He should be seated on a buffalo and adorned with all ornaments. On his left lap should be Dhumorna, his consort, whose complexion is like that of a blue lotus. Here Yama is mentioned as having four hands. In his two right hands he holds as rod (danda) and a sword (khadga) and in the two left hands a flaming trident (jvala-trisula) and a rosary (aksamala}. On his right side Citragupta stands with a pen and palm-leaf to record the deeds of the mortals. To the left of Yama stands the terrible Kala who is armed with pasa.

According to other authorities in iconography the image of Yama should be made as follows:

He should be dark in colour resembling a rain cloud having two arms and fiery eyes. His face should be made fearsome with sharp teeth. He should be covered all over with red sandal paste and draped in red garments. He should be adorned with crown and ornaments.

His hands should be shown holding a sword (khadga) and a club (khetaka). He can be depicted as seated on a throne or mounted on his buffalo.

At another place Yama is described as holding (in addition to his rod) a pen, a book, and a cock.It may be noted here that a cock was given to Yama by his father Surya in order to minimize the effect of Chaya's curse to him.

All the Puranic accounts depict him as a dark and fearsome god. He wears many ornaments, among them is a highly lustrous crown which was presented to him by Brahma \[VarahaP, 195.18)

In spite of his fierceness he possesses a divine lustre which is equated with that of the summer's sun. He makes himself look more fearsome to the sinners.On the other hand he takes the form of Visnu when the pious souls reach near him.He welcomes them there as a friend (PadmaP, 23.78). His main weapons are his noose and rod (danda). He is also mentioned as holding a sword and a mace (MP.261.13). His clothes are yellow (MP, 261.7) or golden (ViDh P, 3.48). Sinners find him fearsome and, wearing hides (PadmaP Kriya-Khanda, 23.100 ff). A huge and ferocious buffalo is his mount (MP, 261.12,90). In the Puranic accounts Yama is invariably described as black in colour. This is significant since all dangerous things are painted black as this colour represents tamoguna. For example while learning is conceived to be white; no-learning or absence of knowledge is of black colour.Yama assumes the Yamasi form for the destruction of people with a view to maintaining the bounds of moralityand moral laws.

The Natya-sastra describes various colours as expressing various sentiments. According to it the bhayanaka rasa is of black colour.Each sentiment represented by one particular colour is presided over by a deity.

For obtaining siddhi, i.e., success and good effect, in a particular rasa the deity concerned has to be pleased. Yama is said to be the presiding deity of the karunarasa, while Visnu is of the srngara, Pramatha of the hasya, Rudra of the raudra and Indra of the vira.

**Yama as a Judge**

The most majestic aspect of Yama's personality is as Dharma or Dharmaraja. Yama, as Dharma, is a judge of man's good and evil deeds. He sends the pious to higher world while drops the evil-doers into the hells of which he is the lord. Yama is often lauded for his function as an impartial dispenser of justice. One of his epithets is Samavarti which speaks volumes about Yama's equal bearing to all beings. An ideal king is therefore aptly compared with Yama (Mbh, 12.68, 25.76,25.79). No sin done anywhere at any time can escape Yama’s notice. An often quoted text says that whereas a king punsihes the open sins and offences, Yama punishes men even for their secret sins hidden in their hearts.Through prayers to gods one wishes to be freed from all the sins he might have done by action, speech or even thought (manasa, vaca, karmana), because Yama can know even the sins which are deeply hidden in the heart of a man and not seen by anybody. Vasistha Dharma Sutra, chapter 20states that Yama punishes those who offend secretly. According to the BhagP, 6.4.48 Yama sees through this mind’s eye all the deeds of man and decides their fruit.

This aspect of Yama's personality controls the moral behaviour of people. The fear of Yama’s horrid punishments prevents people from leaving the righteous and moral path. Thus he is also ’a moral governor; the god of conscience’.

In this capacity his name 'Yama’ derived from the root yam to control, is very appropriate.

Yama judges the deeds of man when he reaches his kingdom after death. He grants the pleasure to the virtuous by sending him to heaven and the torments by sending him to hells. The sinner goes to various hells, each of them is prescribed for a particular set of crimes.

Sitting in his magnificent court Yama imparts justice. There he is helped by Citragupta, his scribe, who keeps a record of all the deeds of a man.Citragupta gives this account to Yama when the man reaches Yama’s region after death.

**Yama as a Warrior**

In the Puranas Yama is primarily the lord of hells. He is also one of the dikpalas, i.e., the guardians of the directions. Yama is the guardian of the south direction.Other dikpalas are — Indra of the east, Varuna of the west, Kubera of the north.

As a guardian of the south direction Yama is seen in the role of a great warrior. When Ravana attacked him to conquer the south direction, he had to fight a fierce battle with Yama and his close associate Mrtyu (Ram, 7.22 ff). Yama’s chief weapon is kaladanda which is described here as divine, bright like fire (7.22.5) and fashioned by Brahma (7.22 44). He also took with him his pasa and club (7.22.6). For seven days the battle went on. Ravana could not be subdued, although he was severely wounded, and fought vigorously. Mrtyu asked Yama's permission to kill Ravana.
