At this Yama, determined to kill Ravana, lifted his never-failing weapon, the kaladanda. As he was about to kill Ravana, Brahma appeared before him and asked Yama not to use the kaladanda on Ravana. He told Yama that he (Brahma) had given a boon to Ravana and this kaladanda was also made by him (Brahma). Therefore, whether Ravana was killed by the kaladanda or kaladanda failed in killing Ravana both the situations would make his (Brahma’s) word false.

At this request from Brahma Yama kept back his kaladanda and unwilling to fight the battle, disappeared (Ram, 7.22.50). In the devasura battle Yama fought with demon Kalanabha. He rode a buffalo to help Indra against Krsna when he was taking away the parijata tree, but was dlscomfitted by Krsna (BhagP, 8.10.29).

Yama also took active part in the Tripura war where, he used his danda (MP, 135.77) and rode on his fearsome buffalo (MP, 133.63). Indra was told to attack the ’ocean of asura' which had entered the Tripura-fort, and defeat them with the help of Yama. Kubera took charge of the western entrance (MP, 138.25). Yama was defeated by Kalanemi in this battle (MP, 177.50).

In the devasura battle described in the MP, 148.79.81. Dharmaraja (Yama) advanced forth on his buffalo surrounded by his violent-tempered assistants. Yama's eyes began to blaze as if flames were rising towards the sky at the end of a kalpa.

Yama’s fight with Grasana is described in detail in the MP, 150.1-49. Several weapons, used by Yama are mentioned here e.g., formidable arrows, clubs, a javeline named prasa, etc. With the blow of prasa Grasana fell unconscious. When he came to senses, he threw a heavy club towards Yama. At this Yama hurled the most formidable, all-destroying blazing danda, Yama’s danda and the demon’s club collided in midair and a tumultuous sound was produced. They fought with blows too. After a long fight Yama was exhausted and was subdued by the demon.

**Yama and Dharma**

The early commentary of Gunavisnu on ChaBr refers to Yama as Dharmaraja.In the Epics and the Puranas Yama is called Dharmaraja.Dharmaraja is also one of the several names of Yama which are enumerated in the Amarakosa and SabdaratnavaliAlthough Vedavyasa has used the term 'Dharmaraja' and Yama' as synonyms of Dharmadeva in the Mbh whereas Yama is extensively called Dharmaraja in the Mbh.Thus it is clear that the term Dharmaraja is used for both, i.e., Dharmadeva as well as Yama. This has, perhaps, led to a certain amount of confusion in some Puranas where Yama and Dharma are taken to be two names of a single deity. But a study of the stories around each of them clears the confusion and establishes that they are two separate gods.

Dharma is one of the twelve Prajapatis created by Brahma. They are—Dharma, Daksa, Marici, Atri, Pulastya, Kratu, Vasistha, Gautama, Bhrgu, Angiras and Manu.In the Mbh Dharma along with Yama is counted among the twenty-one Prajapatis.

First of all, the parentage of Yama and Dharma confirms that they are two separate deities. Dharma is a son of Brahma. He came out in the human form from Brahma’s right breast.He married thirteen daughters of Daksa. On the other hand Yama is a son of Surya and Samjna. Yama is the sixth descendant of Visnu. From Mahavisnu were descended, in the following order Brahma-Marici-Kasyapa-Surya-Yama.

Dharma is also the name of a deity who presides over righteousness and religious life. He is said to be the father of Yudhisthirawho is also called Dharmarajaas well as Dharmaputra. It was Dharma who tested the sincerity of and righteousness of Yudhisthira (Mbh, 3.296.5; 3.298.24) and king Hariscandra. He was especially pleased with Hariscandra due to his qualities of tolerance, self-control and truthfulness (MaP, 8.46). Dharma tells him that he was knowing about the calamities which would befall on Hariscandra. He had assumed, with his special power, the form of a candala (MaP,8.56). Dharma tested the righteousness of Yudhisthira when the Pandavas were staying in the Dvaita-vana. Dharma, as the Yaksa of the lake asked Sahadeva, Nakula. Arjuna and Bhima, who had come there one by one, to answer his questions before drinking water. They arrogantly refused to do so and ignoring his word they drank water. Consequently all of them fell dead one after another. After some time Yudhisthira came there looking for his brothers. He too was asked by the Yaksa to answer his queries before drinking water. Yudhisthira agreed to do so. He pleased the Yaksa by answering all his questions to his utmost satisfaction. The Yaksa discloses his identity that he was none other than Dharma himself who had come there to test Yudhisthira. Pleased by his answers the Yaksa told Yudhisthira that he could bring one of his brothers to life and asked him to make the choice. Yudhisthira chose Nakula to be granted the gift of life, with a view that since one of Kunti's son (he himself) was alive, may. at least, one of Madri's sons also live. This gesture further pleased the Yaksa who granted life to all his brothers.

There is another instance, at the time of Yudhisthira's ascent to heaven through Himalayas, when Dharma tested his righteousness. While Draupadi and Nakula, Sahadeva, Arjuna and Bhima fell dead on the way one by one, a dog faithfully followed Yudhisthira. Indra welcomed Yudhisthira in heaven but he would not allow a dog to enter heaven. Here Yudhisthira refused to enter heaven unless the dog who had followed him all the way was also granted entry. Indra insisted that the dog should be left behind but Yudhisthira was firm on his stand. At this the dog transformed himself into Dharma, the deity of righteousness. Extremely pleased with Yudhisthira he said, “There is no one equal to you even in the heaven who has refused ascending on Indra's chariot for the sake of a dog” (Mbh, 17.2.1 ff).

Though Yama is also called 'Dharmaraja' it does not conclude that Yama and Dharma are identical. Yama is conceived as an imparter of justice. He weighs the evil and good deeds of man after his death and awards punishment according to the sins committed by him. Because of this special function of his, he is called Dharmaraja. On the other hand Dharmadeva is righteousness personified. Thus it is clear that in spite of both of them being called ‘Dharmaraja' Yama and Dharma are two separate deities. The title Dharmaraja needs to be interpreted differently in connection with Yama and Dharma. Yama is called Dharmaraja because he imparts justice by granting the fruit of good and evil deeds of men, and in this capacity he is Dharmaraja. While Dharma (i.e., Dharmadeva) is the presiding deity of Dharma, or the righteousness personified.

Although Yama and Dharma are separate deities a tendency to identify them with each other is seen in the story connected with Vidura.

Sage Mandavya was punished severely by Dharma for a sin he committed in his childhood. The sage could not tolerate this injustice because a child’s action does not amount to a punishable sin. He cursed the ‘unjust imparter of justice' that he would be born to a sudra woman. Here it is seen that Yama who judges others actions and punishes them was also, at least once, punished for his fault. Due to Mandavya's curse Dharma was born as Vidura, the son of Krsna-dvaipayana and the maid-servant of Ambika, a wife of Vicitravirya.According to Mbh, 1.57.77-81 Dharma was born in sudra order as Vidura due to the curse of Animandavya. According to the BhagP, 3.5.20 and BrahmaP, 2.27.25 also Yama was born, as Vidura to a maid-servant.

Animandavya (or Mandavya) cursed the god who awarded him the punishment. This god, here called Dharma, could be none other than Yama since it is Yama who performed the task of judging and punishing the people. Thus, it is not difficult to deduce that the god whom sage Mandavya cursed to be born to a sudra, was Yama, though he is called Dharma here. The Mbh knows Yama as Dharma in 1.108.8 where the sage goes to the abode of Dharma and cursed him to be reborn as Vidura. This scene is repeated in the Mbh, 1.63 93 but without a complete identification.

Dharmaraja and Yama are mentioned as identical in the MP, chapter 226. According to the BrahmaP, 94.16-32 Yama is another name of Dharma. The BrVaiP contains a eulogy of Yama by Savitri.According to it sun got a portion of Dharma as his son and this was Yama. This clears the confusion and explains convincingly why Yama is called Dharma. Yama was a portion of Dharma, whom Surya got as his son after pleasing Dharma with his devotion.

In the Skanda Purana Yama himself reveals his identity as Dharma. He says. “I am Yama for the evil-doers and Dharma for those who win hearts (with their noble deeds)" (SkandaP, 3.24). Yama practised severe austerities. Indra was troubled and sent an apsara named Vardhani. There is a dialogue between Yama and Vardhani. Here Yama says that he is Yama for sinners and Dharma for the pious. He also disclosed to her that his penance was to gain a good name in every kalpa and mahakalpa. Vardhani returned to Indra and reported that Yama is practising penance for the purpose of gaining fame hence there was no reason for Indra to worry. After one hundred years of hard penance Siva appeared before him and fulfilled the wishes of gaining good name in all the three worlds and make Dharmaranya holier than Kailasa by promising that “a portion of mine shall always stay here". In the Mbh, 3.319 Savitri follows Yama who is taking away the soul of her husband Satyavan. Even after being granted three self-chosen boons and repeatedly being asked to return back, she keeps following Yama. Savitri eulogises to please Yama and thereby gets back Satyavan's life. In the Mbh, 3.319 she says to Yama. “You are the son of Vivasvat, therefore, you are called Vaivasvata. You are also called Dharmaraja because you please people with your quality of the controller and Dharma".

V.S. Agrawala comments that Yama is the lord of death as the principle of Matter, and of immortality as Dharma. In this later aspect he is deva drinking soma with the host of devas sitting on the summit of the cosmic-tree (RV, 10.135.1). Each newborn life-principle is a Yamayana Kumara, and is a link in the endless chain of the preceding fore-fathers (puranam anuvenati).

**Yama and Other Gods**

The personality of the Vedic Yama has undergone a tremendous change in the Puranic mythology. In the RV he is the overlord of a special region where all the dead go and rejoice. There are earnest prayers too which express a strong desire to become immortal in the region where Yama rules. In the Vedic literature Yama stands like any other major god of the pantheon. With his growing connection with death and hells Yama has now become a feared-god, though many Puranas emphasise that Yama shows his dreadful appearance only to the sinners, and that he is extremely gentle and benevolent towards the pious.

**Dharma, Kala and Antaka**

With his new personality Yama’s relation with other gods has undergone a change. In the Epics and the Puranas Yama became a killer-god, as well as the upholder of the ethical laws, the judge of man’s actions and a punisher. The multiple aspects of his personality came to be personified as different deities.

One of Yama’s name is Kala. In this aspect he represents the time which is moving and progressing and, therefore, with its march it makes everything grow older and older. Antaka declares the end of a life, while Mrtyu brings death and carries the dead to the other world.

Another aspect of Yama’s character, of which only glimpses are seen in the Vedic literature, is that of a punisher and an upholder of the ethical laws. In the Puranas it becomes his most prominent function. Here he is all in all of the faculty of judging man's actions and punishing him in the various torturous hells of which he is the supreme lord. Here Yama as Dharmaraja, with his code of justice, ensures a fair play in all this.

Mrtyu is stated to be a chief assistant of Yama.He remains present in the court of Yama when Yama listens about man’s good or evil actions and announces rewards or punishment for him. Mrtyu also accompanies Yama to the battles. In the Yama-Ravana battle when Ravana was not discomfitted even after seven days, Mrtyu asked for Yama's permission to kill Ravana.

**Yama and Siva**

According to the MP, 10.20.21 the overlordship of the yonder world as well as the position of a moral judge was granted to Yama by Siva.

Being a son of Surya Yama is a solar god. His brother Manu came to be regarded as the progenitor as well as the guardian of mankind. His position is complimentary to that of Yama who is the lord of the dead, but Yama’s solar connection gradually became dim and he became increasingly related to the lunar (Siva, Agni, Soma) group of the gods.

Yama's connections with Siva are important. He became closely connected with Siva, the god of destruction and the other two gods of the triad are Brahma, the creator and Visnu, the sustainer. Yama was made the king of the departed by Siva (Mbh, 12.122), whose mount (the bull) is also called Dharma (Mbh, 12.91.13), an appellate of Yama in this context, then Yama as the lord of the Fathers became an integral part of Siva-complex.Furthermore, Yama is frequently called Kala and it is only as Kala he imbibes the attributes of Siva-complex more and more that he becomes the true representative of Kala or Time, for Siva is Mahakala. The three eyes of Siva represent the three division of time, i.e., past, present and future. The crescent moon in Siva’s matted hair portrays the measuring of time by years, while his damru has a shape like that of a time-measuring sandglass.

Yama as the god of the lunar associations controls the path of the Fathers through moon and of the repeated births (pitryana). The other path is the devayana. By this path the solar god Visnu leads the righteous to liberation through the sun.

In the Puranas too Yama is the lord of a special region. Although his overlordship is restricted to the hells only, he is the established king of the next world. He performs his duty by sending his dutas to bring the souls of the people whose life span was over. On certain such occasions Yama’s authority is set aside by other gods. In the story of Ajamila,the Yama-dutas arrive to take him away but they are stopped by the Visnu-dutas. They tell Yama-dutas that although that man had committed many sins, but since he had pronounced the Lord's name at the hour of death he should not be taken away by them.

At another place Yama is found to have been defeated by Krsna. According to the BhagP, 8.10.29Indra, with Yama and others as his accomplices fought with Krsna when he tried to take away the Parijata tree from Indra’s garden. Here Yama was defeated by Krsna in the battle.

Yama was also there with other gods in the devasura battle, especially the Tripura-battle. Thus, Yama, who was the lord of a special region, assisted other gods in their endeavour.

**Epithets of Yama**

In most places Yama is referred to as ’Dharmaraja’ (MP, 102.22; 107.27; 135.76; 136.5; BrVaiP, Prakrti Khanda, 26.1, Bvt P, 104.10; PP, 4.124.13-14; VarP, 191.7). He is also called Dharma. Dharmajna and Dharmasila (GP, 2.3.22). When Hariscandra sees Yama disguised as a candala, the latter is called Dharma (MaP, 8.80).

In the Epics and Puranas Yama is invariably called a controller. In the Mbh 6.34.29 Lord Krsna called himself 'as Yama among the controllers'.In the Mbh, 3.296.66 states that Yama is the controller of all the creatures.Yama controls the actions of people by awarding them reward or punishment in accordance to their deeds.Obviously the vocable yama here is derived from the Vyam, to control.

In the Puranas Yama is a god who controls the dead as well as the living creatures (through fear and by the ethical code) and also as a god who punishes the souls in the hells. Yama, who was the king of the dead in the Vedas, gradually moves towards a Kala-Dharma pattern of divinity and was relegated to the sphere where Siva was a central deity to whom Yama became an associate.

Yama controls people under his discipline and hence he is called 'Yama’, i.e., the restrainer.The fear of Yama's punishments stops people from going astray from the righteous path. Fourteen names of Yama are mentioned in the Puranas.

On the fourteenth-day of the bright-half of Karttika the water-offerings are made of Yama. Here the fourteen names of Yama are given as — Yama. Dharmaraja, Mrtyu (death), Antaka (ender), Vaivasvata (son of Vivasvat), Kala (time, as the consumer) and Sarvabhutaksaya (destroyer of all creatures). Audumbara, Dadhna, Nila (dark), Paramesthin Vrkodara, Citra and Citragupta (MP, 102.22; PP, 4.124.13- 15, BvtP, 104.10). All these names are taken as those of pitrs by the puranakara. It is said here that water with sesamum should be offered thrice to each of the fourteen Yamas. By doing so a man gets immediately freed from all sins done by him during the whole year.

In the Mbh Yama is called samavartin (Mbh, 12.207.35 cf 122.27). The origin of this name is not explained here. Krsnamitra, commenting on the Amarakosa, 1.1.58; where samavarti is listed among fourteen names of Yama, explains this epithet as samam mitre ripau ca. The epithet highlights Yama's quality of a fair judge and punisher. He is neither lenient to his friend nor biased against his foe. As an impartial judge he punishes only on the basis of the crime done.

According the VarahaP, 196.8. ff when Naciketa reaches Yama’s palace, he was received there with honour. He recited an eulogy to please Yama. In this prayer Yama is given several names and epithets. They are:

Dhata (producer), Vidhata (creator), Pitrpati (lord of the Fathers), Catuspada (four-footed). It is to be noted here that Yama is also called Dharma and Dharma is conceived to be four- footedKalajha (knower of time), Krtjna (knower of action), Satyavadi (truthful), Drdhavratah (having firm resolve).

Dandapani — holding a rod in his hand, Virupaksa—having fearsome eyes, Pasahasta — holding a noose, Duradharsa — hard to be subdued, Havya-kavyavahah — carrier of oblations, Papahanta—destroyer of the sins, Maha-tapah — great ascetic, Mrtyu — death.Earlier in this episode he is called ‘Dharmaraj’ and ’Pretaraj’.

The Amarakosa, 1.1.58 provides the following list of Yama’s fourteen names:  
Dharmaraj, Pitrpati, Samavarti, Paretarat, Krtanta, Yamunabhrata, Samana, Yamarat, Yama, Kala, Dandadhara, Sraddhadeva, Vaivasvata, Antaka.

Another list of Yama’s fourteen names/epithets which occurs in the Sabda-ratnavaliis as follows:  
Dharma, Dharmarat, Jivitesh, Dharmaraj, Audumbara, Dandadhara, Kinasa, Dadhna, Mahisavahana, Sirnapada,Bhimasasana, Kanka, Hari.

In the BrVaiP Prakrti Khanda, 261.8 Savitri prays to Yama by reciting a Yamastaka, i.e., an eulogy consisting of eight verses, sung in laudation of Yama. Here eight names of Yama are given as follows:  
Dharmaraja, Samana, Krtanta, Dandadhara, Kala, Yama, Punyamitra (i.e., a friend of the pious) and Brahmavarnsa (born in the lineage of Brahma).

The names and epithets of Yama can be broadly classified in the following way:

Epithets bases on relations—Vaivasvata (son of Vivasvat, Ravitanaya, Suryatmaja, Yamunabhrata.  
Epithets based on his physical features — Sirnapada, Dandapani, Pasahasta, Mahisavahana.  
Epithets based on his position — Dharmaraj,Pitrpati, Dandadharah (a judge). Yamarat, Paretarat, Sraddhadeva, Krtanta.  
Epithets based on his function — Antaka, Kala, Samana, Samavarti (Mbh, 12.207.35; cf. 122.27). Punyamitra (BrVaiP Prakrti Khanda 26.7), Dharma, Subhakarman (Mbh, 8.45.31), Bhimasasana, Mrtyu, Kala.

J. Gonda has pointed out that the same word may be, in a particular context an epithet, but in another passage a proper name, apposition or a purely attributive adjective.He observes that an adjective used as an epithet is not always distinctive. Sometimes it mainly serves to express appreciation.

## 8. Eschatology, Metempsychosis and Worship of the Manes

In the Epics and the Puranas much thought was given to man's life after death. It was now believed that good and evil deeds of men incurred merit and sins respectively which ultimately designed his post-mortem existence. The pious enjoyed a life in heaven, while the sinners, in accordance with the nature of their evil-deeds were tortured in various hells. With the development of the Karma-vipaka theory, elaborate account of heaven and hell are seen the Mbhand the Puranas. They provide graphic pictures of the rich pleasures of heaven and, in greater details, the horrifying tortures of the various hells. Yama's personality as a judge and a punisher also gains dimensions and he becomes the lord of hells, i.e., the regions where the evil-doers are punished. He is now an aweinspiring god who frightens men into piety.

The description of the hell also has a didactic and deterrant purpose as they make people adopt the accepted code of behaviour and morality by infusing in their minds the fear of the torturous hells.

It is interesting to note that the manner in which the pious and the evil-doers are taken to the yonder world is enough to suggest that people start getting the reward for their deeds even before reaching the court of Yama. The passage of the pious and the sinners to the region of Yama is described as follows.

**Path of the Pious**

Generally Yama sends his assistants to fetch the souls of the people at the time of their death. Since the sight of the yama-dutas (i.e., Yama's assistants) is inauspicious, Yama does not employ them for bringing a pious soul. He himself comes to take away the souls of the noble and meritorious people, as in the case of Satyavat (Mbh, 3.281.15).In the MP version of this story Yama says “Satyavat has earned a lot of religious merit by serving his parents and therefore, I myself have come to take him".

In the contrast with Yama-dutas who drag the sinners on the horrible path to Yamaloka beating and frightening them all the way,the dutas of heaven are said to take the pious souls very comfortably to the heaven. The GP, II.5.19 states that the behaviour of the Yama-dutas towards the pious souls is like that of loving friends. Thus there are certain people who do not have to go to Yama’s kingdom.They go directly to heaven. According to the Mbh, 9.50.20 the habitants of Kuruksetra go directly to heaven.

According to Mbh, 9.50.20 a person can go to Brahman without going to Yama. It says that people unnecessarily tremble on hearing the name of Yama. One, who has controlled himself, is not remembered by Yama.Similar views are expressed in the Yama sarmillopakhyana in the AgniP.The ten types of Yamas’ or controls enumerated here are — Kindness, forbearance, truth, non-violence, control, simplicity, meditation, lovingness, sweetness and contentment.

According to the VarahaP certain people do not have to go to hell. They include the keeper of the sacred fire, the giver of land, sesamum, cow, gold and land; the master of the Vedas, one who respects the twice-born and the sacrificer and one who studies the Vedas daily.

The BhagP, 6.2.44 mentions that the pious ascend the heavenly car made of gold and proceed through heavens to the abode of Visnu.

The PP records that the people who have done noble deeds enjoy pleasures in this life and after death they become Khecaras and Vidyadharas and go to heaven.

A man is said to be saved from going to hells, if he is freed from his sins by someone offering him the fruit of his own good deeds or by the Lord’s (Hari's) grace. Sukari states that if she were not freed from the sins through the queen Sudeva’s merits, she would have to bear the sufferings of the hells.

**Fate of the Pious**

As a reward for their meritorious deeds the pious at their death are taken to Brahma-loka in the shining aerial cars drawn by swans and peacocks, tinkling with bells; adorned with flags, studded with various jewels and resounding with the songs of the nymphs.

They enjoy the pleasures till the end of a kalpa.The different paths and different vehicles are also mentioned in PP.

The GP states that when Yama sees a pious soul coining to him, he moves from his place and announces that there he surpasses his region and goes to the Brahma-loka.This shows the Yamaloka is not meant for the pious souls.

In the NrP (chapter 13) at the end of the Yama-Yami dialogue it is stated that whoever reads the dialogue, his ancestors feel satiated and they no longer stay in the abode of Yama.This version of the dialogue highlights the strong moral character and firmness of Yama, who did not let himself be allured to do a deplorable and condemned act. One who reads this dialogue also shares this firmness of mind and thereby he not only pleases his ancestors, but also frees them from their sins. This entitles them to be saved from a stay in the kingdom of Yama.

According to the BrP, 22.5.6.7 a generous donor goes to heaven where he enjoys the pleasures in the company of the nymphs in the heavenly garden named Nandanavana.

From these references it is clear that according to the Puranic belief the pious led a happy life on earth and after their death they went to heaven directly without going to the region of Yama. In brief, heavenly regions and the pleasures thereof are for the pious and they do not have to visit the torturous Yamaloka.

By performing various meritorious deeds man secures a place for himself in the various celestial regions or lokas as the reward of his merits. The PP has named several lokas, e.g., Sivaloka, Indraloka, Sarasvatiloka, Brahmaloka, Gauriloka, etc. At places the PP has graded them in order of supremacy as Indraloka, Brahmaloka, Sivaloka and above all in the Visnuloka.

**Onward Journey of the Sinner**

As the time ofa person’s death arrives, Yama or his assistants come to fetch him away. The dead man has to leave his body, but he gets a special bodywhich is termed as yatana-deha. The AgniP mentions the dead man acquiring a body in order to suffer the torments of the hells;he has to undergo the punishments for the sins committed during his life-time (MaP, 10.62). This body is similar to the age and condition of his earthly body. At Yama’s command his servants take away the yatana-deha of the dead man and drop it into the different hells. The yatana-deha, as the word implies, means the body which is meant for undergoing the sufferings after death.It is subjected to many death-like tortures in hells, but it does not perish thereby and get freed from further tortures. It is not destroyed even on being burnt or cut into pieces in the hells like Raurava, Maharaurava and Asipatravana. It regains it complete form after each punishment and thus makes the man experience the pain and torture till the period of the punishment awarded to him is over.

Yama sends his servants to bring the people whose time on earth is over. But as their appearance is inauspicious, he does not employ them to bring the noble and the pious souls. In such cases he honours the pious man by himself appearing before him to take his soul away. The Mbh, 3.281.15 tells that Yama himself came to fetch Satyavan.In the MatsyaP version of this story, Yama discloses the reason to Savitri that on account of his serving his elders. Satyavan had earned enormous religious merit and therefore he himself had come to take him.

The BhagP states that a person who is engrossed in worldly pleasures, who is deeply attached to his own self, his wife, son, house, cattle and wealth, often adopts immoral ways. At the time of his death two frightful messengers of Yama arrive. He is so terrified at the mere sight of them that he voids stool and urine. The Yama-dutas give him the special body for undergoing the sufferings of the punishment in the hells: they put a noose around his neck and drag him on a long way just as a king's men take the convicts.His heart is riven by their intimidations and threats and he trembles in every limb. Hounds are let loose to feed on him. His mind is full of remorse and sorrow, as he recalls his sins. Tormented by hunger and thirst, scorched by sun and the forest-fire he is also lashed on the back. In this manner he is taken to Yama's kingdom. On the way there is no water or shelter. With great difficulty he moves on this path which is plunged in darkness. Stumbling and falling at every step due to exhaustion he is led on this path to Yama’s region.

The yatana-deha has to travel ninety-nine thousand yojanas to reach Yama. This distance he covers in two or three muhurtas but they seem very long and unbearable to him. He is further tormented in various ways, e.g., by burning of his body, by having to consume his own flesh cut either by others or by himself, having his entrails torn out by the dogs and vultures of Yama's kingdom. He is plunged in water; or buried in a pit. Thus he has to bear numerous sufferings.

According to the MP a preta, i.e., one who has died recently, hovers about in the sky in the form of air for a period of sixty thousand and eighty terrible years and then at the time of their fall the pretas are devoured by terrible raksasas (MP, 39.7-8). It also says that those who are not conscious of their duty during their life on earth and whose actions are not meritorious, go to the horrible hells. The PP says that the sinners after death follow the path of Yama (PP Patala- khanda, 97.10.23). The messengers of Yama bind the sinner with the kalapasa and bring him to the hells, where he is beaten up by the servants of Yama (PP Patala-khanda, 48.7-8).They bind his body with ropes and drag it on the path to Yama-loka which is full of thorns, insects, serpents, hot sands and wild animals.This path is terrible, difficult to tread on and full of torments due to excessive heat, cold and darkness.

The GP states that the path to Yama’s kingdom is eighty - six yojanas.This path which connects the earth with Yama’s kingdom is full of sufferings. Yama-dutas bind the sinners with the pasa and they also beat them on the way.They frighten the sinners by describing the horrible hells they are heading to and forcibly drag them on the path (GP. 2.5.28). There is neither food nor water, no place to rest and no shadows of trees on this path which is full of thorns.These sufferings are meant for the sinners only. The noble and pious are taken very comfortably to svarga(i.e., heaven) by the messengers of heaven (svarga-duta). Yama treats the pious souls in a friendly manner, while the sinners suffer intolerable tortures and are threatened by Yama with his rod when they reach him. The preta (i.e., one who has recently died) walks two hundred yojanas daily and reaches Yama's region in forty-seven days and nights (GP, 2.5.78).

At the time of death Yama-dutas take away the angustha matra-purusah. from the body of the dead(GP, 2.5.22). This spatial dimension finds a reference in the Upanisads. The angustha-matra-purusah is conceived to be the individual soul.The soul (purusa), which is of the measure of a thumb and dwells in the middle of the body, is the lord of the past, the future (and the present times).

**Fate of the Sinner**

A new body of the dead is formed in nine days time.This body partakes whatever is offered to the dead man, as the part of the sraddha-rites on the tenth eleventh and the twelfth day after the death. On the thirteenth day the dead is taken on the path of Yama-puri. Thus on the thirteenth day the dead now possesses a special body and leaves his house. Bound by the noose he is now taken to the region of Dharmaraja, i.e., Yama. He reaches there after twelve months, since he has to first cross eleven regions which takes a period of eleven months.These regions are —

- (1) Yamya-pura
- (2) Sauri-pura (here he stays for two months)
- (3) Gandharva-pura
- (4) Saitagama-pura
- (5) Krura-pura
- (6) Citra-nagar
- (7) Pura
- (8) Duhkhada-pura
- (9) Nanakranda-pura
- (10) Tapta-pura
- (11) Raudra-sthana
- (12) Sita-pura

From there he proceeds to the land of ‘Dharmaraja’, which is inhabited by divine gandharvas and nymphs and it is forty - four yojanas in size.

The Puranas thus bring out the differential treatment meted out to the pious and the sinner. The object is too obvious to be missed. These horrors are supposed to act as deterrents to prevent people from doing such acts which were considered sinful. Thus the concept of hell has a didactic purpose.

**Rebirth**

The concept of rebirth was well established in the time of the Puranas. As the doctrines of karma and punarjanma were formulated, the Vedic idea of heaven and hell also came to be modified. Unlike the early Vedic thought that the heaven or hell are granted for once and for ever whence the man had no return, the Puranic thought entertains the belief that man's stay in heaven/hell is for a limited time. Duration of this period varied from person to person according to his karma. Now the pious did not go to heaven and become immortal there, nor do the sinners fall in the dark pit and disappear.Now heaven and hell are places for reward or punishment where the soul has to stay for the required period depending upon his merits and sins. Thus it was now believed that the pleasures of heaven and the torments of hell came to an end after which the soul takes up another earthly form, i.e., it is reborn on the earth as an animal, a tree or a human being.Thus it was believed that a man has to pass through several births and deaths. The amount of good or evil deeds done during his life on the earth not only sent him to heaven or hell, but also contributed in deciding his successive birth and also his fate therein.

In the MaP, 10.17 Sumati, a young brahmin reveals to his father that he remembered the period of past ten thousand years and his numerous lives during that period. In this long chain of births he was born as a brahmana, a ksatriya a vaisya, a sudra and also as several insects, animals and birds.In his different births he was born as a rich man as well as a poor man, a master as a servant (MaP, 10.24).

**Return from Heaven/Hell**

It is believed that after exhausting most of his karmas by staying in heaven and hell, a man is again born on the earth. He returns to earth, when some of his karmas are still unexhausted. The MP, 39.2 states that after enjoying the fruit of his meritorious deeds, a man falls down on earthor goes to lower regions. With most of his religious merit exhausted, the status of this man is now similar to that of a penniless fellow. As a penniless man is abandoned by his companions and relatives, a man whose religious merit is exhausted, is deserted by gods in the heaven.

**Rebirth of the Pious**

The MaP (15.1 -42) provides the details of the different births the sinners get on account of their particular sins. It further states that the pious retain their noble character, when they are reborn on the earth. Thus, after his death the pious enjoys the pleasures of heaven till his merits last and with a little of his religious merit still with him he is born as a man with noble qualities. The BrahmaP, 225.6-7 states that when all their religious merit is exhausted, the pious have to leave heaven and be born on the earth, and they are born in rich and noble families.

According to the PP Srsti Khanda, 15.243-244 the meritorious people, after enjoying the deserved pleasures in heaven, are reborn on the earth. Similar ideas are contained in the PP, Uttara Khanda, 36.37. The PP Adi Khanda, 42.12, 44.11 state that the pious attain the leadership of the Jambu-dvipa. The PP (Srsti Khanda, 15.199-232) also tells that virtuous people are born on earth as kings and princes, rich, and handsome people and as glorious devotees. To Kalidasa, the city of Ujjayini is nothing less that a bright portion of heaven borne down to earth by the heaven-dwellers. They had to descend on the earth, when the stock of their religious-merit was nearly exhausted and they, with their remaining merits, had brought to earth this portion of the heaven (Meghadutam, 1.31).

**Rebirth of Sinner**

A sinner also, after having undergone the punishment in the hell is born on earth with some of his sins still unconsumed. Due to his remaining sins he is afflicted by diseases and suffers in many ways.His actions in this birth again take him to hell after his death. This process of births and deaths goes on till he has taken punishment for all his deeds (PP Bhumi Khanda, 52.30-31).

According to the MaP, 10.20 the sinners are born as insects and other lowly creatures and animals. After hundreds of such births he gets a human body again. The stages of his transmigration are worm -\> insects -\> fish -\> birds -\> animals -\> human being.Thus the sinners gradually migrate through the several inferior births until he is once more born as a man.

The VarahaP (198.57-60) provides that when the period of his punishment is over, the deceased becomes fixed objects. From the state of fixed objects he takes birth as animals, insects, birds, kine, etc. whereafter he regains a human body.

Thus the present life of a man is indicative of his past deeds, while his present deeds present an idea of his future life. The BhagP, 6.1,47states that just as the present time is an index of the characteristic of the other periods that precedes and succeeds it, similarly the present life (of human beings) indicates the righteous and sinful actions committed in the past and likely to be committed in the future existence as well.

According to the BhagP people experience pleasures or sufferings in this world in accordance with their meritorious or evil deeds. Wise men say that heaven and hell are here on the earth itself, for one may see creatures on the earth suffering from torments of hells (BhagP, 3.30.29). Thus in accordance with his good or evil deed the earth is the extended heaven and hell for man. This theory can reasonably explain the miserable plight of even the innocent people on earth. When Sita was abandoned by Rama, she could not think of any fault of her which could have brought to her such a sorrowful fate. She believed that it must be the result of the sins committed by her in the previous lives.

So long a man continues to do karma, he has to be reborn on the earth. In each new life he collects a new stock of karma and as the result again goes to heaven and hell and from there returns to the earth. This forms a circle of births and deaths. Even performing one’s duty gives special fruit, if done with a desire for its fruit. If they are performed without such desire, they bring total release (ViP, 6.7.36-38 cf. BG, 3.19). Thus niskama-karma is conductive to release. Performance of one's duties without the attachment to their fruit wears off merits and demerits and leads to liberation.

The three ways to the total liberation are karmayoga, bhaktiyoga and jnanayogaThe karmayoga consists of dedicating all actions to God with mind firmly fixed in Him: Performance of actions is like prayers to God. One is bound by the fruit of his actions, if they are not surrendered to God. Complete renunciation of righteous and evil deeds leads to moksa (VayuP, 17.7).

**Hells**

Though the Vedic seers did not give much though to the life after death, much lesser to the sufferings therein, the germs of the doctrine of karma-vipaka, i.e., fruction of action can be seen there. According to them a man is obliged to bear the fruit of his good or evil deeds. The RV mentions a special region for the pious deed (sukrtam lokam) as well as a dark abyss for the sinners.It also refers to a man attaining higher or lower regions on the basis of his good or evil deeds. The Brahmanic texts contain the doctrine of reward and punishment after death for the acts done by a man during his life on earth. The SB, 11.2.7.33 mentions that in the yonder world the good and bad deeds of man are weighed on a balance. He gets the reward, if his good deeds outweigh and is punished if the evil deeds outweigh. The doctrine of rebirth was not formulated in the early Vedic thought.

The concept of karma-vipaka is found to be well established in the Puranas.A man is believed to be compulsorily rewarded or punished for his good or bad actions respectively. The PP (Bhumi Khanda, 81.47) states that as a call goes to its mother only, though she may be standing in the midst of thousands of cows, similarly the fruit of good or bad action invariably goes to its doer only. The punishment came in the form of a life full of sorrows and deprivation on earth or a torturous life in one or more hells. The BhagP, 3.30.39 states that according to the wise men the heaven and hell are to be found here on earth, for one may see creatures suffering the torments of hells even on the earth.

A man is freed from the sins committed by him in two ways: (1) by making atonement during his life on earth, (2) by bearing the punishment awarded to him after his death.

In the BhagP, 6.1.68 Yama-dutas pronounce that since that sinner had not done anything to expiate his sins, he could be purified only by undergoing the prescribed punishment. The BhagP, states various means of making atonement for one's sins, e.g., asceticism (tapas), absolute continence (brahmacarya), control of mind (sama), restrain of senses (dama), giving gifts (tyaga), purity (sauca) and truth (satya) etc. (BhagP, 6.1.13; 11.5.9; 6.2.11).

**Atonement**

If a man, after committing a sin, realises his mistake and makes atonement for it, he is freed from the sin. There are several instances where a sin is made away by performing atonement. By killing the demon Vrtra Indra had incurred the sin of brahminicide. For this he sought expiation by leaving the heavenly throne to live in seclusion in the Manasa lake for one thousand years. After the expiry of this period he also performed one thousand horse-sacrifices (BhagP, 6.13.15; 18.21). Yudhisthira performed three horse-sacrifices to atone for the sin incurred by him due to the slaughter of his cousins in the Great War.

In the absense of expiation, the punishment becomes necessary. This is the only other way of getting retrieve from one’s sins. The man, after his death is punished with a stay in the torturous hells. The period of his stay and the type of hell are in accordance with the sin committed. After undergoing the punishment he gets freed from the sin.

Life in heaven is meant for joy and happiness on the basis of dharma observed here. There are hells where punishments are inflicted upon the dead on the basis of adharma committed by him when he was alive. The BhagP, 6.1.40ab states that performance of actions prescribed by Veda is dharma and adharma is its reverse.Hells are the result of violation of proper mode of conduct.

The ViP, 2.6 gives a philosophical garb to the concept of heaven and hell. It says that heaven is that which delights the mind and hell is that which gives it pain, hence vice is hell and virtue is heaven.

After death the sinners are taken to Yama by his assistants. Their fate is decided in the court (sabha) of Yama. The VarahaP, chapter 205.9-10 contains a dialogue between Yama and Citragupta in which the latter recommends the fate that the dead deserves for his acts.

The PP mentions that the sinners go to various hells, namely, Raurava, etc. at the command of YamaThe AgniP, 371.12 states that a sinner falls into horrible hells that are declared by Citragupta at the order of Yama.Several hells are described in the Puranas. The types of tortures in these hells are also described in detail.

**Location of the Hells**

According to ViP, 2.6 the hells are situated beneath the earth and beneath the waters. In the BhagP the hells are said to be situated in the abode of Yama. They are within the three worlds, in the south, below the earth and above the waters. Manes live there and Vaivasvata Yama is the king (BhagP, 5.26.5-6; 26). According to the VaP the hells are situated in the Puskara island which is the last and the farthest of the seven islands which encircle the Jumbu-dvipa. The Puskara island is fearsome and full of tortures.

According to the VaPthe earth is fifty crore yojanas in size and it floats on water like a boat. Brahma has established the Sumera Mountain; Prajapati named many islands (VaP, 11.31) and places and made Jambu-dvipa in its centre.This dvipa is encircled by seven oceans and six islands. The six islands are (1) Plaksa-dvipa, (2) Salmali-dvipa, (3) Kusa- dvipa, (4) Kraunca-dvipa, (5) Saka-dvipa, (6) Puskara-dvipa.

The seven oceans which separates them are;

- (1) Ksara-samudra (salt-water ocean)
- (2) Iksurasa-samudra (sugarcane-juice ocean)
- (3) Sura-samudra (wine ocean)
- (4) Ghrta-samudra (ghi ocean)
- (5) Dadhi-samudra (curds ocean)
- (6) Ksira-samudra (milk ocean)
- (7) Svahuda-samudra (sweet-water ocean)

Jambu-dvipa is one lakh yojanas in size, and these seven oceans and six islands encircle the Jambu-dvipa alternately. Each island/ocean is conceived to be double the size of the previous island/ocean. Hells are said to situated in the Puskara-dvipa.

**Number of Hells**

According to Sankaracarya the Vedanta Sutra, 3.1.15 refers to the seven types of hells, namely Raurava, etc. The MaP, 10.81 and the ViP, 1.6.41 also describe seven hells. The ViP 2.6 mentions twenty-eight hells. The BhagP, 5.26.6 also states that there are twenty-eight hells. There names, however, differ from those given in the ViP.

Some works enumerate twenty-one hells, e.g. ManuS, 4.88-90. ViDhP, 43.2-22, AgniP, 371.20-22.

The BrahmaP, 214.14-17; 51.203; 215.83 speaks of twenty-two hells and provides horrowing description of the torments of the hells. Elsewhere the BrP (chapter 22) names twenty-five hells.

However, the BrahmaP (215.82-83), ViDhP (quoted by P.V. Kane in HDS Vol. IV, p. 164) and the GarudaP (Preta Khanda, 3.3) declare that there are thousands, and lakhs and crores of hells.

**Hells in Markandeya Purana**

Each hell is a place of punishment for a particular set of crimes. Most of them have there own methods of inflicting tortures. The MdP (chapter 10; 14; 15) deals with the karma-vipaka in great details and mentions several places and modes of tortures. The hells, distinctly named and described in MaP, 10.81; 12.4-48 are seven in number. They are:

- (1) Raurava
- (2) Maharaurava
- (3) Yamas
- (4) Nikrntana
- (5) Apratistha
- (6) Asipatravana
- (7) Tapta-kumbha

Another hell named KalaSutra is mentioned in MaP, 14.89.

**Raurava**

It is two thousand yojanas in size. Heaps of coals constantly bum there making it extremely hot. The sinner is scorched in this heat, as he is made to walk on the hot surface. Being burnt by the flames he runs about here and there. Only after he was walked one thousand yojanas a sinner is freed from this hell. But this is not the end of his sufferings, for he is now sent to other hells. After finishing his period of punishment in the other hells too he is born as a bird and also as worm insect, fly, mosquito, cow, horse, elephant, tree, creeper and many lowly births (MaP, 10.84-89).

A person who gives false witness, speaks untruth, kills his father or a brahmin, or a cow, one who kidnaps a woman, one who has illicit relationship with the wife or daughter of one’s preceptor falls in the Raurava hell (MaP, 10.81-82).

*Maharaurava*

As the name denotes it is extremely terrible. It is twelve square yojanas in size.

Eating prohibited things, cheating a friend, breaking the faith of the master, abandoning his own wife and having lust for other women, destroyer of a path, a pond or a garden — these are the sins which take a man to be burnt in this hell.

The Maharaurava hell is extremely hot due to its copper surface which is constantly heated by fire. The sinners are kept here with their heads and feet bound. They move by rolling and are preyed upon by crows, herons, wolves, owls, scorpians and vultures, etc. (MaP, 12.3-9). They have to stay there for thousands of years (MaP, 12.11).

*Yamas*

This hell is bitterly cold and dark. People suffer due to hunger and thirst. Afflicted with cold the sinners run about in total darkness and seek refuge in clasping each other. The wind laden with snow particles pierces their bones. The hungry sinners feed on marrow and blood dropping therefrom.

The killer of a brother, a child, a cow and the destroyer of water and food falls in this hell. He has to remain there till all his sins are washed away (a man consumes his sins by undergoing the punishment) (MaP, 12.13-18ab).

*Nikrntana*

In this hell the sinner is mounted on an incesssantly revolving (potter’s) wheel and is cut by the strings of ’Fate’ which are borne on the finger’s of Yama’s servants. The bodies of the sinners are cut again and again, because the parts of their bodies join together everytime they are cut. In this manner they suffer the pain of being cut again and again for thousands of years till their sins are consumed. A man of bad character, one who is disobedient to his preceptor and one who does not respect a person as his guru even when one was taught something by him—these sinners are destined to fall in this hell (MaP, 12.18d-24).

*Apratistha*

There are jars, wheels and ropes in this hell, which cause intolerable pain to the sinners for a thousand years. The sinners are bound to the jars and well-rope like a jar in water. Men whirl around continuously spitting out blood. One who creates obstructions to a brahmin engaged in his work is thrown into this hell (MaP, 12.25-30).

*Asipatravana*

This hell is of one thousand yojanas in size. This is a forest of sword-leaves. It also has a blazing fire. Winds blow and hurl the sinners on the sword-leaves of the forest. In this way they are cut into pieces again and again. Terrifying dogs, with their large teeth and as fierce as tigers, tear away the limbs from the bones of these wailing sinners (MaP, 12-39). One who poses obstacles for a celibate, an observer of a vow or an ascetic is thrown into this hell (MaP, 12-31).

*Taptakumbha*

This hell contains hot-pitchers. These pitchers are filled with oil and ferrous-powder and they are constantly healed.

In these pitchers the Yama-dutas lower the sinners with their heads downwards. There are vultures who pull them out and drop again in the pitchers. Vigorously stirred up by Yama’s servants the sinners are churned in the pool of oil. (MaP, 12.43-44).

One who violates the sanctity of Dharma sastras and holy places, one who does evil to a friend and one who abandones his innocent wife is thrown into this hell.

Kalasutra

One more hell is mentioned in the MaP, 14.89. In this hell the sinners are cut into pieces.

Apart from these hells MaP, 13,14 also describe in great detail the fruit of different sins committed by a man. Due to his sins a man has to suffer various types of tortures like that of swallowing burning coals, being flung down from high mountains or being eaten up by worms.

**Hells in Visnu Purana**

The ViP (2.6) enumerates twenty-eight hells. They are (1) Raurava, (2) Sukara, (3) Rodha, (4) Tala, (5) Visasana. (6) Mahajvala, (7) Taptakumbha, (8) Lavana. (9) Vimohana. (10) Rudhirandha, (11) Vaitarani, (12) Krmisa, (13) Krmibhojana,(14) Asipatravana, (15) Krsna, (16) Lalabhaksa, (17) Daruna, (18) Puyavaha, (19) Papa, (20) Vahnijvala, (21) Adhosiras. (22) Samdamsa, (23) KalaSutra, (24) Yamas, (25) Avici. (26) Svabhojana, (27) Apratistha and (28) another Avici.

These hells and many other places of torture are the provinces of Yama’s kingdom. They are terrible with the instruments of torture and the blazing fire therein. Sinners are hurled into these hells where they are tortured with burning sand, fire and weapons. Some are cut with saws, some roasted in forges, some chopped with axes and some buried in the ground, some are mounted on the stakes, thrown to wild animals in order to be devoured by them, gnawed by vultures, torn by tigers and fried in oil.Different hells and the types of sinners punished therein are mentioned as follows:

- (1) Raurava (dreadful) — for bearing false witness.
- (2) Rodha (obstruction) —for causing abortion plandering a town, killing a cow and for strangling a man.
- (3) Sukara (swine) — for murdering a brahmin, stealing gold, drinking wine. A person assisting in these is also punished in this hell.
- (4) Tala—for murdering a vaisya or sudra; adultery with the preceptor's wife.
- (5) Tapta-kumbha (the hell with heated pitchers) — for incest with sister, for killing an ambassador.
- (6) Tapta-loka — for selling one's wife, a gaoler, a horse dealer, and one who deserts his adherents fall into this.
- (7) Mahajvala (of great flame) — for committing incest with daughter-in-law or daughter.
- (8) Lavana (i.e., salt hell) — for being disrespectful to one's spiritual guide, for abusing the betters, for selling the Vedas.
- (9) Vimohana — for stealing.
- (10) Krmibhaksa— one who scorns his father, brahmins and the gods and also one who spoils the precious gems.
- (11) Krmisa (hell of worms) — for practising magic-rites with an intention to harm others.
- (12) Lalabhaksa— for eating meals before offering it to the gods, to the manes and to the guest.
- (13) Vedhaka - this hell is for the maker of arrows, lances, swords and other weapons, where the sinners are pierced).
- (14) Visasana — one who offers in sacrifices improper objects and one who is observer of stars (for the prediction of events)
- (15) Adhomukha (head-inverted) — he who takes unlawful gifts goes to this hell.
- (16) Puyavaha — who eats by himself the sweets mixed with rice, and a brahmin who sells lac, flesh, liquor, sesamum, or salt, or one who commits violence falls into this hell. Those who rear cats, cocks, goats, dogs, hogs or birds, also fall in this hell.
- (17) Rudhirandha — public performers, fishermen, a poisoner, an informer and one who lives by his wife's prostitution are punished in the Rudhirandha hell.
- (18) Vaitarani — a treacherous friend, one who destroys a bee-hive is condemned to this hell.
- (19) Krsna — one who trespasses on others land, is impure and who deceives others is punished in this dark hell.
- (20) Asipatravana — he who wantonly cuts down trees goes to this hell.
- (21) Vahnijvala.— this is the hell of fiery flame. One who tenders sheep and hunts deer go to this hell.
- (22) Samdamsa (the hell of pincers) — one who violates his vow is punished in this hell.
- (23) Svabhojana — the religious student who sleep in the day and those who are instructed in sacred literature by their children go to this hell where the feed upon dogs (or eaten by dogs!).

The ViP, describes 28 hells in all.

**Hells in Vamana Purana**

According to the VamanaP there are twenty-one hells

(VamanaP, 11.50). The names of these hells are given as follows:

- (1) Raurava: It is two thousand yojanas in size and is full of burning coal.
- (2) Maharaurava: It is double the size of Raurava hell. Its floor is of hot copper. One who steals gold, commits brahminicide or adultery with the preceptor's wife, grabs others’ cows and land, one who kills a cow, a woman or a child; a brahmin who sells cow, Soma or the Vedas (teaches Vedas for money) one who stays impure, one who does not perform his nitya (daily) and naimittika (occasional duties) and one who offers false witness all these fall in the Maharaurava (i.e., extremely terrible) hell (VamanaP, 12.38-40).

Such a sinner has to spend ten thousand years in each of these hells Raurava, Tamisra, AndhaYamas, Asipatravana, Ghatiyantra and then Taptakumba.

The other hells are,

- (3) Tamisra
- (4) Andhatamisra — this is double the size of Tamisra
- (5) Kalas Citra
- (6) Apratistha
- (7) Ghatiyantra
- (8) Asipatravana — it is seventy-two thousand yojanas
- (9) Taptakumbha
- (10) Kutasalmali
- (11) Karapatra
- (12) Svanabhojana
- (13) Samdamsa
- (14) Lohapinda
- (15) Karambhasikata
- (16) A terrible, salty river
- (17) Krmibhojana
- (18) Vaitarani
- (19) Sonitpuyabhojana
- (20) Cakraka — this is sharp like blades

Another hell is mentioned in VamanaP, 12.37, this is Vrkabhaksa hell.

**Hells in Bhagavata Purana**

According to the BhagP, 5.26.7 there are twenty-eight types of hells. They are situated between the three worlds in the south direction, below the earth and above the water. Manes live there. Vaivasvata Yama, the lord of the Fathers, rules there. He is helped by his assistants who never transgress their lord's command. Yama inflicts punishments according to the crime.

**The twenty-eight hells mentioned in the** BhagP, are — Tamisra, Andhatamisra, Raurava, Maharaurava, Kumbhipaka, KalaSutra, Asipatravana, Sukaramukha, Andhakupa, Krmibhojana, Samdamsa, Taptasurmi, Vqjrakantakasalmali, Vaitarani, Puyoda, Pranarodha, Visasana, Lalabhaksa, Sarameyadana, Avici and Ayahpana, Ksarakardama, Raksoganabhojana, Dandasuka, Sulaprota Avatanirodhana, Paryavartana and Sucimukha(5.26.7-36).

*Tamisra*

A person who seizes others' property, progeny or woman is sent to this hell. Another hell called Samdamsa is for a person who steals or grabs the gold or jewellery of a brahmin or of any other person.

*Andhatamisra*

This is a place of absolute darkness. A man who deceives someone and molests other’s wife is sent to suffer in this hell. Another hell Taptasurmi is enjoined for a person practising illicit relations. For such grave sins (MS, 11.54). Manu prescribes an expiatory rite. "The seducer of the preceptor's wife shall sleep on hot iron bed admitting his guilt or shall embrace the hot (iron) image of the lady. Death verily is his purifier” (MS, 11.103).

**Raurava**

Raurava is for a person who is always engrossed in selfish motives, and who troubles others for himself or his family. These victims become Rurus (fierce animals) and torment him.

**Maharaurava**

Maharaurava is styled on the same lines. The Khumbhipaka is the hell for a person who kills animals for eating them. In this hell Yama’s assistants throw the sinners into the boiling oil.

The KalaSutra is the hell for a person who insults his parents, brahmins and the sacred texts. A man who, unless in utter difficulty, adopts the religion of the 'non-believers of the Vedas' is destined to go to the Asipatravana. Sukaramukha is the hell for a man who uses his authority for punishing innocent person and who awards corporal punishment to a brahmin. The Andhakupa is for those who kill insects.

**Krmibhojana**

The hell Krmibhojana. is for a person who does not perform any of the five great sacrifices, nor gives anything to others. Various kinds of worms trouble the sinners here.

**Vajrakantakasalmali**

In Vajrakantakasalmali the sinner is pierced by thorns. A man is dropped in this hell for dealing badly with others.

**Vaitarani**

Vaitarni is a river-hell. It is full of blood, hair, bones, etc. The hot water of this stinking river moves swiftly carrying bones

and hair, etc. If a king or his official breaks any code of morals he is thrown in this river.

*Puyoda*

Puyoda is the hell for a person who does not follow the rules of purity. Pranarodha is enjoined for persons who though belonging to a higher varna came dogs and donkeys and who kill the animals that are not ordained to be killed.

*Visasana*

Visasana is for those who kill animals in mock sacrifices.

*Sarameyadana*

Sarameyadana is the hell for the theives, or kings and his officials who burn people’s houses, grab their property and poison others. Yama’s attendants become dogs and bite the sinner in this hell.

*Aviciman*

Aviciman is for there who lie as witness. When a brahmin (man or woman) takes wine, and a ksatriya or vaisya drinks Soma, he is destined to go to Ayahpana Yama-dutas thrust red hot iron in the mouths of the sinners.

A low-caste man who thinks himself to be of a higher caste and does not respect the people of the higher caste goes to the Ksarakardama hell. Those who sacrifice a human being in the human sacrifice are sent to the hell called Raksoganabhojana. One who deceives others goes to the Sulaprota. Those who trouble others go to Dandasuka. Those who imprison others in dark places (caves, etc.) go to Avatanirodhana hell. He is imprisoned here in the smoke of the poisonous hell-fires.

One who does not treat his guests properly goes to the Paryavartana hell. Here he is bitten by sharp-beaked birds. Sucimukha is for those who do not trust anybody and think themselves to be very wealthy. Here, Yama’s men sew all the limbs of the sinners.

After describing these various hells the BhagP, 6.26.37 says that there are hundreds and thousands of hells in the region of Yama. Various types of hell for various sins are described here. The possibility of new types of sins under new circumstances cannot be ruled out and therefore BhagP adds that there are many more hells, though they are not specially mentioned.Sinners exhaust their sins by undergoing the punishment after which he is born again. Thus a stay in hell purifies man and he does not carry the load of his sins to his next birth.

Apart from the two paths, one leading to heaven and the other to hell, there is another path for human being and that is the path to moksa, i.e., the total liberation (IInd Skandha). Thus the moksa is considered to be the highest fate of man from where he does not return to earth and he gets totally liberated from the cycle of births and deaths.

**Hells in Garuda Purana**

The GP describes the torturous journey of the sinners as they are taken to the kingdom of Yama. The GP, 2.57 gives the list of the names of the hells as follows:

1\. Raurava 14. Asipatravana   2. Sukara 15. Krsna   3. Vodha 16. Nanabhaksa   4. Tala 17. Puyavaha   5. Visasana 18. Papa   6. Mahajvala. 19. Vahnijvala.   7. Taptakumbha 20. Samdamsa   8. Lavana 21. KrsnaSutra   9. Virndhita 22. Yama   10. Rudhir 23. Avici   11. Vaitarani 24.Svanhojana   12.Krmisa 25. Apratistha   13.Krmibhojana 26.Usnavici

**Hells in Varaha Purana**

The VarahaP (198.1-2) mentions eight hells. They are (1) Tapta, (2) Mahatapta, (3) KalaSutraka, (4) Andhakara, (5) Andhakarpara, (6) Raurava and Maharaurava. Here the sinners are burnt, severed, pricked, torn and tortured in many ways (VarahaP, 198.19). It also mentions Asipatravana and Srngatakavana and Kutasalmali. The sinners have to cross a river Karambhavaluka which is of hundred yojanas in size and very dreadful.

Vaitaranl very big in size and full of fierce creatures, and Yama nadi, a river full of hot pus are other two rivers mentioned in the VarahaP (198.26-28) where the sinners are trortured.

It is to be noted here that some hells, e.g., Raurava, Tapta-kumbha, (or Kumbhi-paka), Asipatravana, KrsnaSutra (or KalaSutra) and Apratistha, etc. are mentioned in most of the Puranas.

**Worship of the Manes**

It is traditionally believed that the death is not the ultimate end of one's existence. After the completion of the funeral rites the deceased joins the rank of the Fathers. The Fathers, even though they are far removed from the world of the living, take keen interest in the welfare and happiness of their descendants on earth. They are remembered and invited with due honour to the sraddhas which are performed to please them. They are also invited on the happy occasions to shower on their descendants their blessings and also share their joy. It is obvious that they are still considered a part of the family and respected as the family-elders. It is expected that the Fathers would be pleased with the offerings made to them and would grant prosperity and happiness. An element of fear can also be seen here, since it is believed that the discontented Fathers can also cause harm.

The word Fathers (pitarah) is used in two senses viz.

- (1) A man’s three immediate ancestors.
- (2) The early and ancient ancestors of the human race that were supposed to inhabit a separate world (loka) by themselves.

**Classification of Fathers**

The Fathers are divided in many categories, some of them are (1) Agnisvatta, (2) Barhisad, (3) Havismat, (4) Somapa, (5) Usmapa. (6) Ajyapa (MP, 102.20-21).

Agnisvatta and Barhisad are pitrs of Rgvedic antiquity.

**Ancestor Worship**

The love, gratitude and respect to the deceased members of this family prompt people to remember and present solemn offerings to them. Sraddha or the rites to the manes is a form of ancestor-worship. On the occasion of a sraddha the ancestors are invoked and conceived as actually present.

At the time of some approaching festivity like a marriage; or on the occasion of the birth of a son it is natural to express gratitude and to assume that the Fathers also share the joy of those happy occasions.

The vocable sraddha is derived from srat + dha i.e., truth, reality and dha to hold, therefore, it signifies the belief in truth. One who performs sraddha does so with utmost faith in it, with the conviction of these rites being true.

The importance of the sraddha is highly stressed. The Manu S.. 3.203 says the rites to the Fathers are even of more importance than those to the gods.

Sraddha-rituals are performed by a man for his deceased father, grandfather and great-grandfather. Offerings which are comprised of rice-balls, and water mixed with sesamum, are offered to the Fathers. The efficacy of the sraddha depends upon, among other things, on the proper oblations. A verse quoted by Narayana the commentator of AGS, 1.96 mentioned the following ten substances as fit for the (havis) offering. viz. milk, curds, gruel (yavagu), clarified butter (sarpis), cooked rice, (odana) and husked rice (tandula), flesh, sesamum oil and water.There is, however, “general prohibition against offering of flesh as a fire oblation in the homa (fire-rite) at a sraddha”.

Madhu (honey) and sesamum seed are specially recommended for sraddha. A simplified form of the sacrifice to the Fathers is making libations with water for them (pitrtarpana).

**Classification of Sraddhas**

The sraddhas have been classified variously. One classification divides them as (1) Nitya, (2) Namittika and Kamya. The Nitya sraddha is performed on a certain or fixed occasion viz. everyday or on every amavasya, etc. In these sraddhas no oblation of invocation or the Vaisvadeva-worship is necessary. It is done thrice daily — once before the daily meal, once after the noon rite and once at night again. The sraddha is done in the form of feeding a qualified brahmin everyday for one year at least (Apastamba, 2.18.6-19), as this would give permanent satisfaction to the Fathers.

The food prepared for the sraddha may, after being offered to a brahmin as a representative of the Fathers, be given to the ascetics and acolytes. The Apastamba Smrti (2.18.6-19) cited for the daily sraddha directs that it should be performed in a pure place, outside the village using new vessels for preparing and serving the food. After the rites these vessels are given to the brahmins who are fed in them.

**Naimittika Sraddhas**

These sraddhas are performed on occasions which are not fixed, such as on the birth of a son.

**Kamya Sraddhas**

These sraddhas performed with the desire to get some reward are called Kamya sraddhas e.g. a sraddha performed on Krttika or Rohini by one who desires heaven or progeny.In the true spirit of Dharma, a Kamya sraddha is an inferior act. As said earlier the sraddhas are performed with sraddha (faith) in order to gratify the Fathers and in the Kamya sraddha only follow the ritual, not the spirit of the sraddha.

**Parvana Sraddhas**

They are performed on the parvans hence this designation.Some of the Paro ana sraddhas are:

- 1\. Anvaharya: It is always performed on the amavasya.
- 2\. Sadharana: This can be performed even by one who is not invested with the sacred thread (MP, 17.63).

**Ekoddista Sraddhas**

The sraddha done for a person who has recently died is performed only for one individual in contrast with other sraddhas which are performed for all Fathers in general, and therefore, it is called Ekoddista In this sraddha gods are not invoked (as in nitya-sraddhas) and the movements are from right to left and with the sacred thread kept on the right shoulder. In this sraddha the dead is not addressed or referred to as Pitr since he does not unite with the Pitrs until the sapindikarana is done. The first Ekoddista is done on the eleventh day after the death of the person for whom it is done. The brahmin is invited and offerings of food and flowers are made. He is also given clothes, umbrella and shoes, etc. (Visnusmrti, 20.1-11). The ekoddista sraddha is performed every month (on the date of death) for one year, after which the sapindikarana is performed.

**Sapindikarana**

This sraddha is performed for the peace of the deceased and to unite him with Fathers in their world. Apart from getting an honourable title of Pitr the sapindikarana guarantees the supply of the sraddha benefit to the deceased, even if his direct descendants fail to perform further sraddhas. He gets the offerings made to all the manes is general.

This ritual is ideally done after one year when all ekoddista (sixteen in all) have been performed, but sapindikarana-rites on the twelfth day after death are also sanctioned by Visnusmrti (21.12-21).

Vrddhi sraddha, performed at the time of an auspicious occasion like marriage, etc. cannot take place in the family, if the sapindikarana rites are due.

The ritual of sapindikarana is symbolic. Four vessels of arghya are kept, one for the recently dead and one each for his three immediate ancestors, i.e., father, grandfather and great-grandfather. Similarly balls of rice are offered to the deceased and his three ancestors. With the recitation of the mantras the arghya meant for the deceased is sprinkled over the other three vessels. Similarly the rice ball offered to the deceased is divided in three parts and offered to the three ancestors. The father and the grandfather of the dead are invoked to unite with the deceasd. By the sapindikarana rites the third ancestor of the dead gets released.

**Vrddhisraddha**

As the ancestors are also believed to be a part of the family, it is natural that they too are remembered on the occasion of happiness and an auspicious ceremony in the family. The sraddhas being a means of inviting and pleasing the Fathers, they are preformed on such occasions too and they are called Vrddhi sraddhas or Abhyudayikas. They are also designated Nandisraddhas. The ViP, 3.13.56 states that on the occasion of the wedding of a son or daughter, moving into a new house, birth of a son, the naming ceremony or the cudakarma, etc. the householder should worship the nandimukha ancestors. Thus no important event should pass without performance of the nandisraddha as the preliminary rites.

The Vrddhisraddha is different from the usual rites to the Manes. Firstly the sacred thread is worn in a normal way, i.e., as in the rites to the gods and not placed on the right shoulder, movements are from right to left and not from left to right as in other sraddhas. The pindas or balls are not made of cooked rice nor the offereings made with svadha.

It may be observed that, with the doctrine of karma- vipaka and rebirth in mind, a doubt is expressed regarding the utility of the sraddhas. It is found difficult to believe that the offering made to the ancestors could reach them. A similar doubt is raised in the MP, 19.2 that how could the food eaten by the brahmin at a sraddha feast could be enjoyed by the Fathers who might have been reborn. The following verses (MP, 3-9) provide the answer. It is said here that the father, grandfather and great-grandfather are identified with Vasus, Rudras and Adityas respectively. It also says that the name and the gotra of the Fathers mentioned at the time of sraddha, the mantras uttered and the faith in these rites carry to the Fathers the offerings made to them. If the ancestor has become a god (by his good deeds) the food offered reaches him in the form of nectar, if he has become a daitya it reaches him in the form of various enjoyments, if he has become a beast, the offering become grass for it and if he has become snake, the offerings wait on him as wind (it is believed that the serpents subsist on wind). Thus even in the case of rebirth the offerings made to the Fathers gratify them.

## 9. Yama: The Preacher and Law-Giver

**Yama: The Preacher**

A facet of Yama's personality as a preacher which is reflected in the KathaUp, is also seen in the Mbh and the Puranas. There are several instances in the Mbh as well as in the Puranas, where Yama is seen preaching some kind of higher knowledge and philosophy to others. Some of these passages where Yama is depicted as a preacher project him as a great moral teacher. In others he is sometimes seen describing the merits of charity, while in some others he is telling about the supremacy of Visnu (BhagP, 6.3.12-24) or Siva (Mbh, 13.71.178-179). In the Mbh also he plays the part of a great moral teacher. The Mbh, 12.127.4-11 contains a dialogue between Yama and the sage Gautama. On being asked by Gautama Yama tells him the way to get released from the debt which one owes to one’s parents. He also tells Gautama the way to attain the divine regions which are hard to be obtained. This story is narrated to Yudhisthira by Bhisma who, as desired by Yudhisthira, was instructing him about Dharma. According to this story the sage Gautama performed penance for sixty thousand years in his asrama on the Pariyatra mountain. Once a lokapala happened to come to Gautama's asrama and see the sage performing severe austerities. Brahmarsi Gautama, by the power of his penance, discerned that he was none other than the god Yama. With folded hands he approached 'Yamadeva' who welcomed the sage and said "I am Dharmaraja. What good shall I do for you?” On being asked like this Gautama took it as an opportunity and asked Yama to tell him the way for getting freed from the debt towards his parents. He also wanted to know how one can attain heaven, i.e., the regions of the pious and Yama replied to these queries.

In the KathaUp. Yama reveals to the earnest seeker, i.e., Naciketas, the secret knowledge relating to the nature of the Supreme Self and death.Aversion of this story is also found in the Mbh, 13.71-74. In the Mbh story, the young boy Naciketa is told by his father to bring the gift-objects from the river-bank. Those gift-objects were, in the meantime, swept away in the river. Naciketa went to the river and not finding any gift-objects there, reported to his father that he did not see any items at the river-bank. The father, was extremely angry on hearing that the boy could not 'see' the items which he told him to fetch. He cursed Naciketa, “See Yama (Yamam pasya)." Naciketa immediately fell down as dead. The father realised his mistake and his anger gave way to deep remorse and anguish.

All night the father wept over his son, who was at last, revived by his tears. Greatly pleased on seeing his son alive Uddalaki asked the boy about his night-long trance. Naciketa related that he had actually gone to Yama's region. Yama greated him in his court (Vaivasvati sabhd). There Naciketa, at his own wish, was shown by Yama the regions of the pious, i.e., those who have given proper gifts to the brahmins.

In both the versions (the KathaUp. version and the Mbh version) Yama is depicted as a preacher. In the KathaUp he is seen eleborating the nature of the Supreme Self and eschatological doctrines whereas in the Mbh, he is louding the merits of giving gifts to the brahmins, but the character of Yama as Dharma is present in both legends. Though “the mysteries are different but in both the instances Yama is the all knowing preceptor revealing mystic truths to the eager enquirer'.Yama also tells Naciketa about the intricate topic of ayu ksaya, Mbh, 13.104.72-76.

Yama also taught the merits of charity to the brahmin named Sarmin (Mbh, 13.69). Yama-dutas had brought him to Yamaloka by mistake. On seeing Sarmin coming near him. Yama rose up from his throne and greeted him. At Sarmin's request Yama also told him about the merits of charity.

In the MP, 49.68 it is mentioned that Yama, pleased with Janamejaya, imparted to him the highest knowledge about redemption.In the BrVaiP. 2.23ff contain the story of Satyavan and Savitri. Here too Yama granted self-chosen boons to Savitri and additionally he gave a discourse on karma, karma-vipaka, heaven, hell and the total emancipation. (BrVaiP. 2.20; 2.27-34).

In the Ajamila-episode in th BhagP. 6.1.20ff Yama is seen praising Lord Visnu with utmost humility. He relates of his dutas the glory of Lord Visnu and commanded them not to go near a worshipper of Visnu. According to the Mbh, 13.71.178-179 the Siva-sahasranama was taught to Yama by Lord Narayana and he instructed Naciketa in chanting this eulogy of Siva. Thus. Yama is seen accepting the supremacy of Visnu and Siva.

The BhagP, 7.2.36-56 contains a story about Yama preaching the relatives of a dead king about the immortality of soul and the futility of crying for the dead.

The story goes like this: Lord Hari, assuming the form of a boar, killed Hiranyaksa, the brother of Hiranyakasipu. Hiranyakasipu, filled with grief and anger told the danavas to destroy everything which was related with the worship of Visnu. Then he tried to console his mother and other relatives grieving over Hiranyaksa's death saying that the soul is immortal. Hiranyakasipu tells them about an ancient legend which contains a conversation between Yama and the relatives of a dead king. In the Usinara city a king named Suyajna was killed in a battle. His queens were deeply grieved. They wailed and grasped their dead husband, and were not prepared to allow the funeral-rites to begin.

As the sun set in the sky, Yama approached them in the form of a boy and told them the impropriety of weeping for a man who has (at his death) returned to the unmanifest state from which he has come (originally).He also said that the Lord at his own will creates, protects and destroys the universe. Bodies of all species come into existence and perish at a particular time according to the karma. But the soul, though it inhabits the Prakrti (the corporeal body), is not affected by the attributes of prakrti (body) and he is different from it. This corporeal body of the purusa is different from the soul, just as the house is different from the dweller. Like bubbles made of water, pots made of earth, this body which is formed out of the elements of time also undergoes modification and perishes. Just as fire which remains latent in a piece of wood, is different from it, or the vital breath existing in the body remains different from it, or the sky pervading all things does not stick to anything, similarly the soul is distinct from body. It is foolish to cry over the dead. He can neither see you, hear you nor give a reply, The principle which is the master and controller of senses is the atman. and it is distinct from the body and the life-breath (mukhya-prana). The atman takes up a body consisting of the five elements (bhutas), sense and mind, and regards himself as one with them. But with the power of his wisdom and knowledge he casts them off at will.

He also gives an example of a Kulinga bird who was grieving for his mate caught in a net. As it kept sorrowing over this situation, it was totally unaware of the fowler who was aiming at it and was pierced by the fowler's arrow. Yama tells the queens that like Kulinga they do not see their own destruction. Even if they wailed for a hundred years, they could not get their husband back (so they should think about their own lives that lie ahead).

In this way Yama in the form of child admonished all the relatives of Suyajna and made them realise that everything was transient and unreal.

**Yama-gita**
