There are also some dialogues between Yama and (mostly) his measengers in which Yama is been as a preacher. These dialogues(i.e., ViP, 3.7. AgniP, chapter 382 etc.) are called Yamagitas.

The learn gita as a literary form may be trailed to gatha or gita mentioned in earlier literature. They are connected with some important event and a discourse of an authority on the relevant topic. It may conveniently be inferred that in olden days. It may have been a popular custom to commemorate events of importance illustrating royal valour (or wisdom) by means of popular songs knowns as gatha or gita.

The AgniP devotes the chapter 382 to Yamagita, which was, as its name denotes, told by Yama, the god of death, to Naciketa.The story of Naciketas and his dialogue with Yama can be traced back to the KathaUp. When Naciketas reached Yama’s abode, he had to wait for three nights before he could see Yama since Yama was away. When Yama returned, he thought that it was a great offence that a brahmin was kept waiting. As a compensation he asked Naciketas to choose three boons. After being granted two self-chosen boons Naciketas, as the third boon, wanted to know whether man existed after death or death was the end of a man's existence. On finding in Naciketas a true seeker of knowledge. Yama revealed to him the secret of man's existence after death and also about the final emanicipation, which can be achieved by realising the identity of the individual soul with the Supreme Self (Brahman).

The Yamagita (AgniP. 382) begins with referring to the doctrine of the various teachers about the spiritual upliftment. Yama said that due to his utter foolishness man, without realising that his own existence is momentary, wishes for worldly pleasures and possessions.The doctrines of other teachers are also mentioned here. Kapila stressed that self-realisation was much greater and more beneficial than the gratification of one’s senses. The holy sage Pancasikha (382.4) declares that the feeling of equality towards all and keeping oneself free from desires was highly beneficial to man.According to Gangavisnu (382.5) true knowledge is the correct perspective of the miseries of birth, youth and old age, and it is the highest good in life. In the opinion on Janaka (382.6) guarding himself the threefold evil (i.e., Adhyatmika, Adhidaivika and Adhibhautika) is the best thing for a man. According to Jaigisavya (382.8) performing one’s duty, as laid down by the Vedas is highly beneficial for man. According to Sanaka (382.10) the knowledge gained after abandoning all desires leads one to Brahman.

The Visnu cult supplies the basis for the philosophy of the Agni Purana. In the 382.12 Visnu is called Brahman (brahmana visnu sarnjnena).Reaching Visnu is the most desired goal of the man (nasti visnu samam dhyeyah — 382.14).

Whosoever remembers Visnu at the time of his death gains oneness with the Lord (382.16). A man can reach to Visnu by performing holy deeds, taking dips in water at holy places, by meditation, fasts, worships and listening to holy discourses.

The physical body is compared to a chariot of which the intellect is the charioteer, the mind is the reins, the senses are the horses. Self or atman is the occupant. This imagery is traceable to the KathaUp.

The man, who does not realise this knowledge, never attains the region of Visnu amd falls in the cycle of births and deaths. The man, who gain this knowledge, reaches Visnu and therefore, is not born again (23, 24). He, who know that his intellect is the charioteer which controls the reins (mind), crosses the path and reaches the highest abode of Visnu. The objects of senses are higher than the senses, the mind is higher than the objects, the intellect is higher than the mind, and the mahat is higher than the intellect (26,27), the unmanifested self is greater than mahat, and purusa is greater than the unmanifested self. Nothing is higher than the purusa (the Supreme Self). The hidden Supreme Self is not realised by all. Only those with sharp intellect and sharp vision can attain the knowledge of the Supreme Self (28, 29).

By knowledge, worship and yogic poses, by meditation, samadhi and realisation of the fact that ‘I am Brahman' (aham brahmasmi), a jivatman becomes liberated and gets merged in Brahman. This is beautifully expressed with the allegory of an empty earthen pot. When the pot is broken, the sky inside the pot becomes one with the sky outside it; in the same manner when the body of an enlightened man falls (at his death), his soul becomes one with the Supreme Self. The highest goal of the jiva or individual soul is to realise that he is Brahman, and this is possible only through knowledge.

The Yamagita. in the VisnuP, 3.7 consists of a dialogue between Maitreya and Parasara. Maitreya asks Parasara that by performing what pure actions people are freed from subjection to Yama. At this Parasara asks him to listen what Bhisma had said to Nakula this connection. Bhisma relates to Nakula a dialogue between Yama and one of his assistants;

Beholding his duta, with a noose in his hand, approach, Yama said in his ears; Never bring anyone here who has obtained the shelter of the slayer of Madhu. i.e., Lord Krsna, for I am the lord of all spirits but not of the spirits of those who are devoted to Visnu. I was appointed by Brahma, honoured by immortals to judge the good and evil conduct to mankind. Hari is my Lord, and I am not independent, for he can mete out punishment to me. Though gold (in reality) is one substance, it appears in many forms like bracelets, ear-rings, etc., so Hari, though he is one, appears many gods, animals and men.

Yama asks his dutas to avoid a man who is freed from his sins due to his devotion of Visnu, self-control and moral discipline. Yama's speech is followed by along eulogy of Visnu.

In the above-mentioned passage Yama is also seen instructing the non-dual entity of the Supreme Self and also its presence in all beings, i.e., gods, men and animals. The non-duality of Brahman is also beautifully expressed with the help of an allegory. Yama says that although gold is one substance, but it appears diversified as bracelets, ear-rings and tiaras, in the same way Hari, although he is one appears many as gods, men and animals.There is a striking similarity of this expression with that found in the ChaUp, 6.1.5. Here Aruni in the process of instructing his son Svetaketu about the oneness of the soul, explains the point by similar allegories. The ChaUp, 6.1.5 states "as on knowledge of a single lump of gold one knows that all articles of gold indicated by different words are mere variations of form of the same substance; in the same way the Supreme Being is manifested in various ways. Thus in this passage Yama is described as an instructor of non-dualism. Here he appears very near to his Vedic personality of a preacher of philosophy who imparted the secret knowledge to Naciketas."

There are several other instances where Yama is seen explaining to his duta about who could not be taken to his region (ViP, 2.66; 3.7.5, 13.20). It is also said that Yama, Yama-dutas, Yama-pasa (Yama’s noose) Yama-danda (Yama’s club) and Yama-yatana (the torments inflicted by Yama) can bring no harm to a man whose heart is totally devoted to Lord Krsna. Different versions of story of Naciketa are found in the TB, 3.11.81; KathaUp, (vide chapter V) Mbh, 13.71 and VarahaP, chapter 193-213.

According to the TB, 3.11.8 version Vajasravas performed a sacrifice in which everything the sacrificer possesses are to be gifted away. Young Naciketas thought that since he too was his father’s ’property’ he should also be given away as a gift. And he asked his father, ‘whom would you give me to?’ When he asked this question again and again the father was angry. He retorted, ‘I give you to Yama’. As Naciketas stood surprised, a divine voice spoke to him and told him to go to Yama when he is away, and stay there without eating for three days. When asked by Yama as to what did he eat during that time, he should tell him that by fasting there for three days he had eaten all his people, his animals and his sukrt. Naciketas did as he was told to when he reached Yama. Yama told him to select three boons.

In VarahaP (chapters 193-212) the story is similar in contents to the KathaUp, version.Naciketa goes to Yama’s place. Yama welcomes him and then sends him back to the earth. Uddalaka who was happy beyond words, collected all the hermits and Yama replied their questions regarding his visit to Yamaloka.

**Discussion**

The different versions are quite similar in their contents and this shows that the basic story is the same, i.e., Father performing the sacrifice — getting angry and cursing his son to go to ‘death'. But a comparative study of these versions reveals that this story is used, at different places to serve a different purpose. In the KathaUp Naciketas displays a tremendous maturity of thought. His concern for his father who was incurring sins by giving useless cows as gifts, prompts him to ask his father to give him to someone, since by doing so his father would earn some merit. The father could not understand what the son wanted him to realise (that these cows would not bring him any merit and that a giver of such cows would be sent to dark hells). Naciketas' firmness of resolve is revealed at the time of his encounter with Yama. At last Yama finds in him a true seeker of knowledge which is a cherished desire of every preacher. Yama could not help saying — “May we have more enquirers like you, Naciketas!"

In the Mbh and the Puranas, Naciketa is depicted as holding those higher qualities. He is nonetheless an obedient son who was cursed by his father to ‘see Yama’. The Mbh records the cause of the father’s anger that the boy could not find smidha, darbha, flowers, etc. at the river bank. VarahaP does not mention the cause of his anger.

It is significant to note that all these versions of the Naciketa-story are connected with the cows. It was at the sight of useless cows being given as gifts which make Naciketas ponder over the futility of such gifts. In the Mbh the greatness of godana (gift of cows) is related to Naciketa by Yama himself. He tells Naciketa that the pleasure of heaven, like river with flowing milk, ghi, etc. are enjoyed by those who have offered cows as gifts (Mbh, 13.71.29). The remaining chapter is about Yama telling Naciketa about the greatness of godana (gift of cows).

Thus, except in the AgniP version of the Naciketa story Yama is no more a preacher of metaphysical philosophy. In the Mbh he is only telling about the pleasures one would go for gifting away good cows at good time to a worthy receiver. In the VarahaP (193.212) also he tells Naciketa about different regions for the pious and the sinners.

This story of Naciketa which basically has a ritual purpose in the Brahmanas, evolved into a medium for instructing the knowledge of the Supreme Self and the individual Self (KathaUp). In the Mbh it stresses the greatness of godana. In the Puranas the main purpose of the story is to relate the fruit of various actions (karmaphala). No sign of Naciketa’s previous personality is seen either in the Mbh or the Varaha Purana.

**Yama: The Law-giver**

In the Upanisadic, Epic and Puranic literature Yama appears as a preacher of moral and metaphysical philosophy. In the Dharma-sastra literature also he is the author of Yama-smrtiYqjnavalkya-smrtt (1.4-5) mentions Yama as one of the twenty law-givers.

The Smrtis or the Dharma-sastras are the law-books of the ancient India. They deal with a variety of topics relating to the social order and code of conduct based on the varnasrama system, duties and rights of the ruler and the ruled. They throw light on the customs, religious and social rules and code of conduct. They also serve as guidelines for various ritual practises. Thus all the laws — civil and religious which govern and regulate the life and conduct are styled as Smrti. They are the “earliest Indian work on law, treating fully its religious but only partially and briefly its secular aspect”.

There are twenty main Smrtis and their authors are Manu. Atri, Visnu, Harita, Yajnavalkya, Usanas, Angiras, Yama, Apastamba, Samvarta, Katyayana, Brhaspati, Parasara, Vyasa, Sankha, Likhita, Daksa, Gautama, Satatapa and Vasistha.

**Yama-smrti**

In the Jlvananda collection (Part I, pp. 560-68) there is a Smrti of Yama in seventy-eight verses dealing with the topics of prayascitta and purification (suddhi). Here Yama is cited in third person (verse 65). Some of its verses are identical with those of another Dharmasastra-kara named Manu (e.g. verses 26, 28 are the same as the MS. 11.178; 3.19).

In the Anandasrama collection there is a Yama-smrti containing ninety-nine verses. They also deal with Prayascitta, sraddha and purification. Though the topics mentioned in this Smrti are same as those in the Calcutta text, but the verses are not identical. A few verses are found in both the collections, e.g., the verses about the seven lowest castes (antyaja).This Smrti contains the verse saying that after marriage a woman enters into the gotra of her husband (verse 78).

The Anandasrama collection also has a Smrti of Brhad-Yama (pp. 99-107). It contains 182 verses which are divided in five chapters.

It deals with the prayascitta for the various offences, the purification rites which are considered necessary in certain situations/events and the sraddha, etc. In this Smrti Yama is often quoted by his name and at several places it is identical with the Jlvananda text, e.g. the III. 1-3 of the Brhad-Yama are identical with the verses 15-17 of Yama-smrti in the Jivananda collection.

The numerous MSS of the Yama-smrti contain either one or the other of the above three texts or different texts bearing on the same topic.Over three hundred stanzas of Yama on all topics of Dharma-sastra are quoted by Visvarupa (a commentator of the YajS), Vijhanesvara (the author of the Mitaksara commentary on the YajS) and Apararka.Some other works also quote Yama, e.g., the Krtyakalpataru of Laksmidhara Bhatta, etc. This establishes that they had are extensive work of Yama before them. Apararka quotes five verses of Yama referring to the twenty-five tattvas, mentioned in the Samkhya philosophy, and regarding PurusotYama as the 26th tattva.

In the moksa-khanda of the Krtyakalpataru (pp. 101, 102) Laksmidhara quotes five verses of Yama.

As noted earlier the main subject of the Yama-smrti is expiation. It tells about the penance for drinking spirituous liquor, for one who returns from the order of hermits, penance, for taking the forbidden food, for a brahmin who in spite of being a mendicant wishes to become a householder again. Apart from these, it also tells about the right age for the marriage, a girl, conditions for performing the religious rites. It tells about the persons who are to be shunned at a sraddha. What gratifies the Fathers is also said here. According to the verse 37, one who purchases articles at fair price and sells them at a higher price is called Vardhusika. He is censured by the Brahmavadins (i.e., knowers of the Vedas).

Apararka (p. 596) quotes Yama’s verse which requires a king to look into the disputes very carefully and impartiallyYama lays down that a brahmin was never to be awarded corporeal punishment, but that a brahmin, if found guilty of a crime, should be imprisoned and made to work. Vasistha Dharma-Sutra, 18.13-15 and 19.48 cite the stanzas of Yama. Yama prescribes punishment for those who wrongfully take the water of a lake, or disturb the course of water (Apararka, p. 822). Yama observes that the Vedas or the Sastras do not permit the order of sanyasa for women, and that their real dharma is to bear children of someone of their own caste (Smrti-candrika, p. 254).

From this brief account of the contents of the Yama-smrti it may be said that Yama’s character as a moral and ethical preacher/philosopher is not obliterated entirely.

## 10. Epilogue

The character of Yama is unique in the whole Vedic mythology.In spite of his being a major god very few hymns are addressed to him and there is a conspicuous absence of legends connected with him which could throw light on the real character of this god.

On the basis of the number of hymns addressed to them and the occurrence of their names in the RV the Vedic gods are classified in five categories.In such statistical classification Yama cannot be regarded a major god in the RV. Only three hymns (RV, X.10,14,135) are addressed to him and his name occurs about fifty times in the RV, mostly in the Mandala X, a few times in the Mandala I and in a few scattered places in other Mandalas (e.g. 7.33.9; 7.76.4, 9.113.8). Yama is the ruler of the region of the dead (yamarajnah) (RV, 10.16.9). He is, therefore, also mentioned in the hymns connected with the dead and the funeral rites (RV, 10.15, 18, AV 18th Kanda). Yama is mentioned as a path-finder. He himself passed through death and discovered a path for all the dead to follow for reaching a special abode meant for them.

There is a considerable amount of inconsistency, discrepancy and vagueness in the picture of Yama as derived from the RV references. The most striking fact is that he is not explicitly called a god, but only a king (RV, 9.113.8. 10.14.1,4, 7. 11). Although he is not expressly called a god, there are a number of clear references which leave little room for any doubt regarding Yama’s divine character. He is the son of Vivasvat and is often referred to by the patronymic ‘Vaivasvata', i.e., ‘son of Vivasvat'. Apart from his divine birth, the divinity of Yama is also ascertained by his enumeration among other gods (RV. 1.164.46, 7.46.4, 10.135.1). He is also mentioned with individual gods like Varuna (RV. 10.14.7) and Brhaspati (RV, 10.13.4, 14.3). As a conductor of the dead Agni is also closely related with Yama. Agni is also called a dear friend of Yama (RV, 10.21.5).The RV. 10.51.1 and 3 tell that a god (who, it can be implied, is Yama) found out the hidden Agni. When Agni’s three elder brothers, were killed while performing their task as the oblation-bearers of the gods, Agni was terrified. He concealed himself in waters and eventually all oblations to the gods were stopped. It was Yama who spotted Agni in his hiding place. Agni was given assurances by the gods who requested him to resume the task of their oblation-bearer, Yama is also mentioned with other deities like Agni, Matarisvan as the different manifestation of the Supreme-Being (RV, 1.164.46). According to this rc the Supreme-Being (Brahman) is only one, but scholars describe ‘It’ differently like Agni, Yama, Matarisvan, etc. Here Yama is counted among the major Vedic gods. Glimpses of his sovereignty can also be seen in the Vedas. Yama’s relation with Varuna, the supreme sovereign in the Vedic pantheon, reveals the former’s equally high position. He too like Varuna punishes the evil-doers. Defaulters pray to be spared from the padbisa of Yama.

Though Yama is not called a god in the Vedas, it is equally important to note that he is not called a man either. Further more what contributes to the complexity of his character is that he is mentioned as ‘first to die’,and it is suggested that he himself passed through death and discovered a path to a settlement where all the dead had a right to live in.

Thus this is the unique case of a divine being who is not called a god in spite of his divine birth; an immoral being who had died, and in spite of his death he is never referred to as a ‘mortal’.

Though Yama is mentioned among gods he is constantly called a rajan which highlights his overlordship of the pitrloka. This has led to an assumption that he was not a god, but a mortal king who, due to his virtues, was exalted to the position of a god.But Yama cannot be regarded a human being since his personality and functions are far from human. There are clear references which suggest Yama's superhuman character. His act of self-immolation for the sake of creation is too cosmic or divine for a human being. These paradoxical features of Yama's personality, i.e., 'cosmic' or 'divine' on one hand and ‘human’ on the other are tactfully represented in the RV, 10.13.4.The RV, 10.13.4 states that Yama chose to immolate himself in the primeval sacrfice which was arranged for starting a (human) progeny.

From the study of the obscure, vague and broken account, provided by the Rgvedic references different features of Yama’s character can be put together as follows'.

Yama, the son of Vivasvat, was initially a god and so he too was immortal like other gods. For the cause of progeny he chose to surrender his immortality. He sacrificed his own body as the final offering in the primeval sacrifice, and hence he is remembered as the ‘first to die’. After his self-immolation Yama regained his divine status and became the lord of a heavenly region where all the dead get assembled and enjoy in the company of their king Yama.

**Father of Humanity**

The RV, 10.13.4 states that Yama chose to immolate himself in the primeval sacrifice which was arranged for the sake of progeny. It is not stated as to how Yama’s self-sacrifice helped in starting a progeny, however, the sacrifice of Yama in the primeval sacrifice appears as an earlier version of the sacrifice of Purusa mentioned in the Purusa-sukta (RV, 10.90) and in this light Yama is a prototype of Purusa. The creation of the universe out of a primeval sacrifice can be seen in the RV, 10.90. Here the cosmic-man Purusa created out of himself Viraj and from there the creation started. An earlier vague version of the primeval sacrifice mentioned in the RV, 10.13.4 finds a detailed expression in the RV, 10.90. This hymn (10.90) tells how the creation of the universe started out of the body of a cosmic-man. Thus Yama/Purusa is considered the creator of the universe as well as the parent of humanity. It may be noted that Yami does not play any role in the creation of mankind and the Yama-Yami dialogue (RV, 10.10) has a ritual purpose as shown earlier. As the ritual was lost and the dialogue remained, it has led to a conception that Yama and Yami are the progenitors of the human race.The ManuS also provides that the cosmic-man divided himself into two parts, one male and the other female, and the creation started with them.

**Yama and Godhood**

In the Vedas there is nothing to suggest that initially Yama was not a god and he gained godhood after defeating the gods. The TS (2.4.2) account of a struggle in which Yama defeated the gods only tells that Yama gained more power by defeating the other gods.

It is possible that Yama’s gaining the overlordship of the earth is suggested here. Thus it can be said that a god who was already the lord of the yonder world and the king of the Fathers defeated other gods and gained the overlordship of the earth too. The TS (2.1.4) records this strife in which Yama is stated to have achieved superiority over gods. Yama’s victory troubled the gods considerably and they reflected 'Yama has become here the we are’ and it is possible that Yama’s attaining the overlordship is suggested here. TS, 5.2.3and SB, 7.1.1.3 state that Yama is the lord of the whole earth and therefore his permission must be taken before preparing the fire-altar for a sacrifice.

It is important to note that as a king of the dead Yama comes to be indirectly connected with death. But in the RV he is not a feared god whose company people would like to avoid. On the contrary there are prayers where the worshipper expresses his earnest wish to live for ever in the kingdom of Yama (RV. 9.113.8). This suggests that region of Yama was believed to be a place for the happiest post-mortem existence. In this capacity, Yama's kingdom is similar to the Puranic concept of Vaikuntha, the abode of Visnu and a place of total emancipation.

**Identity of Yama**

Yama, as the god of the dead, himself had to pass through death. In other mythologies also the lord of the other world died and came to life again. Osiris died and rose up again as the king of the departed. Pluto was also swallowed and later disgorged by kronas. It seems that “Transmigration and metempsychosis demanded that the god who was the guardian of the dead should himself pass through death".As a son of Vivasvat Yama is a solar god. It is to be noted that the Sun is also called Yama.The Sun creates days and nights and the rotation of days and nights creates lime. With the passage of time man approaches death. In this way Yama is connected with 'time' through the Sun. In an account,connected with Adityas, i.e., sons of Aditi, it is said that Aditi gave birth to eight sons. She went up with seven and left the eighth (mrtanda) on earth.

This may suggest that the seven immortal Adityas ascended and Martanda was left behind. Martanda represents a chain of existence with birth -\> death -\> birth cycle. Since the stages of egg -\> broken-egg -\> bird also represent death and rebirth. This connects Martanda closely with Yama who after his self-immolation become immortal in the yonder wold.

Yama is connected with one or two other natural phenomena. Yaska and Sayana give a common explanation that Yama is lightning. According to Nir, 12.9 Yama is lightning and Yami the sound of thunder.

A number of scholars believe that Yama represented a phenomenon of nature. According to HillebrandtYama represents night (death) while Vivasvat represents day (life). Bergaigne thinks he is a form of Agni particularly the lightning.According to Max Mailer Yama is the setting sun and as such the leader of the Fathers.

Hillebrandt believes that the two characteristics of Yama's personality that he is Vivasvat's son, and that he died, can be explained only on an assumption that Yama was originally the moon-god. But the difficulty in accepting Hillebrandt's hypothesis is that though moon comes back after its each periodical death (dissappearance at no moon-day) Yama does not undergo repeated deaths and births. Thus Yama does not represent the moon.

Yama is also considered to be an ordinary human being because he died. He was an ordinary king who due to his powers and virtues was later exalted to the position of a god. But as R.N.Dandekar observes "The part played by Yama with reference to the creation of universe and to procreation of the human race and also his special connection with sacrifice is not adequately explained on the assumption that Yama was just a human being” and “that he as a matter of fact possesses in the Vedas a peculiar character of a god man”.The Vedic mythology has been reacting to various changes in the cultural history of the Vedic age. The personality of Yama too has undergone tremendous changes. The various changes contributing to the evolutionof Yama-mythology, as observed by R.N. Dandekar, are as follows.

The first stage of Yama's personality is that of a sacrificer who immolated himself in the sacrificial fire. The name Yama is very expressive and reveals the true character of the god. As observed earlier the word Yama means (1) A twin and (2) a hermophrodite. The X. 13.4 states that Yama gave up his own dear body. According to R.N.Dandekar this is the first stage of Yama-mythology where a bisexual being sacrificed himself for the cause of creation. Purusa of the Purusa-sukta is only a priestly modification of the original mythological conception of Yama. Mythologically as well as linguistically Yama of the Veda is similar to Ymir of Edda. a primeval hermophrodite who sacrificed himself for creation.

In the second stage the original function of Yama as the creator of universe is considerably restricted. Yama and his twin sister Yami procreate a human race.

In the third stage Yama became the ruler of the dead. The idea of the origin of the humanity from an incest was repulsive to the mind of the Vedic seers. In this stage Yami quietly disappears and both the previous stages were combined and modified in the Purusa-sukta where the creation of universe is said to have originated from the different organs and limbs of Purusa. In the fourth stage the Yama-myth interacts with the Manu-myth as they were coexisting for some time. The Vedic seers found in Manu a suitable alternative to avoid the offensive incest.

In the fifth stage Manu takes over the function of Yama. But Yama's self-immolation in the sacrifice is still remembered. It is this aspect of Yama’s personality which is continued and Yama was considered the first mortal (AV. 18.3.13) whose self-immolation is preserved in the RV, 10.13.4.

**Upholder of Morality**

In the Vedas Yama is depicted as punishing the offenders with his padbisa. In the capacity of a punisher he is even considered as a duplicate of Varuna.

Yama also displays a high degree of moral sense and self-control in the famous Yama-Yami hymn (RV. 10.10), where he does not succumb to Yami’s passionate and immoral request. The NP chapter 13 contains a version of the Rgvedic Yama-Yami hymn. Yama observes here, that the high-placed people of the society should be most careful about their moral conduct because common people consider them as their ideals and try to follow their foot steps. In the closing verses of the NP version Yama is louded for morality. It is also said here that by refusing to do an immoral act Yama gained godhood.

The RV, 10.10.2b is repeated in the RV, 10.12.6b and the RV, 10.12.6 can be rendered as follows:

By doing this condemned act (i.e., the incest) Yama would have earned a bad name (durmantu) because he would have treated his blood-relative (salaksma) as a non-relative (visurupa), i.e., his sister as his wife. But Yama saved himself from the ill-fame by not indulging in this forbidden act.

It is probable that this verse originally formed the last verse of the Yama-Yami hymn (and it was later misplaced). In that case it also provides the conclusion of Yama-Yami legend in the absence of which many scholars hold the view that this hymn was left incomplete because the suggested incest and the human progeny having originated out of it was highly objectionable to the morality of that age. But as the concluding lines of the hymn, this verse sums up the story by telling that Yama did not do the condemned act (i.e., incest).

In an article Shruti Pradhan gives a sociological interpretation of the RV, 10.10.She refutes the views of a number of the western scholars who think that the twin Yama and Yami represent the pair of day and night (F. Max Muller), or that they refer to the origin of the human race (Roth, Bloomfield, Geldner, Oldenberg). In her opinion all these views are out-dated. According to her it is questionable that behind this drama there is a ritual (Ulrisch Schneider).

She observes that the explanations given by Yama to his sister, stressing the unnaturalness of incestuous relations, are the poet’s rationalisations of a folk memory of the practice of incest.

However, attention may be drawn to an important fact here is that in the hymn special stress is laid on Yama-Yami being twins (RV, 10.10.4 and 5) and so it is not about incest between a brother and his sister but a twin-brother and his twin-sister. As the incest of a twin-brother and his twin sister cannot be a wide spread social practice (due to the rarity of such twins) which was required to be rationalised.

The birth of a twin, on the other hand is considered as inauspicious, and it can be assumed that the birth of a twin of both sexes is even more inauspicious, hence the necessity 25.4.35 tells about the purification rites to be performed in the event of birth of twins to one’s wife or to one’s cow (vide P.V. Kane. HDS, V.II. p. 139).

**Yama's Region**

Yama"s adobe is a place where all the dead find a resting place. They are described as rejoicing in the company of their king Yama and other gods. The worshipper wants to become immortal in the kingdom of Yama (RV.9.113.8).

In the post-Rgvedic literature a gradual change in the concept of Yama s abode can be noticed. There are some references where it is wished that one should not go to the realm of Yama.Thus departure to Yama’s region is not something to wish for.

This change in attitude can be attributed to the development of Yama’s personality incorporating sinister dimensions. Yama was previously a gatherer of the people, who reach his kingdom after death, because all the dead get collected in Yama’s region. It was the place he found for all the dead to live in. But gradually Yama, whose jurisdiction was beyond death, came to be identified with death itself. The element of fear cannot be missed in such lines, ‘May you not go to Yama’s place’.

In the Puranas, the kingdom of Yama is regarded as a place for punishment since hells are said to be situated in the kingdom of Yama and there he sits as the magnificent judge of man’s deeds.

Thus the region of Yama, which was the goal of all the dead and was conceived to be a place of enjoyment, where they lived under the loving care of Yamahas now become a most dreadful place with its gruesome methods of torturing the sinners. In the RV Yama’s region is the abode of the blessed souls and Yama is not connected with naraka, i.e.. hell. In the AV, 12.4.36 the Narakam lokam is mentioned as a region which is different from the blissful Yamaloka. where men get all their wishes fulfilled. It is no longer the abode of the blessed dead, but of the sinners who are tormented in the numerous hells which are mentioned as situated in the Yamaloka.

**Yama the Judge**

Supreme sovereign and upholder of the cosmic as well as the moral order. Varuna punishes those who break the law (ria).

It is this function of Varuna that Yama smoothly takes over and himself becomes, in the later literature, the judge of man's all good and evil deeds. Surya, the all-seeing eye of Varuna reports all the deeds of man. Like Varuna. Yama is also an all-knower. He even know the hidden sins of man.

Tracing the evolution of Yama-mythology from the RV down to the Epics and Puranic age it is noticed that Yama was not originally the god of death but it is in this capacity that his stature grows in the post-Vedic literature. At this stage the Rudra-mythology has significantly reacted on Yama-mythology, because Rudra is a particularly fierce god (RV, 2.33.9. 11; 10.126.5), who used death-bringing missiles (RV, 2.33.10), and also is called a cruel god(TS. 76.1.7.8 also cf. MS. 3.8.1).

In the Puranas Yama is the regent of the hells. Some aspects of his personality grew into independent gods such as Mrtyu, Kala and Antaka. His form is terrible to a sinner but for a pious soul who approaches him he take up the benign form of Visnu and welcomes him. This reminds one of Yama's benevolent form where he on seeing a dead soul reach his kingdom announces that “by reaching here this (the dead) has become mine, he would henceforth get a portion from the offerings made to me" (AV. 18.2.37).

**Yama the Punisher**

It is in the Epics and the Puranas that Yama’s status as a punisher grows. He controls the humanity by granting the appropriate results of their acts. The BhagP, 6.1.48 states that Lord Yama, through his inner vision, sees all the actions of people and decides the fruit to be awarded to them. The fear of Yama's punishment keeps people on the right tracts BrahmaP. 125.23).

Most of the Puranas give graphic pictures of the hells and the punishments meted out there. Thus Yama who was the lord of a heavenly region, lord of the whole expanse of the earth, upholder of morality who punished the defaulter with his padbisa has now become the lord of hells. His most august position as a judge, however, is not lost sight of. As a judge of man's good and bad deeds he exercises a control over the living beings also. He thus became a god who controls the dead as well as the living (through the fear of hells). The sovereign of the whole earth is now reduced to the position of one of the dikpalas and now he is the guardian of the south direction. The benevolent aspect of his character is not forgotten. To the pious he is extremely benign. He does not sent his fearsome dutas to fetch a pious soul. As it was revealed to Savitri by Yama, he himself goes to bring a pious soul. When the pious souls reach Yama’s region they find him in a very pleasant form, because for the pious Yama takes up the form of Visnu.

**Yama’s Counterparts in Other Mythologies**

There is remarkable similarity in the characters of Vedic Yama and his Iranian counterpart Yima. The Vedic Yama is the son of Vivasvat and he is mentioned as the first of the dead. He found out a new path and a settlement for the dead. He is the king of this region, he is called the gatherer of people (samgamanam jananam) since all the dead get collected in his kingdom. His region is a place of happiness and the dead live there blissfully.

Avestan god Yima is son of Vivanghat, who is the Iranian counterpart of Vivasvat. He is also a king and founder of a civilization, the best mortal gather around him in a marvellous palace in ‘Airyanam Vaego’, which is same as the Yamasadana, the abode of Yama.

According to a myth this world would end due to dreadful winter (Farg. XIX 28ff). This winter will be succeeded hy spring. All the blessed souls living in Yima’s region will come down and inhabitate the earth. Yima was told hy Ahura Mazda to become a preacher and bearer of his (Ahura Mazda's) laws.

It may be recalled that Yama figures prominently as preacher in the KathaUp, where he impart the secret knowledge to Naciketas. Yama is also one of the Dharmasastra-karas, i.e., the law-givers.

Yima declined to become a preacher since, he told, he was not taught to be a preacher. At this Ahura Mazda told him to look, to nourish, to rule and to watch over his world which Yima promises to do. Ahura Mazda also gave him a golden ring and a poniard. Darmesteter comments that these are symbols and instruments of sovereignty. Yima took good care of the men, Hock and herd, and their number grew. The earth became over-crowded. Ahura Mazda drew Yima’s attention to this and Yima stepped toward the south direction, pressed his golden ring on the earth and bored it with the poniard. He requested earth to stretch out so that there can be a place for everyone. Thrice Yima stretched out earth in his manner. (Vendidad, Farg. II.7).

Yima also saved his people (the dead) from fatal winters, Ahura Mazda foretold about the deadly winter and asked Yima to make a big vara (literally, an enclosure) and keep the best seeds of all the things, two of every kind.

Yima made a beautiful ‘Vara’. There Ahura made special arrangement for light and the ‘Vara’ remained brightened all the time. One year was equal to one day for the residents of the Vara’ (Vendidad, 2.40, Yasta, 19.28). Heat and cold could not torment the people in his ‘Vara’. According to the Iranian tradition the ‘Vara’ would open again after the universal destruction when people would open the Vara and a new creation would start.

In the RVno 'Vara' of Yama is mentioned. But Manu is said to be the progenitor of the mankind and therefore man is called manuj, manava, etc. According to a famous deluge story the water of the ocean covered the whole earth. At that moment a huge fish came and according to his instruction Manu with the seeds of all vegetation and the seven great sages (the saparsis) ascended the boat. The fish drew this boat to a high altitude and when the waters receded Manu started the creation again.

It is significant to note here that though linguistically Yama’s name is nearer to Yima but functionally Manu (Yama’s brother) and Yima are closer. Yama does not make any Vara’ he is surely mentioned as a lord of the region of the dead.

**Dogs**

Parallel figures of Yama’s two dogs can also be seen in the Avesta.Yama's two dogs are described as black and copper-coloured. They guard the path which leads to the region of Yama. These dogs are considered to be the two messengers of Yama. They move among people and spot a person whose life is over. Yama's dogs are described with many epithets. They are called caturaksau. i.e., four-eyed or having sharp eyesight, asutrpau (delighting in takings the asu (life) of the people). They are also called pathisadi. Thus they sit on the path leading to Yama's region and ensure that no unworthy person should enter the region of Yama. Yama’s dogs appear to be Indo-European and counterparts of these dogs are seen in the Avestan, the Greek as well as the Egyptian mythologies.

In the Avesta also two dogs are mentioned. They guard the Kinvad, bridge (the bridge of paradise). In the Vendidad. X.III.9 the killing of dogs is forbidden because if a dog is killed no soul will come to help the dead on his way to paradise nor will the dogs who guard the Kinvad bridge will come to help him despite his cries of terror and woe (Vendidad, XIII.9). In this way, it is believed that, the dogs should be kept satisfied so that they can come to help man in other world, where no one else can come to help them. The look of a dog is also believed to drive away the evil demons. Vendidad.III.14-22 state that the dogs come to help the soul when a man dies, as soon as the soul has parted from the body, the evil corpse demon (Druz-Nasu) falls upon the dead. The demon is expelled from the dead by the look of the dog a four-eyed dog. In practice, a dog with a spot over each eye is brought near the dead and is made to look at the dead, whereupon the demon leaves the dead and flees back to hell (Vendidad, 8-14-22) Vendidad, 8.16 mentions that the death-fiend is driven away by the look of a yellow four-eyed dog (i.e., a dog with two spot above the eyes) or white dog with yellow ears drives away the fiend to his region.

The identity of the four-eyed dog in the Iranian mythology has parallels in Kerberos of the Greek mythology and the two four-eyed dogs Syama and Sabala of the Vedic mythology.

In the Greek mythology the entrance of the realm of Hades, the lord of the lower world, is guarded by the dog of Hades. It is the dreaded Kerberos, a monster with three heads and a serpent’s tail. He fawns on those who entered, but shows his horrible teeth to those who tried to pass by.

**Hades or Pluto**

Hades, the brother of Zeus, is the god of the lower world, the region of the dead. He is the monarch of the dark realm inhabited by the dead. This region is considered to be under the earth's surface. It is believed that the vegetation grow steadily upwards due to some divine force below. In addition to that most precious metals are obtained by digging out the most dark region under the world. Therefore, in conceiving a god who should be supreme lord of this dark region, it was necessary to attribute a double character to him. First as the source of all treasures of the earth, and as the monarch of the dark realm of the dead. Whoever entered the realm of Hades was not permitted to return. The fierce dog Kerburos guards the gate of Hades’ realm. Hades is not only the ruler of the souls of the departed, but was also believed to exercise the function of the judge of mankind after death. In this capacity also he is Yama’s parallel.

In the Egyptian mythology Osiris is the lord of the other world. At the time of the judgement of the disembodied souls, it is believed that Anubis leads the dead man in the court of Osiris. There the heart of the dead is weighed against a feather. Forty-two officers attend the trial and ask question about man’s various deeds. These forty-two officers are called Assessors of the Dead, and they are similar to the bench of judges in a court.They are supposed to represent forty-two crimes from which a virtuous man was expected to be free. Each of them examined if the deceased was guilty of the peculiar evil which it was his province to avenge.

After weighing of the heart. Thoth delivers his judgement. In the course of enquiry the dead man speaks the following words — "I have begun no evil against any man, I have ill-treated no beast.... nor have I blasphemed against God".

A parallel to Indian Citragupta can be seen in Thoth. He keeps the record for the Great Judge Osiris, and reads it out to him at the time of the judgement. Thoth is also considered the original law-giver who sets forth laws which governed the movement of the heavenly bodies — he also became the god of wisdom and eventually the judge of the dead. He pronounces the verdict when the souls were weighed against the feather of the truth at the weighing of the heart before Osiris.

It is to be noted here that the functions of Yama as the lord of hells as well as the judge of the dead are represented by two different gods. Thus Osiris is the lord of the nether world and Thoth is the judge.

The jackal-faced Anubis, takes the soul to the seat of judgement in the Court of Osiris.  
In the Tibetan mythology Yama Gsin-rje (i.e., Yama) is the god of the dead and he also bears the title of the king of the law (in Tibetan chos-kyi-rgyal-po).

In the Japanese mythology Yama's parallel can be seen in Emma O (Yama raja). He writes the sins of men in a book. In his work Emma O is helped by a female head (miru-me) endowed with the capacity of seeing the most secret faults.There is also a male assistant who with the gift of smelling at the secret of all misdeeds. Emma pronounces his judgement and hands over the condemned to demons, who torment him by administering the penalties proportioned with the degree of the guilt.

**Yama-Naksatrani**

In the Indian astronomy, Yama is said to be connected with certain constellations. These constellation are called ‘Yama-naksatrani.The total number of constellations is given as twenty-seven and there names are as follows:

Krttika, Rohini, Mrgasirsa, Ardra, Punarvasu, Tisya, Asresa, Magha, Purva-Phalguni, Uttara-Phalgun). Hasta, Citra, Svati, Visakha, Anuradha, Jyestha, Mula, Purvasadha. Uttarasadha, Srona, Sravistha, Satabhisaj, Purva Prosthapada, Uttara Prosthapada, Revati, Asvayuj, Apabharani. Each of these naksatras has a presiding deity.Some divergence is seen regarding the name of naksatras and their presiding deities.

Out of the twenty-seven naksatras, the first fourteen, i.e., from Krttika up to and including Visakha are called deva- naksatras. Rest of them, i.e., from Anuradha to Apabharani are called Yama-naksatras.TBr, 1.5.2.8-9 give the etymology of the names of the constellations enumerated as Yama-naksatras.

Sayana comments that these are called Yama's constellation because they, like Yama, caused the death of the asuras.They are deemed as adhamanaksatras and no religious rites/ceremony should be performed under Yama-naksatras. All religious rites whether laukika, Vedic or kamya should be done only under deva-naksatras.

Apart from a whole set of constellation named as Yama-naksatras the last one, Apabharani, also known as Bharani is presided over by Yama. The gender of this naksatra is feminine and the number is plural. Its various names are:

Apabharani — TS, 4.4.10.1-3; TBr, 1.5  
Bharani — TB, III. 1.4.5  
Bharanyah—AV, 19.7-5  
Apabharanih — KathakaS, 39.13  
Bharanih— MS, II.13.20.

109\. The adjective pracetas means clever, wise, attentive which also qualifies a special aspect of practical knowledge. The quality was in all probability supposed to be proper for those great gods who excelled in intellectual abilities, cleverness, and resourcefulness in achieving extraordinary deeds. (Vide J. Gonda, Epithets in Rgveda, p. 89).

With his p \[a (noose) VaruGa holds the universe, for he is the binder god (\*Ueru - n - OS) and regulates its movements (ZV, 7.84.2; 10.149.1). Linguistically VaruGa is best derived from \*Uer = to bind. Vide R.N. Dandekar, op. cit... p. 58. Its other derivation, i.e., from v\[/ to cover (Nir, 10.4 VaruGo v\[Goti sata% also yacca v\[tv aticmha tad VaruGo abhavat), does not cover all the important traits of this god’s personality. Secondarily. In that case its form would be VaruGa.…. vide R.N. Dandekar. op. cit., p. 35.

Thelme (in ‘Mitra and Aryaman’) combines part of two distant theories viz. Lūuder's equation \[ta = truth and Mūller’s derivation of VaruGa from IE ver ‘to speak’ and proposes that VaruGa means true speech'.... vide RN. Dandekar, op. cit., p. 319.

Hillebrandt strongly asserts that Soma is essentially identical with moon (HVM, p. 202). He also takes Yama to be a moon-god (HVM, p. 227). Following this equation Yama comes to be identified with Soma.

Some scholars entertain a different view.

The concept of six worlds is clear in the Zgvedic tradition and S yaGa in his commentary on the ZV, 10.14.6 gives the names of the six worlds as dyaus, earth, waters, herbs, the sun and good speech.

On this use of the middle voice, T. Burrow comments that the middle is used when the subject is in some way or other specially implicated in the result of the action. (The Sanskrit Language, p. 293).

Mah+dhara, while commenting on the VS, 8.9 also deals with the word b\[haspati-sutasya and gives two Interpretations of the word B\[haspati — (1) squeezed by B\[haspati the yajam na who has performed great sacrificial deeds — b\[hato mahato yajṣakarmaGa% pati% b\[haspati% yajam na% (2) Impelled by B\[haspati the \[tvij br hmaGa yad va b\[haspalayo br hmaGa% \[tvija%.

In this connection Yami has no role to play since the progeny originated from a sacrifice. It must be noted that there is nothing in the text to support the Idea of the birth of a human progeny through the supposed Incest of Yama and Yam+.

Vide chapter 4 — 'Yama and Yam+.

Similar myths of the origin of the different aspects of creation out of the different parts of a divine character possessing a human form are also found in other primitive mythologies, e.g., the creation of heaven and earth from the two halves of the Goddess Tiamat. In Edda (11) in the ancient Germanic mythology of Tuisto and Ymir lived, there was neither land nor sea, neither earth nor heaven above. From Ymir's flesh the earth was created, out of his blood the oceans, mountains from his bones and trees from his hair, from this skull the heaven originated. Cf. ZV, 10.90.13.14.

Vide R.N. Dandekar, op. cit., p. 130.

According to S yaGa 's Interpretation the apsaras. wearing the garment of Yama, approached Vasicmha like a mother. But the next stanza states that Vasicmha has decided to be born. Hence he must have approached the apsaras, not the vice versa. Evidently the subject of surpassed should be Vasicmha and not the apsaras.

This was regarded as one of the three obligation to be fulfilled by men:  
The obligation to gods by performing sacrifices, that to the \[cis by celebacy and that to the Fathers by progeny.

This third obligation Is referred to in the ZV, 10.56.6 (t\[t+yena karmaG ). “It is a kind of cosmic-ethical obligation which developed in the course of time Into the concept of pit\[-\[Ga. — R.N. Dandekar, VMT, p. 336. H.D. Velankar, op. cit., p. 86.

R.D. Ranade refers to an Interesting suggestion made by R.G. Bhandarkar in BBRAS Journal. According to it the Sanskrit equivalent of the word asurya may here refer to the Assyrian country. Assyrian and Asuryan being philologically identical, the Y and U are interchangeable as in Greek, A Constructive Survey of Upanicadic Philosophy, p. 157.

According to the Brhaddevata Manu was a son of Vivasvat and Savarna (Savarna is named Chaya in the Puranas).  
With the view of Yama's function as a controller in the Puranas the name Yama can be derived from Vyam = to control.

34\. Yama raised his leg to strike Chaya in righteous indignation but controlled himself quickly and did not hit her. Thus he displayed immense control on himself and also a righteous vision. So his name can justifiably be derived from the root yam, to control.

35\. Gokarna is a place sacred to Siva on the western coast about 30 miles south of Goa in the Kumta Taluka of North District — Vide P. V. Kane, HDS, p. 753. The LP speaks of two Gokarnas (1.92.134; 135); KurmaP, II.35.31 mentions Uttara Gokarna and the VarahaP, 213.7 mentions a southern and a northern Gokarna — Vide P.V. Kane, op.cit., p. 754.

40\. Yama also is Surya, the regulating principle, and Yami his sister, is the female power that is in the Sun. The two Asvins also represent the principles of prana and apana or twofold energy as it is becoming manifest on the plane of Matter in the conscious world — Vide V.S. Agrawala, op. cit., p. 89.

The GP, 11.7.11.14 provides a story related to the birth of Sravanas. According to it Rudra, Visnu and Dharma ruled the earth. They wanted to know the good and evil deeds of men. They consulted the other gods and approached Brahma. Brahma created twelve sons out of the kusa grass in his hand. These Sravanas inform all the happening in the world to Brahma. They also knowall the endeavor of all living beings which they report to Dharma Yama, at the time of death (GP, 11.7.16).

The term smrti initially denoted the vast Vedic literature which included the six Vedangas, Dharma-Sutras. Epics, Puranas, etc., but gradually came to be used in a narrower sense, i.e., the Dharmansastra. (srutis tu vedo vyneyo. dharma-sastram tu vat smrtih/ — MS. 2.10). In the RV the word dharma is used as an adjective or noun and it means upholder or supporter. In most of the Rgvedlc passages it means religious ordinances or rites and in rare cases ‘fixed principle and rules of conduct' (vide P.V. Kane, HDS, Vol. 1, pt. 1. pp. 1-4).

Cf Sruti smrtih sadacarah  
svasya ca priyam atmanah.  
samyak samkalpajah kamo  
dharma mulam idam smrtam—YajS, 1.7
